On the other hand a 3 day power outage in the winter (not normal, but not unusual) is no joke. It is enough of a “no joke” the most houses around here have an external input for a generator or other 240V power source. Mine in particular will power the house’s heating (well the water circulation

Blackout protection is not the main reason to do this. The main reason to do this is arbitrage. And it would take you only seconds to do the maths and realise that arbitrage requires bugger-all extra strain on the battery, a few cycles a year at most. 70 or 80kWh battery vs maybe 4 or 5kWh of draw per day in the summer and as high at 8 or 9 in the winter.

Tesla experimented with this and discovered many people would charge up at work, then use the power at home in the evening leaving just enough charge to get back to work in the morning to recharge. A great life-hack to cut your utility bill, but the downside was the battery cycling dramatically reduced the life of the cells. If used properly, such as only during power outages or emergencies it is a very handy solution. When used daily, it will cost more for early replacement of the battery than the owner will save in utility bills.

Trading the future for the present. Can't say I really blame them, and plus EV batteries just may get cheaper in the future, so it's a bet too.

#4157. What Happens When We Stop Trying to Win a Difficult Conversation?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It’s become a common story: Divided by politics, my father and I grew more and more distant, and after a while we could not talk about much without tension rising between us. He passed before that distance was ever repaired. I did not have the tools in time. I kept wondering why this happened so easily, to families and friendships and people who had once known how to be in the same room together. Then I went to graduate school at Colorado State University. I became a graduate teaching assistant and facilitator for the Center for Public Deliberation (CPD), which became the most fulfilling part of my time in graduate school. Part of what drew me to this work was my experience with my father, though our story is one many people will recognize. In recent years, political division has worked its way into kitchens and living rooms across the country, coming between people and making once-ordinary conversations hard to have. You may have felt it at your own table. When I found the CPD, I found the tools I had needed so desperately and had not known existed. These were not techniques for winning an argument or even holding your ground. They were ways of reaching the “why” underneath another person’s perspective, the experiences and stories and information that had built it. The goal, I discovered, should never have been agreement. It was understanding, the point where someone’s view makes sense to you as something a person could reasonably arrive at, even when reaching that point is hard. That was exactly what my father and I had never had a way to give each other. It showed me the path I wanted to take in life. Even beliefs that look irrational on the surface usually trace back to a real concern or value underneath. Understanding that root does not mean every belief holds up equally against facts. Some do not. What it means is that curiosity about the person’s underlying concern is what makes deep value-based conversation possible. You can respect where someone is coming from and still hold the line on what is true. The CPD is hired by cities and local organizations across Northern Colorado to bring community members together around shared challenges. Rather than holding traditional town halls, where people often talk past one another, the CPD designs events that foster collective wisdom: the recognition that the knowledge of a group is far more powerful than any single person’s alone. What I loved most was who filled the room. A retiree and a college student, a small business owner and a recent arrival, people from across the political spectrum and from neighborhoods that may rarely interact. The issues were as varied as the people: housing, how communities care for their aging members, student mental health, and, with the local newspaper, misinformation and political polarization. Whatever the topic, the aim was the same: to give a group what it needs to work through a shared problem, together. As a facilitator, my role was to help people, using techniques like uncovering the value tensions underneath their disagreements. This is more delicate than it sounds. When people argue, they tend to argue about conclusions, but conclusions are not always where the real disagreement lives. More often we hold many of the same values and simply weigh, rank, and apply them differently, shaped by what our own lives have taught us. Take accessory dwelling units (ADUs), small additional homes built on existing properties. One neighbor might prioritize privacy, worried that an ADU next door changes the quiet street or brings people they don’t know closer to their daily life. Another might prioritize freedom, believing homeowners should have the right to build on their own land as they see fit. Both values can be held by reasonable people. Neither is wrong. The disagreement isn’t really about ADUs. Instead, disagreement lives in which value, privacy or freedom, someone weighs more heavily when the two come into tension. Naming the value underneath a position does two things at once: It shows the group where the actual tension sits, and it makes the other person’s view legible as something a reasonable person could hold. Once you can see that, movement becomes possible. We were not necessarily searching for common ground that already existed. But, rather, we were building it together. At some point, tools stopped being something I reached for and became something I embodied in everyday life. I noticed it less as a single moment and more as a pattern showing up everywhere. I found myself curious in interactions that used to pass by unnoticed, wanting to know more about people for no reason other than wanting to know them. When someone said something I disagreed with, my first instinct stopped being to argue. It became asking what shaped that belief, what experience or value sits underneath it, digging past the kind of surface-level exchange most conversations settle for. Curiosity tends to be contagious. When I show someone true interest, they often show it back. What started as one person asking questions turns into two people learning from one another. That only works, though, if it stays voluntary. Some people are private, and that is their right. I am not asking anyone to disclose more than they want to. If someone wants to share, I listen. I ask. I learn. It stopped being a skill I used and became a way I move through the world. Long after the events ended, I would catch myself in a heated debate at a dinner or with friends, reaching for the why underneath what someone had said instead of what I deemed to be disagreeable or flawed. I stopped needing to win. I started assuming the person across from me knew something I did not, and that searching to discover their reasoning was the most respectful thing to do. The harder lesson was patience, and most of it was with myself. Some conversations made me frustrated. Some I simply got wrong. The discipline was never to pretend otherwise but to notice the mistake and repair it, and then to extend that same grace outward. Other people arrive carrying their own frustration and stumble in their own ways, and they need the room to learn that I needed, too. Sometimes the best thing I had to offer was curiosity. It was a single conversation across difference that left someone feeling heard, which can feel rarer than it should. That curiosity follows me well beyond any event, into encounters that have nothing to do with policy. In an old mining community in Wyoming that had largely emptied out, I stopped to talk with a man who made pottery. The town was the kind of place where the buildings tell the story before any person has to, where you can read the initial boom and the migration in the empty storefronts and homes. We sat together while he told me what it had been like to watch his town change, the prosperity that came and went, the quiet the exodus left behind. He spoke about staying when others left, about his craft, about what it means to keep making something with your hands in a place that feels as though some had forgotten. I was not there as a researcher and I had no agenda. I simply wanted to know, and so I asked, and he told me. I think about that conversation more than he would ever guess, and it gave me something no book could impart. This is what curiosity does. It lets you hear about other people’s lifetimes rather than just their opinions, and it asks something of you in return: the willingness to admit that your own understanding is incomplete, and that other people’s stories and experiences are part of what fills in the gaps. This requires us to choose curiosity over certainty, again and again, even when certainty would be easier. I do similar work now at Unify America, helping design and support civic assemblies that bring everyday people together to address shared problems. The timeline is slightly longer than those evening events in graduate school, but the ethic is the same. What moves me most still happens in the small moments of connection: people who would never have crossed paths in ordinary life, of different ages and neighborhoods and political realities, sitting down together over weeks and slowly getting to know one another. They share meals. They learn the names of each other’s kids. They discover, often to their own surprise, that a person they assumed was their opposite carries many of the same worries and hopes they do. I have been in the room while it happens, and you can feel the shift when it comes. The distance that seemed permanent reveals itself as circumstantial. I don’t say this to suggest that dinner-table tension is the whole story. Many people are living through a period of real precarity right now, for reasons that differ sharply depending on who you ask and what news you consume. Some people are afraid for their safety. Some are afraid for their livelihoods. Some are afraid of being unheard or unseen in a country that no longer feels familiar to them. Whatever the reason, whether it belongs to you, your neighbor, or a stranger you pass on the street, it comes from the same place. We are all moved by our hopes, our dreams, our values, our experiences, our fears. That’s part of what makes this work harder than it looks from the outside. You cannot ask someone to be curious about a neighbor’s perspective while pretending the stakes are small. The fear is real, even when two people in the same room are concerned about two completely different things. Deliberation does not erase that concern or resolve it. But it does something else that matters as much. It reminds people that they are not just living alongside one another, they are co-creating the communities they share. That means everyone has a seat at the table, not to win an argument, but to build something together that helps all of us navigate life the best way we know how and become a little more of the people we hope to become. Belonging grows out of that kind of collaboration. It is hard to build when we default to blaming our neighbors for our differences, and it gets easier when we choose curiosity instead, again and again, until it becomes a way of being with each other rather than a technique we reach for. The same attitudes carry weight beyond any single conversation. When people practice curiosity and patience with each other, they build the capacity to do something bigger together: to take part in shaping the decisions that govern their shared life. Civic processes work best when the people inside them can actually hear one another, weigh trade-offs, and hold a disagreement without needing to end it in conquest. That capacity does not appear on its own. It is built the same way any skill is built, through patience, in rooms like the ones I have described, and in the ordinary conversations that happen everywhere else. The more of us who carry these attitudes in public life, the more capable we become, together, of building a collective vision of what it means to participate in a shared civic life. This is why I do this work. Not because I am trying to solve polarization as an abstract problem, but because I know what it feels like when understanding breaks down between people you care about, and what it feels like when it can be repaired. Every assembly, every conversation, every moment of curiosity is a chance to help others experience what I am still learning myself: that we do not have to choose between our perspectives and our relationships. We can seek to understand one another not out of self-interest, but because human experience is rich beyond measure, and we are all a little incomplete without each other’s stories.

#4138. Three Qualities That Can Make You an Inspiring Leader

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Imagine you’re on a plane and suddenly hear an explosion. The whole plane rattles, and you see the left engine is on fire. After another loud noise, you realize there’s a hole in the side of the aircraft. As air rushes out, the plane is rapidly descending—over 20,000 feet in less than five minutes. If you faced what passengers of Southwest Airlines 1380 experienced, you would probably do what they did: frantically write goodbye messages to your family and loved ones, thinking that these are your last moments on Earth. But on that flight, the pilot, Captain Tammie Jo Shults, came on the intercom and said 10 simple words: “We’re not going down; we’re going to Philly.” The passengers would later talk about how those 10 words were transformative, turning their panic into possibility. By sharing those 10 words, Captain Shults was helping her passengers make sense of their experience and reframe the dramatic descent, from “plummeting to Earth” to a planned and orchestrated path to safety. As Captain Shults expertly landed the plane, she calmly and peacefully talked to air traffic control. After landing, she got up and went row by row, looking every passenger in the eye to make sure they were OK. People talked afterward about her words, her stature, and her empathy as she walked down that aisle. Contrast Captain Shults’s experience with another captain who also dealt with a massive hole in his vessel, Francesco Schettino, captain of the Costa Concordia. Headed out on a seven-day cruise in the Mediterranean, the ship got too close to shore. It’s debatable why that happened: Captain Schettino claims he was saluting mariners on the shore; prosecutors claim he was trying to impress his mistress who was on board. But what isn’t in dispute is what happened next: The rocks tore a 115-foot hole in the side of the ship, which caused it to eventually capsize. Before calling the Coast Guard, Captain Schettino spent time getting his story straight. The crash had caused an electrical blackout, but he was going to try to say that the blackout caused the crash. He tried to pin the responsibility on the helmsman. It took him almost an hour after the crash to sound the evacuation siren. While people were frantically trying to find safety, Captain Schettino was nowhere to be found. Finally, the Coast Guard discovered him in a lifeboat—though Captain Schettino claimed that he “accidentally” fell into the lifeboat. But he also somehow fell out of his captain’s uniform. He was sitting in a lifeboat watching his boat sinking, hiding in civilian clothing. You can hear the outrage in the Coast Guard’s voice when he says in an audio recording, “I’m gonna make you pay for this.” And Captain Schettino did pay. He was sentenced to 12 years in prison for manslaughter and abandoning ship. These two people faced very similar situations. They were both captains, dealing with holes in the side of their vessels, but how they responded to their crises was completely different. Captain Shults helped people make sense of their experience. She was courageous, competent, and calm in landing the plane, and she compassionately connected to each of her passengers. In contrast, Captain Schettino caused the accident, refused to take any responsibility, and did not take any action to help his passengers save their lives. As a result of his selfish negligence, 32 people died, and numerous people were injured. Why do some leaders create feelings of inspiration that percolate a wellspring of hope and possibility in others, while other leaders create seething cauldrons of infuriation and resentment? I’ve asked this question to tens of thousands of people across the globe, and here is what I learned about what makes a leader inspiring. Three aspects of good leadership My first big idea is that “inspiring” and “infuriating” leaders exist on an enduring continuum that is made up of three universal factors:Being a visionary, how we see the world Being an exemplar, how we are in the world Being a mentor, how we interact in the world We can inspire people in how we communicate, how we present ourselves, and how we interact with others—through our words, our actions, and our interactions. My second big idea is that these are the three factors of inspiring leadership because each one fulfills a fundamental human need. Being visionary fulfills the fundamental need for meaning and understanding. That’s what Captain Shults provided when she said, “We’re not going down; we’re going to Philly.” She helped people make sense of their experience. Being an exemplar fulfills the fundamental need for protection and passion. Captain Shults courageously protected her passengers with her calm and competence. And being a mentor fulfills the fundamental human need for belonging and status. That’s what Captain Shults offered as she walked down the aisle: She made her passengers feel recognized and seen. By surveying tens of thousands of people across the globe, I have found these three dimensions to be universal. There is not a single inspiring characteristic that is mentioned in one country that’s not mentioned in the dozens of countries in the world where I have collected data. Visionaries Effective visionaries not only offer a clear perspective on the world, but they also make their vision easy to follow and understand, and so engaging that it captures our attention. The easiest way to make something easy is to make it simple. I call this the Inception Rule, after the movie Inception, where Leonardo DiCaprio tries to plant an idea in someone’s head during a dream. He discovers it’s not about how deep you go; it’s about how simple the idea is. As a leader, you want to constantly be thinking how to reduce your ideas to their simple core. Another way that we can make things easy to understand is to repeat them, the way a chorus repeats in a song. The more people hear it, the more they will remember it. Let’s consider Zoom’s vision: Delivering Happiness. It’s simple, and bubbly. But that vision is also deeply practical—it helped Zoom’s employees efficiently make high-stakes decisions during the chaos of the COVID-19 pandemic. As millions of people began using the product for the first time, Zoom needed to decide on its product’s baseline level of functionality. Too many features would overwhelm people, but too few features would stymie people. They needed the Goldilocks baseline of features to deliver happiness. Similarly, delivering happiness led Zoom to invest heavily in customer service. Most of us know how much an hour-long wait for customer service can destroy happiness. So, Zoom hired many agents so wait times would be as short as possible. To make our visions more engaging, we can use visual language. Andrew Carton, an associate professor of management at Wharton, did a study where people were told that a company’s mission was to either “make our customers satisfied” or “make our customers smile.” Then, he had people create a product. When they were working under the more visual vision (“smile”), they created more engaging products. How to become more visionary: We can inspire people to be more visionary by having them reflect on their values. In one of my studies, we worked with a Swiss employment agency. When people came in to register for benefits, half of them were asked to reflect on their values, why those values mattered to them, and how they demonstrated them. For example, my own values include generosity, courage, and kaizen (a Japanese word for continued improvement). Two months later, the people who had reflected on their values were twice as likely to have a job. The effects were so profound that the employment agency asked us to end our study and give everyone the reflection activity. Exemplars To be a great exemplar, the first thing you want to do is to consistently embody your vision; don’t be a hypocrite. As former CEO of PepsiCo Indra Nooyi once noted, “Don’t ask someone to do something that you wouldn’t do [yourself].” Inspiring exemplars are calm and courageous protectors, like Captain Shults. And they show passion. When you ask someone to talk about a passion of theirs, their eyes light up, they smile a big beaming smile, they speak quickly and dramatically use their hands, and they lean in as if telling you a secret. When we are passionate about something, it’s revealed in our eyes, our mouth, our voice, and our bodies. And when you listen to someone’s passion, you smile, your eyes light up, and you feel the energy inside of you. In my research on Dragon’s Den, which is a variant of Shark Tank, when entrepreneurs expressed more passion—in their eyes, their voice, their hands—they were dramatically more likely to receive funding. You can’t inspire others if you aren’t inspired. How to become a better exemplar: Think about the moments in your life when you felt the most powerful and in control, your best self. Hundreds of studies around the world have used this technique that my colleagues Deb Gruenfeld, Joe Magee, and I created. We’ve even found that people are more likely to be selected for jobs if they’ve engaged in this reflection. Mentors Consider this proverb by Confucius: “Tell me and I will forget, show me and I may remember, involve me and I will understand.” When you involve people, they feel seen and heard and they feel like people, not objects. Inspiring leaders empower others everywhere across the globe. Even in cultures that are less individualistic than the United States—like Thailand or Saudi Arabia—there’s nothing more infuriating than when a boss micromanages you, rather than involves you or trusts you. Inspiring leaders also elevate others. They share credit rather than steal credit, which is one of the most infuriating things that can happen at work. Finally, inspiring leaders empathize with others. They do what Captain Shults did, in making sure each of her passengers were OK, versus only thinking about themselves, like Captain Schettino. How to become a better mentor: Reflect on the perspectives of others. My colleagues and I did a study with medical students who were tasked with diagnosing what was wrong with patients. Before meeting their patients, half the students were told to take the perspectives of their patients and to think about a recent time when they had engaged in perspective taking. Ultimately, the patients were more satisfied, trusting, and likely to follow the advice of the doctor who had been reminded to engage in perspective taking. How to become an inspiring leader My third big idea is that inspiring leaders aren’t just born; they’re also made. Because there is a universal set of inspiring attributes and behaviors, they can be practiced, nurtured, and developed. My fourth big idea is that when you’re a leader, you don’t have the option of not having an impact. Your behavior is going to inspire or infuriate. I call this the “leader amplification effect.” Everything you say and do as a leader gets amplified. Your criticisms become humiliating criticisms, but your compliments become glorious praise. My fifth big idea is that you can REIP what you sow. This phrase, drawn from the Bible, suggests that if we put inspiring energy out in the world, we receive inspiring energy in return. But my REIP is a homonym of reap, and it stands for reflect, emulate, intend, and practice:Reflect on your own inspiring and infuriating experiences as a leader once a month. Emulate: Identify the inspiring people in your life and think: How could I emulate them? Intend: Turn these reflections into an explicit intention. What specific behavior can you commit to enacting or implementing? Practice: Turn your intention into a daily practice. My final big idea is that inspiring leaders think like architects. Like an architect who designs buildings to produce particular reactions and interactions, leaders design policies and practices that lead people down the inspiring path, even when they’re not present. So instead of just changing individual behavior, you can think about: How can you change a broader dynamic? Thinking like an architect moves you from viewing a situation as a person problem—What’s wrong with this employee?—to start to think about what’s wrong in their environment, and how you can redesign that environment to help the employee be the best version of themselves. Here’s one practice that Joseph Stagliano, president of NBT Bank, designed to inspire those around him. Although he has over 1,200 employees, he sends a birthday greeting to every single person. That sounds impossible, but he does it in 10 to 15 minutes over coffee every day. The message is simple yet personalized. He showed me an example that said, “Hey Monica [pseudonym], Happy birthday! I hope you had a great weekend? How did track and bowling go?” Although his message was short, Monica’s response was extensive, overflowing with information and enthusiasm. I shared that idea with the CEO of another company, and then a month later, he told me he started doing something similar. Every morning over coffee, he reached out to one person in his organization, and he expressed either gratitude or praise. He said he did this to put a skip in their step, but they ended up sending him such glowing replies that it put a skip in his step. The CEO reaped the inspiring energy he sowed. You can, too.       This essay is based on a talk that is part of the Positive Links Speaker Series by the University of Michigan’s Center for Positive Organizations. The Center is dedicated to building a better world by pioneering the science of thriving organizations.

#4122. Five Things Students Taught Us About Connecting Across Differences

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When I’m producing a documentary, one of my favorite parts of the process is the moment when a bit of our footage sticks to me. Permanently. I never know when it will happen, but it always does. The sticky bit could be a snippet of dialogue or a quote from an interview. After watching the footage over and over again, the bit becomes part of me. Often it’s a funny line—a perfect, punchy distillation of the truth that gets adopted as an inside joke among the team of producers and editors. We repeat it like a collective spell that buoys us when we’re sinking under the weight of the project’s heavy subject matter. Sometimes the thing that sticks is not funny at all. Sometimes it’s a splinter, a cutting insight that I would have rather avoided. Or it’s a solidly true, heartfelt moment that sits like a smooth stone in my pocket. The touchstone grounds and guides me on the long journey of making a film. For the better part of the past year, I’ve been working as an executive producer on a series of short documentaries for the Greater Good Science Center called Connecting on Campus: Stories of Courage, Curiosity, and Empathy. The series features students at four universities across the country who are attempting to connect in some meaningful way with people who have different life experiences, political beliefs, or visions for the future. At Iliff School of Theology, master’s and Ph.D. students imagine a version of the United States that embodies their values, and then they share those visions with one another. As they listen to one another’s values, they discover where they align, where they diverge, and where empathy can keep them in relationship with each other even when they disagree. In a two-part story set at Bowie State University and the University of Maryland, students from both campuses gather at an event hosted by the schools’ Social Justice Alliance, a first-of-its-kind collaboration between an HBCU (historically Black college or university) and a PWI (predominantly white institution). At Dickinson College, a deep friendship between the presidents of the Dickinson College Republicans and the Dickinson College Democrats creates the conditions for an unexpectedly collaborative relationship between the two clubs. Members of both clubs gather regularly and listen to each other’s thoughts about complex and often contentious political issues. Back when we were in the middle of editing these episodes with our collaborators at Anaconda Street Productions, I spent an afternoon reviewing footage from one of the shoots, and then I took my dog for a walk. On the walk, I found myself repeating a line from an interview. It stuck! It’s been one of those smooth stones in my pocket ever since. Of course, I’m not the only one who was changed by the experience of working on this series. To celebrate the release of Connecting on Campus, I invite you behind the scenes to meet my collaborators as we share some of the many things we learned as we helped tell these stories. 1. Your voice matters and everyone has a part to play It’s a pretty straightforward message, yet I hear it over and over in my head in the voice of Mrs. Dawn Collins. Your voice matters and everyone has a part to play. Mrs. Collins says these words during an interview in the first of our two episodes about Bowie State University (BSU) and the University of Maryland (UMD). Mrs. Collins and her husband, Mr. Richard Collins Jr., founded the BSU–UMD Social Justice Alliance after their son, Lt. Richard Collins III, a BSU student, was murdered on the UMD campus by a student in 2017. The Alliance brings together faculty, staff, and students to join the Collins’s commitment to “eradicating the ideology of intolerance, while confronting individuals and combatting systems that normalize indifference, justify injustice, perpetuate hate, and inflict violence.” Mrs. Collins’s words have stuck with me, in part, because they insist on every person’s dignity, power, and responsibility. Our voices matter, and we all have a role to play in eradicating the ideology of intolerance and in combatting systems that perpetuate hate and inflict violence. That’s a strong call to action. Mrs. Collins’s words have also stuck with me because they clearly articulate the heart of this documentary series. The students featured in this series offer us examples of what happens when people step into their power and accept the responsibility to understand themselves, understand each other (differences and all), and work together to build something new. That new thing could be a collaboration between clubs on campus or a vision for our future as a nation. Either way, these students know their voices matter and they have what it takes to practice doing this work together. It was so helpful to have Mrs. Collins’s voice in my head, keeping me anchored in this truth as I worked. — Kelly Rafferty 2. Small moments can lead to meaningful choices When we first imagined Connecting on Campus, we wanted to find places where students were actually practicing the skills and qualities that make connection across differences possible, and to make those moments visible.  As I spent time with these students and their stories, and as we shaped the episodes in the edit, I found myself thinking more and more about choice. Across the series, you see students staying curious when they could shut down, listening to understand someone else’s experience, sharing their own perspective while making space for someone else’s, or finding the courage to remain in a difficult conversation.   In our Dickinson College episode, that choice shows up in a moment that could have easily passed. Ryan Thies recounts meeting Matthew Craig at a Halloween party during his freshman year. Matthew was dressed as Donald Trump. Ryan could have seen that costume, made an assumption about who Matthew was, and walked away. Instead, he leaned in and got to know him. An unlikely connection became a friendship, and eventually the two chose to become roommates.    Ryan’s choice changed what became possible between them. That choice reflects the kind of empathy we talk about in our Bridging Differences work: the deliberate, and often difficult, choice to engage with someone else’s experience even when it feels unfamiliar or challenges our own worldview.   The choices look different in our Bowie State University and University of Maryland episodes—and the stakes are obviously very different, too. Here, the students and the Collins family are stepping into a history that is devastating: the murder of Lt. Richard Collins III on the University of Maryland campus and the work that emerged from it to confront hatred and bring students from the two campuses together.   Here, courage looks like choosing to show up and engage when discomfort, conflict, and fear might make it easier—and completely understandable—to withdraw. It’s the decision to say: This matters. I’ll attend. I’ll represent. I’ll listen. I’ll do my part.  Practices like “Listen with Empathy,” “Understand Their Values,” “Expand Your Views and Circles,” or “Perspective Giving and Getting” from our Bridging Differences in Higher Education Playbook can be learned.   But the choice to engage is one each of us has to make. My hope is that people watch these stories asking themselves, “What did that person do that made greater understanding possible, and where might I try that myself?”  — Juliana Tafur 3. Connecting with someone across a difference isn’t about erasing what separates us I came away thinking that “bridging” is sometimes misunderstood as compromise. Across all four videos, something more interesting emerges: You can build a relationship across a difference without necessarily resolving the difference itself. At Iliff School of Theology, students imagine very different versions of America without being asked to arrive at one shared vision. At Dickinson, Republicans and Democrats engage seriously with each other without abandoning their political beliefs. And at Bowie State University and the University of Maryland, students move beyond assumptions, allow themselves to be vulnerable, ask difficult questions, and really listen to people whose backgrounds and experiences differ from their own. The circumstances are very different, but the lesson is consistent: Bridging isn’t about erasing what separates us. It’s about developing the curiosity, trust, and courage to stay connected across those differences.  The series asks a harder and more useful question than “How do we get everyone to agree?” It asks how we live, learn, and build community with people who may never see the world exactly as we do. The answers aren’t presented as a formula. You get to watch people experiment with listening, vulnerability, curiosity, disagreement, and repair, then decide for yourself what actually works. — Omar Lugones 4. Disagreement can actually bring us closer together and create new opportunities In my own experiences in smaller, more intimate classroom settings, I often observe an echo-chamber effect when opinions are shared, especially when the class is discussing a controversial topic. I have noticed a general apprehension about “going first” or initiating the conversation. Speaking for myself, I am often afraid of offering the “wrong” opinion, meaning a perspective that could be interpreted as supporting a politically or socially unacceptable position and cause my peers to view me as an outsider. I do not necessarily think my peers are experiencing the exact same thought process, but I sense that they are also self-conscious about expressing a conflicting opinion. As a result, people may default to agreeing with whoever offers an opinion first. I thought it was remarkable that at Iliff School of Theology, Lindsay Ambler and Stephanie Nelson initially disagree with one another, and that moment opens space for others to enter the conversation and offer their own perspectives. By disagreeing respectfully, they demonstrate how disagreement can produce a deeper understanding of an issue than a conversation built only around agreement. Lindsay takes a strong position on the effects of religion based on her past experiences with Christian institutions. By sharing an extremely vulnerable personal story, she creates the conditions for others to share their experiences and, in turn, promotes a greater sense of empathy within the conversation. When Stephanie offers her own perspective on whether society can reach a state of perfection and equality, her response conflicts with Lindsay’s position. This point of contention could seemingly disrupt the vulnerable environment Lindsay has created, yet it does not. Instead, Lindsay responds to the challenge by deepening her explanation. In doing so, she creates new points of entry for others to join the conversation and contribute their own experiences. At the end of the discussion, Stephanie expresses gratitude to those who shared their perspectives and revealed gaps in her own thinking. The conversation demonstrates how respectful disagreement can foster empathy, growth, and greater participation from every member of a group. — Tyler C. Tang 5. Character development is often uncomfortable, but the payoff is worth it Working on this series, I noticed the relationship between character and discomfort. Courage, empathy, humility, and curiosity all sound admirable in the abstract, but you really discover whether you possess them when another person challenges you. Character in these films often reveals itself in what someone does immediately after hearing something they don’t agree with. For example, I identified with Josh Perez, a Ph.D. student and teaching assistant at Iliff School of Theology, in particular. I appreciate the way he draws on his own experiences as a second-generation Mexican American while recognizing that other people carry struggles he may never experience himself. His vision isn’t simply about creating a world that works better for people like him. It’s about making room for others to find liberation from their own struggles, as well. That ability to be grounded in your own experience while still making space for the experiences of others is at the heart of bridging differences. — Antonio Carrasco III 6. Intellectual humility and curiosity pair well together; when in doubt, admit what you don’t know and let your curiosity guide you forward If you’re exhausted by watching people argue about politics, identity, and social issues, watch people actually talk to each other instead. The series doesn’t suggest that differences disappear when people sit down together. It shows that disagreement can be productive when people approach each other with curiosity and humility. Iliff School of Theology student Lindsay Ambler’s story affected me in a way I didn’t expect. It was hard to hear how painful some of her experiences within religious spaces had been. Learning how she was rejected after people discovered she was gay gave me a much deeper understanding of what she carried into the classroom. It reminded me how little we may know about someone until we give them the space to share their story. Logan Midler from the University of Maryland stands out to me, too. I appreciate how honest she is about not having much interaction with people from different backgrounds and how easy it can be to start those conversations. Her desire to listen, learn, and become a better ally feels like a simple yet powerful example of bridging differences. — Ryan Carrasco I hope there’s a moment in Connecting on Campus that sticks with you, too. Maybe that memorable line, that bit of dialogue, or that mindful pause will become a touchstone for your own experiments in bridging differences. As another academic year begins, may the stories in this series also serve as an example of what students are capable of doing when they’re given the space and support to practice connecting with one another. Campuses across the country are facing real challenges—everything from threats of censorship to rising costs and cuts to funding—and these opportunities to practice bridging differences must not be sacrificed along the way. They’re too important to our individual and collective well-being. Watch Connecting on Campus: Stories of Courage, Curiosity, and Empathy.

#4118. Can Community Programs Reduce Gun Violence?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A new study by researchers at UC Berkeley’s Center for Global Healthy Cities published in the Journal of the American Medical Association Network Open found that the California city of Richmond’s Office of Neighborhood Safety (ONS) was responsible for a 61% reduction in that city’s firearm homicides and injury shootings over the last 14 years. This is one of the only studies in the country to document the sustained impacts of a community violence intervention program on city-wide firearm violence over more than 10 years. According to lead author Alice Bruno, a doctoral student in epidemiology at UC Berkeley’s School of Public Health, “we leveraged data from dozens of comparable cities across the United States to estimate what firearm violence would have looked like in Richmond without the ONS, Peacemaker Fellowship. We found that the Fellowship contributed to long-term reductions in violence, and that the declines only grew as the program operated.” Other UC Berkeley coauthors include Jason Corburn; Mahasin Mujahid, chair of the Division of Epidemiology; Sean Darling-Hammond; and Juan Cabrera. Sam Vaughn, director of ONS, said, “The statistics back up what we have seen in the streets: fewer shootings, fewer deaths, and a healthier community overall.” Homicide rates are down across the country; the FBI recently released data showing murder rates are down a record 18.1% nationally. However, Richmond has consistently outperformed national declines for over a decade, suggesting the results are due to that city’s innovative, street-outreach interventions and their mentorship of active firearm offenders, a program called Operation Peacemaker Fellowship, which was instituted in 2010. The Fellowship uses a public health approach to preventing gun violence.   According to James Houston, the program manager of the ONS who oversees the day-to-day implementation of the Peacemaker Fellowship, including managing 14 street-outreach workers who currently serve 123 participants, “the credit goes to the young people making better decisions and the team that gives them support, mentorship, and encouragement every day to make a change.” According to Shasa Curl, Richmond city manager, Richmond had over 300 police officer full-time equivalent in 2014 and 180 in 2020, but was down to 121 active sworn officers by 2024. Yet during this time, the ONS, through continued city support and grants, was able to expand its staff and increase the number of fellows and participants. Even during the COVID-19 period, the study shows Richmond did not have as significant a spike in firearm violence as most cities did across the Bay Area and nationally. According to the ONS, the health impacts are lives saved, safer streets, and community members with a renewed positive life outlook. One former Peacemaker Fellowship participant said, “Before the ONS came to me, I didn’t care if I survived past 19, as long as I had a name in the streets. After my experience with the ONS, with my mentor coming at me every day, I got support for my stress, got a job, paying my bills, and have a kid now. I’m no longer terrorizing my community.” The paper notes the cascading health benefits for entire communities when gun violence reductions are sustained, such as the case in Richmond. Further research will explore how unique aspects of the ONS might be contributing to declines in gun violence, including that the program is a city government agency, independent from the police department, has had consistent funding, has trained and retained many of the same outreach workers for over a decade (these city staff are well paid with benefits), and delivers individualized supports to each participant based on their needs, such as food and housing assistance, cognitive behavioral therapy, addiction and anger management counseling, and other social services aimed at both reducing gun violence risk and addressing trauma. This article was originally published on Berkeley Public Health News. Read the original article.

#4116. How Love Saves Lives

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When someone takes his own life, the grief of parents, lovers, children, and friends can be unspeakable. At a memorial for one young suicide I attended a few years ago, the father of the deceased could barely walk to the altar to light a candle. Everyone was crying. How could this talented, beautiful young person not know she’d be terribly, horribly missed? Most experts attribute suicide to severe mental illness caused by a brain injury or genetic predisposition, or due to some other, as yet unknown, physical or chemical trigger. Severe depression, bipolar disorder, and schizophrenia are all inordinately common among people who die by suicide. Severe mental illnesses are no doubt responsible for many, if not most, of the eight hundred thousand or so suicides that occur around the world each year, including, perhaps, that young person’s whose memorial I attended. My book, Why Live: How Suicide Becomes an Epidemic, isn’t primarily about such cases—or about how to prevent suicide. Such a book would have detailed sections about psychotherapy, restrictions on firearms, safety barriers for bridges, and other measures that really do save lives. Instead, my aim was to explore why some people want to take their own lives in the first place and how that desire might be reduced, in both individuals and communities. Having spent the past few years reading about suicide, speaking with scores of people who’ve experienced suicidal feelings, and having experienced them myself from time to time, I’ve come to understand that what suicidal people feel is the indifference, not of the universe, but of those closest to them. They sense that their own feelings don’t matter to the people they care most about. These experiences are backed up by studies showing that the main reason suicidal people want to die is because they think no one cares about them. Some suicides even seem to believe they’re doing everyone a favor by taking their own lives. Psychologist Thomas Joiner, who’s been studying suicide for thirty years, calls it a force of nature that distorts people’s minds so they become convinced that they are a burden to others and no one wants them around. In study after study, the most common trigger for suicide is a perceived rupture in a close relationship, either with a lover, parent, or other close relative, that seems irreparable to the suicidal person. Given that suicide seems driven by fraught or broken connections, it’s perhaps not surprising that I also discovered that suicide can spread within social groups like a disease. The suicide rate of particular groups can jump—sometimes increasing three- or four-fold or more—in a short time, meaning it behaves like an epidemic, like malaria during the rainy season or flu in the fall. Suicide epidemics unfold more slowly than these microbial plagues—over years or decades, not weeks or months—but they happen far too quickly to result from genetic changes, and affect far too many people to be explained away as spontaneous cases of biologically caused mental illness or brain injury. The causes therefore must have something to do with the changing social environment in which these people live. Suicide epidemics have occurred on virtually every continent. Between the 1820s and World War I, suicides nearly tripled in Belgium, Italy, and France, and they quadrupled in Austria. In 2014, young veterans of the Iraq and Afghan wars were roughly five times as likely as same-aged civilians to take their own lives. The suicide rate doubled among middle-aged Russians after the collapse of Soviet communism in the early 1990s. It quadrupled in white middle-aged working-class Americans after deindustrialization commenced in the 1970s. Between 1980 and 2003, suicide among Inuit youths in the Canadian Arctic soared from almost zero to a rate 10 times higher than that of the white majority Canadian population. What’s scary about this is that it means suicide could come for any of us. The very existence of suicide epidemics proves that people with suicidal feelings aren’t a species apart. Anyone can feel suicidal under certain conditions—and the key to stopping suicide epidemics is to change those conditions. Why suicide spreads I teach and write about public health and have long been fascinated by epidemics because they always tell a story. In most literary depictions of epidemics, such as Albert Camus’s The Plague or Daniel Defoe’s A Journal of the Plague Year, they seem to strike from nowhere, but this isn’t how epidemics typically behave. Individual cases of disease are hard to predict. Some of us will become ill tomorrow, but there’s no telling who. Even those who take risks may well escape the consequences. What might cause a suicide epidemic? First, it’s important to understand what doesn’t. Suicide isn’t more common during wars or where people are poorer or have recently experienced some environmental disaster like a flood, earthquake, or wildfire. Suicide was common among African slaves, especially on the ships en route to America and when they first arrived, but despite segregation, police brutality, mass incarceration, and other outrages, the suicide rate of African Americans has been generally lower than that of U.S. whites since record-keeping began in the 1930s. What seems to matter is whether people believe someone, somewhere, really cares about and understands them. The mental pain that drives suicide is really the flip side of love, says Israeli neuroscientist Yoram Yovell. “It probably evolved along with motherhood. . . . Inherent in us are very strong emotional forces that function to keep mothers and babies together. When they get too far from each other, they feel mental pain,” which we experience when we search for love. This is why “so many children’s stories are about a boy looking for his lost mother.” Evidence supports Yovell’s theory. A 1999 study found that some 78% of high lethality suicide attempters had recently experienced the loss of a close personal relationship, and suicide notes often express love for those left behind. In 2012, Zimri Yaseen and his colleagues reported that suicidal people interviewed in emergency rooms were especially likely to report that in the three preceding days, they’d felt “unusually intense or deep feelings of love,” which they presumably also felt were not reciprocated. But why would large groups of people—from the islands of Micronesia to the Arctic Circle, from the cities of post-communist Russia to the towns of contemporary America—suddenly begin questioning their most intimate attachments? How does love suddenly disappear—or seem to—from the lives of thousands of people? In my book, Why Live, I argue that it has to do with how we internalize socioeconomic change. Fragmenting societies, isolated individuals The first well-researched suicide epidemic occurred across Western Europe during the 19th century. Intellectuals debated the causes as we debate the vicissitudes of the stock market. Rising suicide rates were attributed to socialism, atmospheric moisture, the vice of masturbation, and the Romantic era inclination to preserve—through death—the passions of youth against the onslaught of Bourgeois boredom brought on by industrialization and urban life. Doctors even searched, in vain, for a “suicidogenic” organ in the cadavers of people who’d died by suicide. But others blamed society, that collective property of human groups that shapes our thinking and behavior. As a Jew growing up in France amid increasing anti-Semitism, Émile Durkheim had seen how shared values and beliefs buffered his people against the effects of discrimination and how anti-Semitism tightened bonds among them. He went on to become one of the founders of sociology, which is the study of those bonds: what holds us together, what drives us apart, and what happens as a result. In his 1897 book, Suicide, Durkheim theorized that suicides increase wherever society is fragmenting and people are growing more detached from one another: where divorce rates are higher, for example, and where Protestants, with their individualistic culture and aloofness from collective ritual, outnumber Catholics. He likened it to entropy—the universal tendency toward chaos and disorder at the atomic level discovered by physicists earlier in the century. Appearing on the eve of humanity’s, as yet, bloodiest century, Durkheim’s book seemed to foreshadow the trouble to come. What is it about fragmenting societies that causes mental pain? Durkheim was vague, even a little loopy, when it came to explaining this. At the time, psychologists were just discovering the unconscious, which they explored through studies of hypnosis and hallucinations. Durkheim followed this research closely, and believed that values and meanings flowed through populations like electrical currents, as if societies had what scholars Laurent Mucchielli and Marc Renneville refer to as a “collective soul.” These currents could change people’s behavior through unconscious mechanisms, just as changes in temperature and pressure change the behavior of molecules. Many late-19th-century scientists believed that our brains generated signals analogous to radio waves. Alexander Graham Bell, Nikola Tesla, and James Clerk Maxwell even wired up helmets and other gizmos to try to measure them. Mark Twain told Harper’s Monthly that thoughts could shoot from brain to brain, without their owners having to open their mouths to communicate. Likewise, Durkheim maintained that whatever suicidal people said about their feelings were just expressions of resolutions planted in their minds by the vibes of a disintegrating society. The brain-wave theory eventually fell out of fashion. In the 1930s, one of Durkheim’s protégés came closer to explaining the psychological effects of social fragmentation. Maurice Halbwachs taught at the University of Strasbourg from 1919 until his death in a Nazi concentration camp in 1945. His 1930 monograph The Causes of Suicide identified an existential loneliness that, if not entirely new, flourished in the growing towns and cities of 19th-century Europe and the countryside they engulfed. According to Halbwachs, cities create opportunities for social and economic advancement, but they also create the conditions for exploitative relationships, swindles, romantic betrayals, fragile friendships, broken families, unstable jobs, and many other sources of despair. As European cities grew, so did a new class of people who were particularly prone to suicide, including abandoned lovers, lonely widowers, workers who’d lost their jobs, nobles shamed by some dishonorable act, gamblers who couldn’t pay their debts, merchants who’d ruined themselves, heirs who’d squandered their fortunes, and others who’d suffered a common bitter fate: sudden exclusion from a cherished social community. As Halwachs writes: They all see their social level lowered. They are to a certain extent declassed. But what does it mean to be declassed? It is to pass from a group that one knows, that esteems you, to another that ignores you and whose appreciation one has no reason to hold. One then feels a void opening around oneself. Those who once surrounded you, with whom you had so many ideas in common, so many prejudices in common, with whom so many affinities brought you together because you found yourself in them as they found yourself in you, suddenly move away. You disappear from their concerns and their memory. Those among whom you find yourself understand neither your disorientation, nor your nostalgia and your regrets. Detached from the group by a sudden shock, you are incapable, or at least you believe yourself incapable, of ever finding in another any support, nor anything to replace what you have lost. In the midst of the 19th-century suicide crisis, the very meaning of the word “lonely” changed, from an adjective typically used to describe empty places and people who stood alone before God to one with the emotional and psychological connotations it has for us today. Suicidal despair, Halbwachs wrote, had been rarer in the past because traditional rural life was simpler and more predictable: We lived on the spot . . . adapted to each other, knowing each other too well to be frequently exposed to the clashes that occur when we move from one place, one situation, one profession, to another. Business carried less risk. Ambitions were less aroused, humiliations rarer. We thought and felt in common. Sorrows and troubles, instead of concentrating within the consciousness of this or that individual, dispersed and died out within the group. What Halbwachs was describing was the transition from what German sociologist Ferdinand Tönnies called Gemeinschaft, or communities based on trust, mutual respect, and sharing of whatever material goods one had, to Gesellschaft, societies based largely on market transactions and political calculation in which ties of friendship and loyalty are relegated to private family life, and may not even reliably be found there. As far as I’ve been able to determine, wherever that transition has occurred, a suicide epidemic has followed swiftly behind. But there is another ingredient, too. As villagers shifted away from subsistence and small-scale farming to wage labor and trade, their intimate relationships became both more fragile and more precious. The extended family, which once formed a social mesh that supported everyone, waned in significance, and walls rose up around increasingly private and nuclearized families, usually dominated by a single, generally male breadwinner, upon whom everyone else in the family depended. If you felt your family didn’t respect and care about you, you were in a new sort of trouble, not just because the community no longer offered you a livelihood, but because your whole identity was at stake. Love and belonging Modern capitalist society creates, in all of us, something of the double consciousness described by the great African American sociologist W. E. B. DuBois. When a modern person looks at a stranger, he wonders not only, as his villager ancestors did, “What can I do to help this person? And is he the kind of person who will help me?” but “What does that stranger see when he looks at me? What essence does he detect? A beauty or a beast? A success or a failure? A respectable person or a fool?” The answer sometimes comes as a disappointment. In traditionally cohesive societies, this mattered less, because a person’s worth was measured by what he did for others, and everyone was capable of contributing something. You might not be the best hunter, fisherman, storyteller, or drummer; you might not be the best grower of breadfruit or builder of igloos; but you still mattered. Only the greedy and selfish were looked down upon and excluded. In modern societies, generosity still counts, but for far less than it used to. If others find us wanting—because of our looks, talents, graces, bank accounts, intelligence, or for whatever reason—we are alone with it, and must make sense of it, somehow. For most of us, what makes modern life, with all its loneliness and insecurity, bearable are close relationships with people we count on to see and accept us for who we really are—whatever our flaws. Romantic rejection is exquisitely painful because we feel that that special quality within us, the thing that makes us lovable—and everyone has something—has either been unrecognized or, worse, seen and rejected. The pain of rejection by a parent or other close relative is even worse because it can make us feel as though no one could ever understand and sympathize with us. After all, they know us best of all. In the absence of an abstract sense of group belonging, interpersonal love, especially between parents and children and romantic partners, has become the main source of our psychological resilience, our ability to withstand the disappointments encountered in the outside world, just as the mutually reinforcing group gave strength to our ancestors fighting for survival on the plains, tundras, beaches, and forests of the past. Love is not just a feeling; it’s also a culture, which developed fairly recently, first in Western societies, and then around the world on the heels of capitalism, modernity, and individualism. It’s no coincidence, the social philosopher Erich Fromm pointed out, that as modern ways of life displaced the traditional, cohesive village and clan, love poems, songs, and stories came to occupy the center of Western culture, displacing archaic tales of heroism and godliness. It’s not that love was absent from the art of the ancient past, but as often as not, it was seen as a cause of calamity and human folly. Think of Helen of Troy, Dido and Aeneas, Adam and Eve. Then, more or less in lockstep with commercial culture and urbanization, love came to be seen as a force superior to the gods or God or honor or historic destiny. The behavior of parents and spouses changed, too. Until the 1800s, childrearing in England, for example, was seen as an exercise less in nurturing than in training for adulthood. Men who were fed up with their wives could sell them at markets or abandon them and marry someone else without divorcing. Infants who died weren’t even listed in parish registers. Only in the 19th century did heartfelt observations of sons and daughters begin appearing regularly in the diaries of American and English mothers. Regrowing community With the growth of markets and formal state institutions, people came to rely less on kinship networks for social welfare and trade and the family gradually shifted from an economic enterprise to a child-centered haven in a heartless world. The 1700s saw the first toy shops, women’s magazines, popular child care manuals, and family portraits. Christian churches, which had previously stressed family discipline and sometimes even the virtues of wife-beating, now promoted ideals of conjugal and parental love. Today, birthday parties, children’s allowances, and Valentine’s Day cards are symbols we use to assure each other that our most important relationships are real. Meanwhile, parenting manuals, happily-ever-after fairy tales, Hollywood movies, and the novels of Jane Austen, George Eliot, Charles Dickens, Barbara Cartland, Colleen Hoover, and countless others illustrate the enactment of love according to Western norms. Premodern Europeans, as well as Indigenous groups the world over, didn’t need this culture because in the mutual aid communities of bygone days, you knew who you were and what you needed to do to gain the esteem of others: You had to pitch in for the group. You helped others and they helped you. That was love, and according to accounts of anthropologists, it was everywhere, as ubiquitous as air. But when these groups modernized, in some cases very rapidly under the forces of capitalism and colonialism, the rules changed. Now it was unclear what the common goals were and what others were looking for in us. If they didn’t like us, there wasn’t a lot we could do about it. If the people we loved—our romantic partners, parents, and children—felt that way, it was intolerably painful because our hearts had nowhere to turn. Every rejection could now elicit devastating feelings of aloneness. “Who am I?” we are left to wonder. “Am I worth anything at all to anyone?” Signifiers of love—birthday cards and so on—aren’t enough. We need the confidence that our own feelings are understood. Some people, either because of the distortions of mental illness or because they really are lost and alone, simply don’t have it. But all is not lost. As I argue in Why Live, communities can regrow, just like decimated forests. The best policy I can think of is to strive for greater economic equality, which will naturally reduce insecurity, and, in turn, foster greater spiritual as well as material generosity. We are kinder to one another when we don’t feel threatened. Otherwise, the best advice I can think of is not to make things worse. When positive communities begin to emerge, the trick is not to ruin them.  Let’s treat communities like ecosystems so that every time a major economic policy shift is proposed—a massive new trade deal or development project—the effects on communities are considered, and the preservation of Gemeinschaft is prioritized. Ideally, studies of this, analogous to the environmental impact studies now required by all World Bank loans to developing countries, would be conducted with the full and meaningful involvement of affected communities. Some elements of Gemeinschaft survive, even in these harsh times—in well-functioning neighborhoods, in friendship networks, sometimes in the military, and in some schools and workplaces. You know it when you see it; it’s where you feel your dignity is recognized. In defending and building that, we give everyone more reason to live.
