#2925. Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Help Me Figure Out Some Life Logistics

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I’d probably look for PNW-area jobs first and consider the move if you find something. Many in your situation (roots in multiple areas) solve their problems by throwing money at it and maintaining two residences and splitting time. It’s typically not the most frugal option of both places are in the US, but to make it work, you generally need the job to be in the higher cost-of-living area.

There’s no predicting how the interpersonal relationships will play out so I wouldn’t fret too much over that. It’s just one of the risks you need to weigh against other things like how much house you could afford or what kind of schooling you’d have or how you plan to manage (or not manage) care for your parents in either location.

If you didn’t have kids, I would also encourage saving up for a test run by renting in the PNW for a few months to really test your theory, but that may be a bit too disruptive depending on their ages.

#861. Kim Kardashian Is Nothing if Not Committed to a Cutout

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Kim Kardashian has never met a cutout she didn’t like. Whether sparkling and sliced into the waist care of custom Erdem, a Gucci gown carved around the hips, or revealing considerably more, strategically placed flashes of skin have long been a Kim Kardashian style signature. And while her new partnership with stylist Law Roach has ushered in a more refined era of archival fashion, some things are simply too good to give up.

For a photocall in Rome promoting the second season of All’s Fair, Kardashian turned to bridal designer Galia Lahav for a rather unconventional take on polka dots. Plucked from the finale of the label’s fall 2027 couture bridal show, presented at New York Bridal Fashion Week earlier this month, the black-and-white dotted gown featured a sculpted, strapless corset bodice and dramatically low-slung cutouts that swooped around her hips and continued towards her buttocks. Below, the fabric gathered into a draped skirt with a sweeping train. Kardashian kept the jewelry to a minimum, pulling her dark hair into a sleek high ponytail and instead accessorizing with two very on-theme Dalmatians. Who needs diamonds when you have dogs?

Next stop: Mexico City. For the next premiere, Kardashian called upon a longtime favorite of hers—and the wider Kardashian-Jenner family—Schiaparelli. This time, she opted for a look from the house’s latest couture collection: a glossy cream silicone dress with a plunging, tightly fitted halterneck bodice and a skirt constructed from layers of hand-cut fringe, which parted to reveal a delicate, micro-polka-dotted sequin sheer underlayer. Her hair was worn long, slick, and gelled close to the head, keeping all eyes on the dress’s intricate construction and flashes of skin.

The two looks also mark a departure from the vintage-heavy wardrobe Roach has been assembling for Kardashian, which has included some archival pulls from Saint Laurent and Elizabeth Taylor-channelling Balenciaga. But even as she experiments with different designers, decades, and silhouettes, Kardashian’s devotion to body-conscious dressing remains steadfast. It’s a little creative continuity: the designers might change, but Kim Kardashian will always find a way to make the cut.

#2978. Personal Investments • Re: CD’s preferable to bond funds?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For long term, no on the CDs. You can use a short term bond fund or total bond market fund, such as BND which is basically an intermediate term fund. If you have a long time horizon then just get BND in a % of holding that you are comfortable with. The longer the time, the less in BND you probably want to hold. Look up glide path. You could also just get a Target Date Fund that has a built in glide path for simplicity. If you want cash like holdings for, say an Emergency Fund, probably better to hold something more liquid such as a MMF or ultra-short bond fund. SGOV or USFR for treasuries.

#871. At Celine, CEO Séverine Merle Is Taking Things One Triomphe at a Time

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

See all that bright red and blue showing up in clothes everywhere? You can probably thank Celine’s Michael Rider for that, and for the flocks of red sweaters draped over shoulders on city streets from Paris to New York. On the runway, too, the excitement around the designer’s individualized approach to dressing (and those disc-shaped glasses) is felt. Even the red carpet is reaping the benefit; just take Carey Mulligan’s pleated silver gown, for instance.

It’s clear that Rider is doing something increasingly tricky for luxury today: creating products that people truly want.

“We need to keep building on that desirability. It’s very strong, as you can see from the press coverage,” Celine CEO Séverine Merle says, speaking from her office at the brand’s HQ, housed within Paris’s Hôtel Colbert de Torcy. She herself is wearing the brand’s Heirloom necklace, featuring an array of charms.

Merle has been in the role since 2017, while Rider joined as creative director in October 2024. The designer returned to the house after working there for nine years under Phoebe Philo, and presented his well-received debut collection in July 2025. Since then, Celine has ascended: Rider’s last three shows — FW26, men’s, and women’s SS27 — felt like a trilogy of triumphs.

At the end of Paris Fashion Week, Vogue Runway and Vogue Business global director Nicole Phelps asked members of the Vogue team to share their favorite moments from the SS27 show season. “As usual it’ll be first-come, first-served. I’m grabbing Celine,” Phelps wrote in her email.

A silhouette from Celine's SS27 show featuring the chariot belt.

Celine, part of the LVMH Fashion Group, which Pietro Beccari has led since January 2026, is not immune to the challenging macroeconomic climate, nor the sharp slowdown in Chinese luxury spending. But Merle is keen to capitalize on the demand that is there — and is growing. “We’re seeing [desirability] take hold among our clients, and particularly among American clients. The ambition is for that desirability to reach increasingly wider circles,” she says. “Of course, we’re committed to remaining highly profitable to fund our broader ambitions. But we put desirability at the heart of the house.”

The brand’s strategy includes addressing both ends of the pricing spectrum, from ultra-high-end offerings to more accessible entry-level categories. And although not the main focus, menswear and fragrances play a role in enhancing the brand’s appeal.

With conditions in China still a challenge, the focus is now on local customers in markets where the brand enjoys strong desirability, including the US, South Korea and Japan. The brand’s regional sales mix is “more balanced than we have ever been”, Merle says. Europe, she adds, remains a very important market.

Founded in 1945 by Céline Vipiana, Celine was acquired by LVMH in 1996. From 2008 to 2017, Philo revitalized the house, transforming it into the go-to label for women drawn to minimalist chic and reaching around €1 billion in sales. Hedi Slimane took the helm as Céline’s artistic, creative and image director in 2018, before famously dropping the accented ‘é’ across all branding. During his tenure, which ended in 2024, he established an aesthetic that blended Parisian haute bourgeoisie with LA rocker glam, and expanded into menswear, couture and fragrance. “Celine is chic, sexy fashion for young people, even if the prices are what they are, and it works,” LVMH chair and CEO Bernard Arnault said at the group’s annual general meeting in April 2024.

After a period of rapid growth, Celine was hit by the broader luxury slowdown. LVMH does not break down sales at individual houses, but according to HSBC estimates and projections, sales declined from €2.6 billion in 2023 to €1.87 billion in 2026.

“Celine is doing better,” LVMH CFO Cécile Cabanis said during the group’s Q3 earnings call in October. She highlighted the double-digit sales growth across both the US and footwear, as well as the strong performance of the Soft Triomphe handbag, launched by Rider. Priced at €2,200, the Soft Triomphe Small Half Moon quickly became the brand’s top-selling bag. (Good luck getting your hands on the purple version.) “What Michael wanted to introduce when he arrived was bags featuring variations on the logo — with slightly smaller logos, softer leathers, and a much wider range of colors. The Soft Triomphe is a perfect example of exactly that,” says Merle.

Celine's top-selling bag: the Soft Triomphe half-moon priced at €2,200.

There’s more: Celine made its debut on Lyst’s hottest brand index at No. 20 in Q1 2026, climbing to No. 15 in Q2. Brand heat is also showing up in resale. On Paris-based luxury resale platform ReSee, the average price for vintage Celine has risen over 50% since Rider started, according to ReSee co-founder and CEO Sofia Bernardin. “We have had a huge uptick in archival Celine,” she says, noting that Phoebe Philo accounts for about 83% of the Celine sold, with the rest being Hedi Slimane and vintage. On Vestiaire Collective, searches combining “Celine” and “Michael Rider” increased 200% between January and September 2026. And following his SS27 show that featured bags with the Triomphe logo, searches for the Triomphe bag surged 1,800% compared with the month prior (October vs September 2026).

It is key to remember that Celine does not have the same budgets as the mega-brands — Louis Vuitton, Dior, Chanel — so Merle has her work cut out to ensure Rider’s Celine continues to ride high. After winning in the US, the challenge is to take its success global amid a crowded soft luxury market and challenging macroeconomics, which are weighing on consumers’ feel-good factor. The belief is there. “We are very confident that Celine is now getting the right level of traction to become a growth driver for us,” Cabanis said during the Q3 call.

Could Celine embody what comes after quiet luxury — a sophisticated yet effortless aesthetic with a playful edge? Backstage after the SS27 show, Vogue chief critic Sarah Mower noted the “undoneness” of the looks. “It’s what I respond to,” Rider said. “I am not turned on by something that’s too rigid, too figé. That’s not what I find stylish. What’s interesting about Celine is that tension, because it is a rigorous place. We are not living on the edge at Celine, and you are coming to get real clothes. I love that; I love when real clothes are worn spontaneously.”

The self-styling of Rider’s shows has been widely praised, as has the scenography of the re-see, which offers an original approach to presenting products through accumulation, layering and a play on color variations or monochromatic schemes. A similar approach is used at 53 Avenue Montaigne, the flagship store that serves as a pilot for the brand’s wider visual merchandising strategy. During a visit to the boutique on Sunday, I noticed the all-red product display at the entrance, the rainbow of silk scarves, and the color gradients of the bags. (I also tried on a fabulous long, black coat.)

“I think there’s a large constituency with finely tuned avant-garde antennae who are turning towards Celine,” Mower tells me. “It feels to me a bit like the buzz that grew around Loewe when Jonathan Anderson began there. It’s attracting those same people, who are after fashion that is not heavily identified with the conventionality of a major Paris house — but who want an insider’s kind of luxury.”

After putting on a standout show, the challenge is also coping with the attention that follows. “I think the real skill is staying cool and managing expectations,” says Mower.

Merle, it seems, masters the art of staying composed under almost any circumstance. My interview with her was scheduled for October 19. After seeing videos of the looted Celine store on Avenue Montaigne on Saturday, a couple of days prior, I assumed she would cancel or postpone. She didn’t. “The best thing we can do is get back to it as quickly as possible,” she says. By Wednesday, the store had reopened. (The store looting took place the same weekend as LVMH’s Les Journées Particulières, where Celine opened its atelier doors to the public for the first time. Visitors still turned up.)

The Celine atelier located at Celine HQ, housed within Paris’s Hôtel Colbert de Torcy.

What impact does she expect from the current unrest in France? “The French market accounts for almost half of Europe for us. So obviously, it’s important from a business perspective, but beyond that, it’s also important in terms of brand image. Celine is a Parisian brand,” she says. “We’ll see how things develop. Paris has been through difficult times before, but it always bounces back. It’s an incredibly resilient city.”

Stateside, the brand is on a hot streak. “We’ve invested quite a lot in the US over the past few years, because our retail network there was relatively small. This also coincided with the moment when Michael’s optimistic, joyful, and colorful vision and aesthetic really began to resonate very strongly with American customers,” she says.

After reopening on Rodeo Drive in September and setting up shop in Aventura Mall in October, there will be a new Celine store in Florida Bal Harbour by the end of the year. “New York is also performing very well for us, although we haven’t reopened any stores there. [The openings in LA and Florida] had been in the works for a long time,” Merle says. “In any case, we’re not pursuing a store opening strategy overall. We’re more focused on expanding and renovating existing stores. But it’s possible that we’ll open a few additional locations [in department stores] dedicated to footwear.” (Celine counts around 200 points of sale retail worldwide.)

Speaking of footwear, shoes are emerging as a key category for luxury, offering an entry point for customers. Rider has played his part in the trend. The Jelly and Cage sandals from SS26, as well as the Aura loafer from Rider’s debut spring 2026 collection with a small Triomphe logo in a wide range of colors, are among bestsellers, Merle says.

“With Michael, no category is neglected; it’s part of the way he thinks about dressing and accessorizing the silhouette. Across all our customer acquisition categories, everything is doing extraordinarily well,” she explains. Merle points to the coveted Maison Celine belt: “We’re struggling to keep up with demand.” Belts and silk scarves are seeing strong growth, with sales increasing at a double-digit and triple-digit pace respectively.

A Celine Winter 26 campaign featuring a silk scarf.

It’s also seen across bags. “We think Celine is a brand that needs to be well balanced. We’ve talked about accessories, but we pay very close attention to the prices of our bags. With everything we launch, we’re very careful to make sure it’s priced right. We have a model [for bags] that allows us to strike a balance between the two ends of the spectrum,” Merle says. Among bestsellers is the Duffle bag in canvas, priced at €1,300. “The canvas material allows us to offer highly creative bags at more affordable prices. We’re also launching lots of tote bags, because Michael has a real creative vision for totes. In fact, we saw them in the SS27 show.”

Menswear, couture, and fragrance all launched under Slimane. Men’s represents less than 10% of the business today. “I think there’s huge potential in menswear, because Michael’s creativity now appeals to a broader audience, but the idea isn’t to increase menswear’s share of total revenue,” says Merle. The idea is for the women’s and menswear to “enrich one another”, she adds, noting they share the same design studio.

Rider debuted his Celine menswear on the runway in June. “We like the idea of holding menswear shows, because it really allows us to express the category in its own right. There have always been a few men in our womenswear shows, too, ever since Michael arrived. I think that will continue. It’s part of the magic of the show.”

Couture, meanwhile, will stay without a dedicated runway for the time being. “For now, we don’t have the capacity to put on another show. That said, we’re really working on developing couture,” says Merle. “I can’t give you any figures, but today, we have a real couture clientele that is very loyal, as well as new clients. There’s a particularly strong appetite for our couture pieces among our American clientele. Some [pieces] are occasionally taken from the runway collections, while others are specially made in our ateliers to expand the offering [while remaining faithful to the spirit of the collection].”

Desirability is built via the beauty business, too, she says. “Fragrance, and everything related to bath and body that we’ve developed, is doing very well,” Merle adds. “Today, we sell these products only through our own network, along with a few retailers such as Le Bon Marché, Harrods, and Umeda Hankyu. We’re staying within our network and at points of sale like that for the time being.”

When I point out to Merle that almost a decade is a considerable tenure, particularly in an industry that continues to undergo major leadership reshuffles, and that she has worked under three successive designers — Philo, Slimane, Rider — she replies, “I feel like it all went by in a flash.”

#3243. Borregaard ASA (BRGAY) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Borregaard ASA (BRGAY) Q3 2026 Earnings Call October 28, 2026 3:30 AM EDT

Tom Foss-Jacobsen - Chief Executive OfficerPer Bjarne Lyngstad - Chief Financial OfficerPal Ramberg - Director of Investor Relations

Magnus Rasmussen - SEB, Research Division

Good morning, everyone, and welcome to Borregaard's presentation of the third quarter 2026 results. My name is Tom Erik Foss-Jacobsen, CEO of Borregaard. I'm joined today by our CFO, Per Bjarne Lyngstad. And together, we will take you through this agenda. I'll start with the main highlights, followed by an update on our business segments and the outlook for the fourth quarter. I will then hand over to Per Bjarne, who will take you through the financial performance in more detail. And before we begin, just a quick reminder to those of you watching the live webcast that you're welcome to submit questions at any time during the presentation, and we will address your questions at the end.

Let's start with the highlights for the third quarter. The key message is strong execution. EBITDA reached an all-time high of NOK 557 million with a record margin of 29.4%. Results improved across all business areas and BioMaterials delivered an all-time high result, supported by higher deliveries and strong production of specialty cellulose. More broadly, the performance was supported by high production and stable operations at our site in Sarpsborg, contributing also to lower unit costs. The result also benefited from savings from our cost improvement program and from optimization of our flexible energy system. #378. 7 Marathon Routes From Australia's International Gateways In 2026

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Merrillie Redden | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Long-haul commercial aviation out of Australian international gateways has entered an extraordinary phase of expansion, brought on by geographical necessity, advanced engine thermal efficiency, and dynamic airframe range limits. Positioned thousands of miles from the world’s major economic centers, Australian international hubs rely heavily on ultra-long-range twinjets and quad-jets to cross vast expanses of ocean in single non-stop sectors.

For passengers, these marathon flights offer unmatched point-to-point convenience and test the boundaries of human endurance, cabin environment optimization, and modern widebody dispatch reliability. To highlight the longest non-stop commercial sectors departing from Australian airports, this list takes a closer look at seven remarkable routes based on geographic flight distance, maximum scheduled block times, and operational network strategy.

Spanning transpacific corridors to Texas, seasonal links to Nevada, and direct bridges into European airspace, these long-haul sectors demonstrate how major global carriers maximize widebody range and payload economics.

Starting December 29, 2026, Qantas will launch Australia's first-ever direct non-stop air link to Harry Reid International Airport (LAS), running a seasonal schedule through March 12, 2027. Operating three times weekly as flight QF55, the non-stop route connects Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport(SYD) directly to LAS. The new transpacific flight removes the need for traditional intermediate transfers, cutting up to five hours off previous one-stop itineraries.

Flown using long-range Boeing 787-9 aircraft, the 8,144-mile (13,107 km) sector carries a maximum scheduled block time of 13 hours and 55 minutes on the eastbound leg, stretching to nearly 15 hours on the westbound return. The service caters specifically to high holiday passenger demand surrounding major global events, including the annual Consumer Electronics Show and peak North American winter travel seasons.

The seasonal Nevada link will be Qantas's fifth mainland destination in the United States, joining established hubs in Los Angeles International Airport(LAX), San Francisco International Airport(SFO), Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport(DFW), and New York JFK Airport(JFK). With the West Coast coastal hubs being skipped over on this route, the Australian carrier can now offer a seamless direct gateway for more than 250,000 Australian residents who visit Las Vegas each year.

Operating as flight QF5, Qantas's non-stop service between Perth Airport(PER) and Rome Fiumicino Airport(FCO) stands out as the carrier's main direct link into continental Europe. Covering 8,278 miles (13,322 km), the seasonal route connects Western Australia and Italy across a scheduled 16-hour and 25-minute westbound block time.

The Australian airline deploys two-class 787-9s on the corridor, outfitted with 42 custom business suites and 166 long-haul economy seats. To be able to keep up with surging European summer travel demand, Qantas expanded the route's seasonal schedule from four weekly flights up to daily frequencies running through late October, as per Qantas press releases.

With other long-haul European routes undergoing temporary routing adjustments due to active Middle East airspace closures, the Perth–Rome sector has become a vital direct conduit for travelers seeking to avoid traditional intermediate hubs across Asia and the Middle East.

It is the largest unserved market between Australia & the US. Find out more here!

Launched as the longest non-stop flight in American Airlines' global network, the seasonal service between Brisbane Airport (BNE) and Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport covers 8,299 miles (13,356 km). Operating as flight AA7, the eastbound transpacific sector takes advantage of favorable high-altitude jet streams, while the westbound return reaches a maximum block time of 16 hours and 50 minutes.

American Airlines assigns its own flagship 787-9 aircraft to the route, equipped with 30 Flagship Business suites and 21 premium economy seats. The non-stop connection gives Queensland passengers direct entry into American's massive Texas megahub, enabling rapid single-stop connections to over 200 destinations across North America, Latin America, and the Caribbean.

The route's operational feasibility is greatly dependent on the 787-9's advanced composite airframe and high-efficiency General Electric GEnx engines, which allow the aircraft to carry full passenger payloads over 8,000 miles (12,874 km) without taking on any severe weight and balance structural penalties that typically plague other aircraft types.

Spanning 8,578 miles (13,805 km), Qantas's daily service between Sydney and Dallas/Fort Worth is famous for being the airline's sole ultra-long-haul North American route operated exclusively by the double-decker Airbus A380-800. Flown as flight QF7, the westbound return sector from Texas back to Australia features a maximum block time of 17 hours and five minutes.

The superjumbo, due to its immense size, provides an increased passenger capacity across four distinct cabin tiers, housing 14 first class suites, 70 business class lie-flat seats, 60 premium economy seats, and 341 economy seats, as per Qantas seat maps. Landing directly at American Airlines' main international hub, the route forms a crucial commercial artery for corporate and leisure traffic connecting Australasia with North America's vast domestic air network.

Operating a four-engine aircraft over 8,500 miles (13,679 km) means that fuel density management and heavy-weight runway performance planning need to be at their best. The A380's massive upper-deck cabin ratio allows Qantas to capture high-yield premium ticket demand as well as offering robust lower-deck cargo capacity for transpacific freight that is especially important on this route.

Covering 8,596 miles (13,834 km), United Airlines' non-stop connection between Sydney Kingsford Smith and Houston George Bush Intercontinental Airport(IAH) holds the title of the longest single non-stop flight across United's global network. Flown as flight UA101, the westbound sector from Texas to New South Wales carries a maximum scheduled block time of 17 hours and 30 minutes.

United operates the ultra-long route using its long-range 787-9 Dreamliner fleet, featuring 48 United Polaris Business Class suites and 21 Premium Plus seats, as shown in Simple Flying cabin analysis. Due to prevailing Pacific headwinds, the return flight to Houston takes approximately 15 hours and 45 minutes, creating an operational block-time differential of nearly two hours between directions.

The route connects Australia directly with United's dominant Gulf Coast hub, serving as a critical commercial pipeline for global energy sector executives while providing seamless connections across two continents. With this route, United gives travelers from Sydney access to over 90 domestic destinations and more than 50 international cities across Central America, South America, and the Caribbean basin.

Boeing's modern twinjet excels on ultra-long-haul routes.

Covering an immense distance of 8,990 miles (14,468 km), Qantas's non-stop service between Melbourne Airport and Dallas/Fort Worth is the Australian carrier's longest active transpacific route in scheduled service. Flown as flight QF21, the westbound leg from Texas back to Victoria reaches a scheduled block time of 17 hours and 35 minutes.

Qantas uses its three-class 787-9s on the three-times-weekly service, featuring a custom cabin configuration designed specifically for extended passenger comfort over 17-hour flight durations. The direct flight allows travelers from Victoria to forget about stopping over at congested West Coast gateways in Los Angeles and San Francisco, though at the expense of a day and a half in the air.

For Qantas, the idea of connecting Melbourne directly to Texas came directly from the desire to use its oneworld alliance cooperation with American Airlines to capture lucrative corporate travel markets and to offer unprecedented single-stop access to major population centers across the central and eastern United States.

Covering a distance of 9,010 miles (14,498 km), Qantas's flagship QF9/10 service between Perth Airport and London Heathrow Airport remains Australia's most famous ultra-long-haul flight. Designed as a non-stop link in both directions with a maximum block time of 17 hours and 50 minutes, the route represents the modern evolution of the historic Kangaroo Route.

Due to active airspace restrictions across the Middle East, Qantas temporarily altered the outbound Perth-to-London leg to include a technical refueling stop in Singapore as flight QF209, as noted in Simple Flying coverage. However, the eastbound return leg, operating as flight QF10, continues to fly non-stop from London Heathrow directly back to Western Australia in under 16 hours and 30 minutes.

Despite operational rerouting challenges caused by international conflict zones, the route is still seen as a pioneering benchmark for long-haul commercial aviation, showing what is really possible when the boundaries of range and airliner endurance are pushed to their limits. It provides a real-world testbed for ultra-long-range passenger economics as airlines prepare for the next generation of non-stop travel, with Qantas aiming to push the limits even further with Project Sunrise. Qantas will use the first of its kind Airbus A350-1000ULR to fly directly between Sydney and London, as well as New York City, and when this route is finally inaugurated, it will overtake every route on this list to stand out on top.

#440. Scientists discover the immune system’s hidden sense of touch

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scientists at McGill University have discovered a surprising ability in immune cells that could reshape our understanding of how the body develops lasting protection against infections.

For years, researchers believed that tissue-resident memory T cells, specialized immune cells that remain in the body's tissues after an infection and respond quickly if the same threat returns, were guided mainly by chemical signals.

However, a new study published in Nature Immunology challenges that assumption. Researchers found that T cells can detect how stiff their surrounding tissues are and use this physical information to influence whether they develop into long-lasting memory cells. The findings suggest that adjusting to the stronger mechanical forces within tissues may be important for these protective cells to survive over time.

"This is an exciting fundamental finding that challenges the way we think about immune cells," said lead author Judith Mandl, a professor in McGill's Department of Physiology. "It suggests T cells can 'feel' their surroundings and adapt to them, making the physical environment an active force in shaping their behavior."

The research brought together scientists from McGill's Department of Physiology, the Alan Edwards Centre for Research on Pain, the Department of Bioengineering, and the McGill University Health Centre, along with collaborating institutions in the Netherlands and the United States.

How the Discovery Could Influence Cancer and Immune Treatments

Understanding how T cells respond to physical conditions could eventually open new possibilities for treating diseases in which the immune system mistakenly targets healthy tissue. These include autoimmune disorders, allergies, and complications involving transplant rejection.

The discovery could also have important implications for cancer research, particularly efforts to improve treatments that use the immune system to fight tumors.

"Tumors are often dense and physically difficult for immune cells to penetrate. Understanding how T cells adapt to these environments could help researchers design cancer immunotherapies that are better able to reach and attack tumors," said Mandl.

How Scientists Discovered the Immune Cells' Hidden Sense of Touch

To investigate whether physical surroundings influence T cell behavior, the researchers studied immune cells collected from humans and mice. They placed the cells inside specially designed collagen gels that mimicked tissues with different levels of stiffness.

The experiments revealed a clear pattern. As the gels became stiffer, T cells were increasingly likely to acquire characteristics associated with tissue-resident memory cells, which help provide long-term immune protection.

These results indicate that the physical properties of surrounding tissues can help shape how immune cells develop, adding an important new dimension to scientists' understanding of immune memory.

The researchers now hope to identify the specific molecular sensor that enables T cells to detect their physical environment. Determining how this sensor converts mechanical forces into changes in cell behavior could help explain how the immune system establishes lasting protection.

The study received funding from the Human Frontier Science Program, the Canadian Institutes of Health Research, the Canadian Foundation for Innovation, the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada, and the U.S. National Institutes of Health.

Materials provided by McGill University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#555. Unexpected cold water from the north delayed squid arrivals and surprised fishers in the northeastern US

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Study links a mysterious pulse of cold water off the northeastern U.S. coast to major fishing disruptions, including delayed squid arrivals and the return of cold-water mackerel.

Commercial fishers in the northeastern United States started seeing odd catches in the summer of 2023. Squid catches dropped off, while mackerel, a cold-water fish that had declined for years, suddenly returned. A cold pulse of fresh water was moving onto the continental shelf.

Longfin squid usually reach southern New England in May and stay through July. In 2024 they did not arrive until August.

Scientists at the Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution (WHOI) and NOAA now tie the delay to that cold pulse. The water came down from the Scotian Shelf and the Grand Banks, but the team can’t yet say what pushed it south.

The paper credits the fishing industry with spotting the event. Industry representatives and scientists held weekly informal calls, known as the Squid Squad, and Rhode Island’s Commercial Fisheries Research Foundation (CFRF) ran regular ocean update meetings.

By March 2024 several commercially important fish species were reaching New Jersey four to five weeks late. Adult shortfin squid tolerate 40 to 58°F (4.5 to 14.5°C) but prefer a salinity of 34.5 to 36.5. In May and June they were missing from the shelf break, the outer edge of the continental shelf.

Noelle Olsen, a senior research biologist at CFRF, said several captains told her in spring 2024 that their target species “were up to five weeks late or missed their usual spring run altogether.”

The R/V Roger Revelle sailed from Morehead City, North Carolina, on June 6, 2024, and worked until June 11. Magdalena Andres was chief scientist. The plan drew on the Squid Squad calls and a May meeting in Rhode Island.

The ship recovered moorings off Cape Hatteras and took CTD casts to 3,300 feet (1,000 meters) with a SeaBird 911+ instrument. It finished with a line north across the shelf along 71 degrees west.

Scientists on board traded notes with four squid companies on water conditions and on squid seen at the surface at night.

Fishing boats out of Rhode Island and nearby states have taken ocean profiles south of New England since 2014. The crews lower an RBR Concerto CTD, a probe that records conductivity, temperature and depth six or eight times a second.

CFRF and WHOI run it as the Shelf Research Fleet. More than 1,000 profiles have been logged, and the data are open to the public.

The study used 11 years of casts from Zone 2, the second of six rectangular zones south of New England. The team also pulled in NASA’s SMAP salinity satellite, NOAA satellite temperatures and the Revelle data.

In May 2024 the boats’ average surface salinity was 30.7, against an open-ocean average of about 35. No May in the 11-year record had been fresher.

NOAA’s ECOMON survey, running since 1977, ranks 2024 as the second-largest freshening of the Northeast shelf, after 1998.

Satellite maps first show the fresh water in July 2023. It lay along the shelf edge from the Tail of the Grand Banks to the eastern tip of Cape Cod. It spread southwest and toward shore and got to Cape Hatteras in December 2023.

By January 2024 it covered the Gulf of Maine, Georges Bank and the shelf down to Cape Hatteras. At the June 2024 peak, much of the shelf and slope was 1.5 to 2.0 salinity units fresher than normal. WHOI says surface water during the pulse ran 5 to 9°F (3 to 5°C) below the 20-year average.

South of Cape Hatteras, the Revelle read surface salinity of 35 to 36, normal there. Northeast of the cape it dropped as low as 29. The track average came out at 31.8, against about 33 for typical mid-shelf water.

On June 10 and 11, the surface along 71 degrees west, south of New England, was about 63°F (17°C). The R/V Neil Armstrong had measured close to 72°F (22°C) in the same area in June 2021. Over the slope, the fresh layer was 165 to 230 feet (50 to 70 meters) thick.

Longfin squid do best in water of 50 to 57°F (10 to 14°C). Few turned up in southern New England in June and July 2024. In late August, boats finally caught them southwest of Hudson Canyon.

The NOAA Fisheries landings database puts the Northeast longfin harvest at 39 million pounds in 2023 and 29 million in 2024. Its value dropped from $39 million to $32.5 million.

Shortfin squid did a little better. Their catch went up from 6.4 million to 7 million pounds in 2024.

Final 2025 numbers haven’t been tallied. Preliminary quota monitoring by NOAA’s Greater Atlantic Regional Fisheries Office shows the catch more than doubled by weight.

The pulse followed years of fast warming. The Gulf of Maine has been transformed by warming waters. Lobster fishers have faced an unprecedented decline in southern New England.

The authors looked upstream at the Labrador Sea and the Tail of the Grand Banks, off Newfoundland. They used daily sea surface height maps from the Copernicus Marine Service, with the 30-year trend removed. The 2023 maps point to a Gulf Stream lying farther south there than during 2009 to 2018.

Earlier studies proposed that a northward shift of the Gulf Stream can block the Labrador Current. That keeps its cold water off the U.S. shelf. The authors write that the 2023 southward shift fits that mechanism.

Monthly JRA55 wind records from the Japan Meteorological Agency showed a change over the Grand Banks in 2023 too. The 2009 to 2018 pattern vanished. In its place came one that earlier studies linked to a fresher Northeast shelf.

Glen Gawarkiewicz, a WHOI senior scientist, led the study. He said that “the coastal ocean in the northeastern U.S. has a very complicated seesaw between flow from the north bringing cold fresh water and flow offshore from the Gulf Stream bringing warm, salty water, and those influences swing back and forth. It’s always in flux.”

Paula Fratantoni leads the oceanography branch at NOAA’s Northeast Fisheries Science Center and co-wrote the paper. “A relatively small shift in the trajectory of a large-scale current like the Gulf Stream can lead to dramatic changes locally,” she said.

Because the Gulf Stream and the winds are linked, the data can’t show which one drove the event. The authors write that targeted model experiments will be needed.

Argo floats are free-drifting robots that dive and resurface while recording temperature and salinity. One of them, float 4902601, took five profiles over the slope in June 2024.

From those casts, the team estimated 200 to 460 cubic miles (840 to 1,933 cubic kilometers) of extra fresh water, a rough figure.

A 2024 model study had predicted a pause in the region’s warming. The cold of May and June 2024 was stronger than that, though the model averaged over larger areas.

By October 2024 a warm core ring, an eddy shed by the Gulf Stream, had ended the pulse. The authors add that the event differed from the cold blob south of Greenland in location, duration and salinity.

How each species responded still needs study, the authors say. In 2024 the first signs came from fishing crews, through the Squid Squad calls and the CTD casts made from their boats.

The full study was published in the journal Journal of Geophysical Research: Oceans.

#3905. In Life, as in the Navy, He Knew How to Change Course

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

The following is an excerpt from my memoir, And Now, Back to Me: Stories from an Empty Nest. How this man responds when he finds out he needs to stay in the rehabilitation facility, when his heart had been set on coming home, is all you need to know about the man I am so lucky to call my father.

Ten minutes or so after it’s been decided that he needs to stay in the rehabilitation facility for another two weeks instead of going home as scheduled, my father leaves a long message on my phone. He makes a point of speaking slowly so that I can take notes. “Reets,” he says. “I need you to get some things from my house. My cell phone is in the little cubby in my car. It should be fully charged. But just in case, the charger is in your bedroom.” I love that he calls it my bedroom, even after all these years. “Also, I need stamps, address labels, and note cards, which you’ll find in the drawers of my desk. And don’t forget the thumbtacks.” He must mean paper clips. Yes, I’m sure he does. “I really appreciate you doing this for me. I don’t know what I’d do without you girls.”

When I arrive at the rehabilitation facility the next morning, his favorite Dunkin’ Donuts coffee in one hand, a bag filled with his requested supplies in the other, I watch him go about the business of setting up shop. Here, in the corner of his room, the nightstand has become his filing cabinet. His bedside table is now his desk. The rocker is his chair. And with that, he’s ready to go about conducting the daily routine of his life, keeping himself busy, making his days productive, no matter where he is or what circumstances he might find himself in.

Maybe his ability to make do with what he has, to make his home wherever he happens to find himself, was born out of necessity. Growing up the youngest of twenty-one children (not a typo!), he and his brothers and sisters ate dinner in shifts and slept three to a bed. If his childhood didn’t teach him enough about how to make the best of what was available, then joining the Navy at age eighteen was all the education he needed. He quickly learned to turn a mug of black Navy-issue coffee into a morning ritual; to express a genuine interest in other people’s lives, total strangers though they might be; and to appreciate beauty, even tucked away in a little sanctuary he made for himself on the deck of a ship while waiting for the sun to rise on an early-morning watch. That is how he made his way in the world from World War II to Korea to Vietnam, and all the places in between.

This way of his, this uncanny ability to accept what comes, to adapt and change course at a moment’s notice, lies at the core, in my opinion, of why he recently celebrated his ninety-second birthday. It’s not as though it’s been smooth sailing. It hasn’t. In the time since we lost Mom, his wife of sixty-three years, there have been heart ailments, a hip replacement, a broken wrist, a small stroke, a blood infection, and a stubborn gallstone. Yet always, there he was, rising to the occasion and finding a silver lining.

As we’ve traveled through these challenges together, he’s said to me many times, “I am where I am. I don’t know why. I’m not sure what’s coming at me next. But I’m grateful to be here, with all the love and care that surrounds me. Might as well make the best of it.”

His words—which echo through my head along with the vision of my dad sitting at his “desk,” the bedside table in his rehabilitation room—are instructive as I go forward, arranging and rearranging the bits and pieces of my new way of empty-nest living. His inspiration reminds me that our attitude means more than any of our circumstances. Our mindset possesses the awesome power to change the course of our moments, our days, and, yes, our lives.

Lucky for me, I have his example to lead the way. I see him calling his relatives and friends to find out how they’re doing, going out to lunch whenever he gets the chance, sending notes of appreciation to all those who help make his days brighter. He's always showing genuine interest in the lives of those around him and finding ways to help however he can.

That acceptance of his, that grace of embracing whatever moment he finds himself in—perhaps shared mostly by example—that’s Dad’s best advice of all.

#1591. Pomona College strikes deal to become parent of Claremont Graduate University

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Under the agreement, the graduate institution will continue operating separately, led by its own board and senior officials.

According to a Pomona FAQ on the deal, the college will provide "financial and strategic" expertise to CGU, which is restructuring its academic portfolio and cutting costs to address its budget deficit. CGU will also gain access to "favorable terms" for financing through its affiliation with Pomona, the FAQ said.

Pomona is not taking on CGU’s debts and liabilities, nor is it planning to use its operating budget to fund the graduate university’s operations, according to the FAQ. CGU will also pay for administrative services from Pomona.

The graduate university has been struggling financially, with a $7.8 million budget deficit projected for this fiscal year. Earlier this month, it announced it would offer voluntary buyouts to faculty and staff to help close the gap.

The voluntary buyouts come on the heels of an academic overhaul at CGU, which plans to consolidate five schools and two centers into just three academic units. Those will be organized around three subject areas: management and technology; education, health and psychology; and arts humanities and mathematics.

According to the FAQ, Pomona serves as a “thought partner” to CGU’s leadership on the academic transformation and has made outside advisers available to the graduate university. Both institutions must accept the academic overhaul plan that CGU is developing before the deal closes.

CGU has struggled with enrollment. In fall 2024, it enrolled 1,771 students, down 18% from a decade prior, according to federal data. Pomona is similarly sized, with 1,700 students in fall 2024. However, that marks a 3% increase from a decade before.

Pomona also has the stronger finances of the two. In fiscal 2025, it logged a $16.9 million operating surplus on $258.5 million of revenue, according to its latest publicly available financial audit. It also ended the year with $3.8 billion in assets.

CGU, on the other hand, had an operating surplus of $4.6 million in fiscal 2025 on $68.8 million in revenue. It ended the year with $290 million in assets.

Both institutions are part of The Claremont Colleges, a consortium of seven higher education institutions on 600 acres of connected land.

“As the founding member of the Consortium, Pomona has a lasting interest in its strength and stability,” the college said in the FAQ. “This affiliation keeps graduate education in Claremont connected to an institution committed to the Consortium’s mission and collaborative model.”

The deal is pending regulatory and accreditor approval, as well as sign off from Claremont Colleges for CGU to continue being a member of the consortium.

#1608. Audit casts doubt on authenticity of emails from former Bard president to Epstein

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Democratic lawmakers have called for a federal probe into whether Leon Botstein participated in the convicted sex offender’s abuse and exploitation.

Democrats on the House Judiciary Committee on Oct. 7 called for a federal investigation into Botstein. They cited an email exchange in which the longtime Bard president asked if a young woman allegedly being trafficked by Epstein was "for me, or are you keeping her for yourself."

In a letter to Bard leadership, Rep. Jamie Raskin said he had "damning evidence that Botstein likely enabled and possibly even participated in Epstein’s abuse while serving as Bard’s President." He also said Botstein may have interfered with Title IX proceedings at Bard and protected "sexual predators" on its faculty, citing whistleblower allegations.

Botstein, who helmed Bard for over five decades, resigned as its president this summer amid intense scrutiny over his association with Epstein.

The same day as Raskin's letter, Bard Board Chair Brandon Weber announced the college was "undertaking a full review" of the lawmakers' allegations. Botstein, who had still held a professorship at Bard and positions within the colleges' music programs, stepped down from those roles effective immediately, Weber said.

Both Raskin and Weber said Bard has been cooperating with the committee's investigation, and Weber said the college’s leaders would continue to do so.

A few weeks later, Bard announced it had retained FTI Consulting as one of its first steps to review the allegations in Raskin's letter.

The firm's Oct. 19 audit found that the 2014 email exchange was not on Bard's servers as far back as 2017. FTI Consulting also highlighted inconsistent formatting and timestamps in the conversation as it appears in the DOJ's files and noted that the responses attributed to Botstein only appeared as in-text threaded messages, rather than as independent emails.

The audit did not address legislators' Title IX or faculty abuse allegations.

"Our review of the other claims in Representative Raskin’s letter remains ongoing, and we are committed to establishing the facts," Bard College said in an Oct. 22 statement. "In the meantime, we will continue our work to provide a secure and respectful learning environment for everyone at Bard."

Botstein's relationship with Epstein — and his downplaying of it to Bard's board and campus — have been a source of consternation for the college for months.

An independent investigation from law firm WilmerHale earlier this year found Botstein and Epstein were in frequent contact, both remotely and in person, from 2012 to 2019. During that period, Botstein made a trip to Epstein's private island and visited his townhome more than two dozen times.

Epstein made donations to the college — the details of which Botstein obfuscated from the board — and visited Bard twice, according to WilmerHale. He also paid Botstein under a consulting agreement.

The WilmerHale investigation found that Botstein did nothing illegal, and its summary did not address the emails central to Raskin's Oct. 7 letter.

According to auditors, Botstein said he “did not see a risk to Bard’s reputation in pursuing Epstein or the potential risk to Bard students of exposure to Epstein, nor did he consider that his actions could validate and legitimize Epstein to potential victims or their parents."

#2891. Inside The Westerly, Hilton Aruba’s New Luxury Tower

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The Westerly tower at Hilton Aruba offers exclusivity, resort amenities and ocean views at a premium price.

I’ve stayed at several upscale hotels in Aruba, but the personal service and incredible views from The Westerly at Hilton Aruba Caribbean Resort made it one of my favorites. Private check-in and one of the warmest concierge teams I’ve encountered gave my stay the intimate feel of a boutique hotel with the amenities of a large resort.

Opened in May 2026, The Westerly is a 161-room luxury tower within the historic Hilton Aruba Caribbean Resort and Casino on Palm Beach. Guests have access to the larger resort’s pools, restaurants and activities, plus perks such as complimentary use of the sauna and other wellness amenities at Eforea Spa. Cash rates start around $660 or 235,000 Hilton points per night. There are no standard rooms at The Westerly, so you won’t be able to use Hilton free night certificates for a stay.

For an additional fee, guests can access Terrace on 10, an adults-only rooftop club with meals, an open bar and sweeping Caribbean views.

I was invited to experience The Westerly during a three-night stay in September 2026. Although the hotel covered the cost of my room and food, the opinions expressed in this review are my own.

The Westerly shares the Hilton Aruba Resort’s 15-acre beachfront property in Palm Beach’s high-rise hotel district. Dozens of restaurants, shops and nightlife venues are within walking distance.

The hotel is about a 15-minute drive from Queen Beatrix International Airport (AUA). Rideshare services don’t operate in Aruba, and a taxi to the resort will cost $40. One of my favorite things about Aruba is the fixed prices for taxis across the island. Prices are set by the government, and you can look up the cost of a ride from one location to another at Taxi.aw.

If you rent a car, parking at the resort is included for registered hotel guests.

Cash rates start around $660 per night for a garden-view room and $1,000 for a swim-up room. Oceanfront and swim-up suites start around $1,800. These rates include the $79 nightly resort fee, which is waived on stays booked entirely with Hilton Honors points.

Hilton doesn’t publish an award chart, and the number of points required for a free night varies but starts at 235,000 points per night. At that price, a comparable cash stay would need to cost $940 to match NerdWallet’s valuation of Hilton points at 0.4 cent each.

The Westerly’s rooms are currently categorized as Premium Room Rewards, making them ineligible for Hilton free night certificates, which require standard accommodations. If you want to stretch your points balance or use a free night certificate, check out the standard rooms at the Hilton Aruba Resort or the Embassy Suites Aruba Resort.

The Westerly’s rooms and suites feature coastal colors, light wood accents and abundant natural light. The accommodations are larger, and the common areas feel brighter and more inviting than those in the resort’s other towers.

I stayed on the fourth floor in an oceanfront suite with a king bed. The room was massive, the bed was extremely comfortable and the bright colors and natural light were so inviting that I found myself not wanting to leave the room.

The views from the balcony were outstanding. It was such a peaceful place to watch the sunrise and get some work done.

All rooms feature a Lavazza coffee machine, a mini-bar and a wellness kit with a canvas bag and local Aruba Aloe products.

The Westerly also has the resort’s only swim-up rooms and suites, which are adults-only. They offer direct access to a shared pool, though choosing one means giving up the elevated views and greater privacy of an upper-floor balcony.

Guests at The Westerly can pay $150 per person per day for access to Terrace on 10, an adults-only rooftop club. The upgrade includes:

A breakfast buffet with made-to-order omelets.

A sunset reception with entertainment and hors d’oeuvres.

The space features modern art, outdoor cabanas and lounge chairs overlooking Palm Beach and the Caribbean Sea. During my visits, it felt like a quiet retreat from the busier resort, with attentive service and few other guests.

I ate four meals at the club. While the breakfast selections were good and a step up from what you’ll find at Laguna (the resort’s breakfast spot), lunch was by far my favorite meal and included tomato carpaccio, spring rolls with peanut sauce, tuna crudo, roast beef sliders and a shrimp cocktail.

The daily $150 per person fee is steep, but could make sense if you:

Plan to wine and dine on-property for the duration of your stay.

Want the best view I’ve found anywhere on Palm Beach.

Crave a more exclusive, white-glove resort experience.

The resort’s dining options include the renovated Sunset Grille steakhouse and Laguna breakfast restaurant, plus the new beachfront Shore Club.

Sunset Grille was my favorite dinner spot of the stay. The steakhouse offers indoor and outdoor seating overlooking the resort, and both the sea bass and scallops were excellent.

The tableside gin cart added a memorable touch: Staff brought it over and prepared our cocktails in front of us. The chocolate cake was another highlight — good enough that I returned for it a second time at the end of my stay.

Opened in 2026, Shore Club is a two-story, open-air restaurant and bar steps from Palm Beach. It’s accessible to both resort guests and the general public and serves craft tropical cocktails, sushi, ceviche and burgers.

The rooftop opens for dinner, with a DJ creating a lively setting for sunset drinks. I enjoyed the sushi and cocktails, but the staff made the strongest impression. Our server was personable and energetic, and the bartender spoke multiple times about how much he loved making cocktails.

Laguna serves breakfast à la carte and from a buffet, with homemade pastries, fruit and made-to-order eggs and omelets.

Hilton Diamond members receive complimentary continental breakfast for up to two guests sharing a room, including fruit, pastries and cereal. During my stay, an upgrade to the full buffet and unlimited mimosas cost $14.

This was the one place where the resort felt crowded during my visit, as it’s the main breakfast spot at the resort. I enjoyed the breakfast burrito and the buffet’s fresh fruit, though the options and ambience at Terrace on 10 were superior.

The Westerly offers direct access to Palm Beach, with white sand and calm water that make for great swimming. I spent each morning in the water and saw multiple turtles and lots of tropical fish. Restaurants, beach bars and water sports operators line the shore near the resort, putting plenty of activities within walking distance.

The resort offers lounge chairs beneath palapas (thatched shade structures), with food and drinks available to order from your seat. Beach seating can be reserved through The Westerly’s concierge or the resort’s online booking system (for an additional fee that varies based on demand).

Guests at The Westerly share two main pools with the rest of the Hilton Aruba Resort. Both had recently been resurfaced, and there was enough space to spread out so that neither felt overly crowded during my stay.

The Westerly’s guests receive complimentary access to amenities of the Eforea Spa throughout their stay, including the relaxation area, sauna, cold plunge pool and steam room. There are 10 treatment rooms for services including facials and individual and couples massages, a full-service salon and a spacious fitness center.

The property’s Las Vegas-style casino includes one of the island’s largest poker rooms, slot machines and table games including craps, roulette and blackjack. Guests at The Westerly receive a voucher for $25 of match play for both the tables and slot machines.

There was no shortage of on-site activities throughout my stay, including a variety of fitness classes, yoga and morning paddleboarding in the Caribbean Sea. But my favorite was hanging out with the rescue birds at the property’s bird garden and sanctuary. There were around a dozen tropical birds, including macaws, parrots and a toucan. Staff open the sanctuary for visitors each afternoon, with an opportunity to feed and take photos with the birds.

The Westerly is worth considering if you want personal service and modern accommodations with the amenities of a large beachfront resort. My oceanfront suite and the warm, attentive staff made it one of my favorite hotel stays in Aruba.

The main drawback is the price, as a room at The Westerly will cost a few hundred dollars more than one with a comparable view at the Hilton Aruba Resort. Guests in the Hilton’s other towers share many of the same facilities, so compare rates before paying more for The Westerly’s rooms and additional perks. High award prices also make it a less compelling choice for travelers trying to stretch their Hilton points.

Terrace on 10 adds excellent food, sweeping views and a quiet place to unwind, but it’s a separate splurge. Its value depends on how much of your vacation you plan to spend enjoying the club.

#563. Light pollution is reaching deep into the sea, putting marine life at risk

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Artificial light from coastal towns and offshore installations is penetrating Europe's seas, raising concerns about its effects on marine ecosystems.

Light pollution is often discussed as a problem for people trying to see the Milky Way. But artificial light also reaches beneath the ocean’s surface, where it can disrupt marine life.

The glow of a seaside town doesn’t stop at the water’s surface. It penetrates the sea, and in clear water, it can reach deeper than a three-story building is tall.

A new risk map reveals which of Europe’s seas face the greatest exposure to artificial light and where marine life may be most vulnerable.

The Adriatic, the Baltic, and the western Mediterranean come out worst, while the open waters around Iceland, the Barents Sea, and the islands of Macaronesia come out cleanest.

The map is built from satellite data and a brightness threshold, not from surveys of harmed animals.

Thomas Davies, a marine conservation researcher at the University of Plymouth, led the work with Tim Smyth of Plymouth Marine Laboratory and colleagues in Britain and Italy.

The goal was to develop a way to measure underwater light pollution risk, something European regulators have lacked until now.

In an interview with Earth.com, Davies explained that the most striking finding was how differently the risk was distributed across Europe’s seas.

“What is interesting is that most European Seas at high or moderate risk are small and bounded by developed coastlines around their periphery, the Adriatic for example,” he said.

“In those regions the maximum exposure risk is concentrated near the coast.”

The team needed a level of light at which marine animals begin responding. They used measurements from a copepod, one of the most light-sensitive marine animals studied.

These tiny crustaceans swim toward the surface at night as part of the largest daily migration of living organisms on Earth.

A tenth of a microwatt of white light per square meter is enough to move those copepods, and they can respond to moonlight more than 164 feet (50 meters) down.

The team used that tiny amount of light as its threshold, choosing a cautious approach to assessing risk.

Only about two dozen such measurements exist, and all involve open-water animals or larvae rather than seabed dwellers.

From there, a global atlas of undersea light gave the depth at which artificial light fades below that line.

Where artificial light fails to reach the threshold at the surface, the area is classified as having no exposure risk under the study’s criteria.

If artificial light reaches 3 feet (1 meter) below the surface, the risk is considered low. At depths of up to 33 feet (10 meters), the risk is moderate, and beyond that, it is high.

The atlas was sliced into 15 European sea regions and into bands by distance from shore.

Within 6 miles (10 kilometers) of the Adriatic coast, 56% of the water was classified as high risk, while another 43% was considered moderate risk.

The western Mediterranean faced even greater exposure near the coast, with 73% of its coastal waters classified as high risk.

The Baltic’s coastal band was mostly medium. Light pollution generally decreases with distance from shore, leaving most open waters at little or no risk.

Beyond 62 miles (100 kilometers) from shore, nearly all European seas were classified as having no risk from artificial light exposure. Only one region was an exception.

In the Greater North Sea, a fifth of the far-offshore water rated medium and 5% high, because oil and gas platforms light the middle of the sea.

Davies told Earth.com that this changes who is affected.

“In the North Sea, the maximum exposure risk is concentrated around these offshore installations in the middle of the sea,” he said.

“This means the marine species and ecosystems most impacted will be very different.”

The team ran a second map for life on the seafloor, using the light that actually lands on the bottom.

Water soaks up light on the way down, so the seabed is less exposed everywhere, and almost every sea region rated nil beyond 6 miles out.

The exception was close to shore. Within the first 0.6 miles (1 kilometer) of the Bay of Biscay and the Iberian coast, 49% of the seabed rated medium and 12% high.

The team put that down to dense coastal development. The Adriatic, Baltic, North Sea, and central Mediterranean followed, with low risk reaching 3 miles (5 kilometers) out.

The one-kilometer grid misses a lot. Ports and harbors produce more artificial light than the atlas can accurately capture, so the researchers emphasized the need for direct measurements in the brightest coastal areas.

The European Union’s marine strategy law of 2008 asks member states to keep “introduction of energy” at levels that do no harm. In practice that has meant underwater noise.

No country has set a target, threshold or test for light, and the law is now under review.

The paper adapts the approach used for underwater noise, demonstrating how exposure thresholds could help regulators assess artificial light pollution.

The authors stop short of saying what counts as good. That, they wrote, needs political agreement and consultation.

When Earth.com asked what he hopes the EU will do next, Davies pointed to the ongoing review as an opportunity for action.

“This paper demonstrates that the knowledge and tools are available to develop useful criteria,” he said.

“We believe we have clear evidence of risk and harm that urgently needs to be translated into meaningful action.”

Davies recommended that coastal communities follow the five lighting principles established by DarkSky International.

These include removing unnecessary lights, shielding fixtures to direct light where it’s needed, reducing brightness, using lights only when necessary, and choosing warmer bulbs that emit less blue light.

Blue light poses a particular concern because it penetrates seawater more deeply than many other wavelengths.

Most white LEDs emit substantial blue light, and their growing popularity could increase underwater light pollution.

According to the researchers, LED lamps grew from 2% of the world’s lighting market in 2012 to 48% in 2019.

The authors also suggest dark zones around islands, where tourists could see a sea without a lit shore.

Although the map identifies areas of potential exposure, it cannot determine how much harm artificial light is actually causing marine life.

The threshold is based on measurements from a single copepod species, and little is known about how sensitive seabed animals are to artificial light.

The atlas also relies on data from just one month, June 2019.

The team expects both the thresholds and the rankings to change as researchers gather more data on other species.

The map is online for any manager who wants to look at their own coastline.

The full study was published in the journal Ocean & Coastal Management.

#923. Artist Jane Benson Has Some Strong Ideas About ‘Frankenstein’

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Popular culture would have you believe that Frankenstein, Mary Shelley’s seminal 1818 novel, is a horror story of male hubris, about a mad scientist contending with the dangers of his own creation. And it is, at least in part. But within Shelley’s critique of patriarchal power, there is another voice that is often overlooked: her own. About a third of the way into the book, the narration switches to the perspective of the so-called monster as he tells Victor Frankenstein what has happened to him during the nearly two years he and his creator have been apart.

The six chapters written in the creature’s voice contain some of the most achingly beautiful prose in the whole novel. He details how he learned to understand language and emotion. How he marveled at the warmth of the sun, the blooming of flowers, the sweetness of fruit. How he grew to love the family he tenderly observed from his hiding place, and how devastating their rejection was. He explains how he came to know anger, loneliness, loss, rage—and the vengeful acts they inspired.

“Reading those chapters, I find it really hard to think of this as a man’s voice,” the British-born, New York–based conceptual artist Jane Benson tells me during a recent visit to her Chelsea studio. “This sounds like a woman’s voice crying for justice, and to be heard. It’s bathed in feelings of isolation and grief and desertion and betrayal—all things that in everyday life, that was the woman’s lot.” She came to think of the creature’s voice as not only Mary Shelley’s, but also a general evocation of the struggles women face in a misogynistic world.

Benson is not the first to find feminist undertones in Shelley’s Gothic classic—scholars like Barbara Johnson, Ellen Moers, and Sandra Gilbert and Susan Gubar have written about themes of motherhood and female authorship that they found embedded within Frankenstein. (When Shelley first published the book, anonymously, many readers assumed it was written by her husband, Percy Shelley.) But as an artist, Benson wondered how she might visually represent this idea of the monster’s voice as a woman’s. What would that look like? What would it sound like?

She tracked down a limited-edition facsimile of Shelley’s original Frankenstein manuscript, released to mark the book’s 200th anniversary, and began her delightfully obsessive deep dive into the text. The resulting artworks make up “her her,” Benson’s new gallery exhibition that opens at Sargent’s Daughters in Tribeca on October 30. (More of the series will go on view at Art Basel Miami in December.) The show contains more than two dozen works, mostly text-based drawings that use the author’s own handwriting as their main material—a decision that allows the focus to stay on the devastating phrases an 18-year-old Shelley used to illustrate the creature’s tragic arc.

“I haven’t altered anything,” Benson says of the drawings. “These are just blow-ups of her pages.… These sentences, that’s exactly how they appear on the page.”

But these are not mere re-creations of the manuscript. Instead, Benson has come up with myriad ways of excavating the text, something that has become a signature in her politically engaged art practice over the past three decades. (Benson, who is dyslexic, finds that this process gives her a way into a text that focuses not so much on the linear process of reading as on the ideas the text contains.) Some works in “her her” are as tall as six feet, and many of them are layered on iridescent yellow film with reflective borders. They seem to vibrate off the wall, as if Mary Shelley herself were knocking around the frame, begging us to listen.

Take the piece her her (2026), which lends its name to the exhibition and features a tight zoom on those two crossed-out words. In highlighting such an instance within the manuscript, Benson “aims to reiterate and emphasize the negation of women throughout history.”

For her “solfège” pieces, Benson has excised all words but the seven syllables of solfège (do, re, mi, fa, sol, la, ti), with the resulting score performed by mezzo-soprano Hai-Ting Chinn, a frequent collaborator of Benson’s. (In a bit of cheeky fun, the recordings will be played on a loop throughout the gallery.) As she cut out all the non-solfège words and letters, Benson found herself surrounded by “a sea of sentences.” Out of these cast-offs, she made little lyric poems befitting her themes. Like this one, missing its initial do: ubtless, / on I wept, without / my limbs / I sat.

And for the visually striking “counter works,” as she calls them, Benson filled in the enclosed space of letters like g and d and o (called “counters” in typography) to create a rhythmic pattern of ovals and dots—a kind of literary animal print. In most of the sentences, only the letters’ filled-in counter space remains, with the rest erased. But some choice Shelley sentences—I waited with forced patience until the moon had sunk, for one example—are left intact. “Those are the ones that drove me to being so struck by this being a woman’s voice,” Benson explains.

There are even more Easter eggs—like the varying shades of yellow, which come from tone notes that match up to the solfège scores—but at a certain point this body of work becomes less about how she’s doing it and more about the dedication to the idea. “I was just compelled to keep going and make the work so that these words can be seen.”

Benson first read Frankenstein alongside other classics at school (she was born in Thornbury, a town in Gloucestershire, in 1973, and grew up in the tiny village of Gawsworth in northwest England). Like many people who first encountered the book at a young age, and like countless others who have seen its various adaptations for the screen, she was swept up in the flattened narrative of a violent creature and the regrets of its maker. “You have the sort of similar arguments, like, Who’s the monster?” she says.

Then, a few years ago—before Guillermo del Toro’s Frankenstein and looser interpretations like Yorgos Lanthimos’s Poor Things and Maggie Gyllenhaal’s The Bride! dragged the story back into the zeitgeist, and after she became a mother—Benson reread the book, and those 40 or so pages from the creature’s perspective triggered a whole new set of subversive ideas for her. “Is a monster the person in society that nobody understands, and that’s why we’re afraid of it?” she wondered. “I’m like, That’s a woman. No one understood Mary Shelley; people were afraid of her.”

Frankenstein is often overshadowed by the lore of its author (the daughter of two famed English intellectuals, women’s rights advocate Mary Wollstonecraft and philosopher William Godwin); her relationships to men (at 16 she ran away to France with the poet Percy Shelley, a pupil of her father’s who was already married); and the fantastical circumstances under which she wrote the book (holed up during the stormy summer of 1816 in a Swiss villa rented by Lord Byron, who one night challenged Mary, Percy, Mary’s stepsister, and his personal physician to a ghost-story contest).

But perhaps more consequential to the writing of Frankenstein was the grief that Mary Shelley carried. “It’s hard to separate any of it from what she was going through at that time,” Benson says. Shelley never knew her mother, who died 11 days after Shelley was born. Seventeen years later, Shelley lost her first baby at less than two weeks old. She wrote in her diary, “Dream that my little baby came to life again; that it had only been cold, and that we rubbed it before the fire, and it lived.” She began writing Frankenstein the following year. (Shelley would lose two more young children, including the baby she nursed while writing Frankenstein, all before her 22nd birthday. Only her fourth child, Percy Florence Shelley, born in 1819, would live to adulthood.)

At Sargent’s Daughters, three tender graphite drawings of elm trees hang on the back gallery wall. Benson made them as an homage to both Shelley’s grief and her own, over the recent passing of her father. The margins of Shelley’s manuscript were full of doodles of what look like English elms, which covered the lush landscape in the part of England where Benson was raised. Benson loved these trees, and was devastated when many were lost to Dutch elm disease. “My first heartbreak,” she says. Her father photographed them back in the 1970s, and after his death Benson looked everywhere for the photos, to no avail.

Seeing them there in Shelley’s manuscript felt like this personal connection between author and artist, a reassuring echo across time and space. Keep going, the trees seem to say. Keep looking.

“Jane Benson: her her” is on view at Sargent’s Daughters in New York City from October 30 to December 5, 2026.
