#1099. Alright, I’ve Seen Enough—Let’s Stop Dating Male Podcasters

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Seeing a man with a mic on my social media is a jump scare. First, because a man with a podcast is a major ick in and of itself. Secondly, a majority of the time, what’s coming out of their mouths is gross, misogynistic, factually incorrect—or all three. And if it’s not what’s coming out of their mouths on the mic, it’s what they’re doing when they’re not “on air.” The latest news cycle only confirms this.

Now, I am proud to say that before this week, I knew virtually nothing about Ethan Klein aside from the fact that he was sort of a public figure and I didn’t like him. From the very few clips of the Frenemies podcast I saw (and very quickly scrolled away from) with Klein and Trisha Paytas, all I gathered was that Klein is sort of a dick. Now that I know he’s allegedly been cheating on his wife for 6 months...I know that my initial assessment was too tame.

Klein is one of many male podcasters who are quite simply put: bad dudes. Aside from some of the obvious offenders like Andrew Tate, Myron Gaines, and Joe Rogan—who all spew toxic, brainless misogynistic trash on their podcasts—even the “positive” faces of male podcasting (Jay Shetty and Andrew Huberman) have faced revelations that complicate their squeaky-clean personas.

I think we need to stop treating the male podcaster archetype as inherently harmless—or, worse, as evidence that a man is particularly emotionally intelligent. With very few exceptions, these men suck—and it’s time to stop dating them.

“A podcast microphone attracts a very distinct psychological profile,” sex and relationship expert Gemma Nice tells Cosmopolitan. “Men who confuse monologue with communication, and external audience validation with genuine self-worth.”

This isn't to say every man with a microphone is secretly a pathological liar, misogynist, or cheater (or all three like Klein, sorry). Obviously, there are plenty of perfectly normal men who happen to record themselves talking for a living. Well, I haven’t seen them, but I’m sure they’re out there. Because what does it actually mean when a man decides he needs an audience for his opinions?

“Hosting a podcast gives an individual an uninterrupted platform where they talk for hours, control the narrative, and are rewarded with applause, downloads, and social media validation without anyone answering back in real time,” Nice says.

But, she explains that healthy romantic relationships require the exact opposite skill set. A healthy relationship consists of active listening, emotional humility, and sitting comfortably in silence. When these men step away from the studio and into their private relationships, they often don't know how to relate to a partner without trying to win the conversation or lecture them.

“When a partner pushes back or expresses hurt, a man addicted to the microphone immediately becomes defensive because his ego cannot handle being challenged without an audience on his side,” Nice says.

When a man becomes a podcaster, it’s typically because he has discovered that there is an enormous market for men telling other men how to live—and that market is not exactly known for producing well-adjusted feminists.

Research into the manosphere has found that podcasts can serve as a particularly effective vehicle for spreading misogynistic ideas because they package them inside more palatable formats like self-improvement, financial advice, mental-health discussions and conversations about masculinity. One analysis of popular “manfluencer” podcasts found that hypermasculine and male-supremacist ideas were frequently disguised as advice about self-reliance, success, and personal empowerment.

And the guy doesn't necessarily have to come out and say women are inferior. He can just spend three hours explaining why modern women are “too demanding,” why men “aren't allowed to be men anymore,” why women should be more feminine, why dating apps are ruining society, why men need to “reclaim” their masculinity, and why the problem with your relationship is probably that a woman hasn’t learned how to communicate with a man properly. He can also spend multiple episodes making gross comments about their cohost’s fertility—like Klein did to Paytas.

The men whose entire public persona is built around being obnoxious, inflammatory, condescending, cruel, misogynistic, chronically online, or just generally kind of a dick is not going to make a good partner. Let’s stop dating and marrying men whose favorite hobby is publicly humiliating other people—doing so only asks you to convince yourself that, with you, he'll suddenly become tender.

Now, I’m not blaming Klein’s wife, Hila at all. But as potential dating prospects, we can learn from this situation. Some people out there see a man behaving terribly in public and immediately start imagining the private version of him they could unlock. They tell themselves, “He's just playing it up for the podcast,” “he's actually really sweet,” “he’d treat me differently,” or “he has a soft side.”

We need to realize that the public persona is not a performance but an exaggerated version of who he actually is.

“In a genuine partnership, moving forward and staying healthy requires being open, honest, raw and real with each other and having a genuine accountability without being the victim,” Nice explains. “But public podcasters often build brands around being infallible authorities.”

So when they’re held accountable in real life, they don’t know how to process it. Nice explains that they instead become passive aggressive, dismiss hard conversations, or even use their platform to publicly air out their grievances.

There are, of course, a few exceptions. Hearon (mentioned earlier) and podcast duo Joe Santagato and Frank Alvarez come to mind. What makes these men with mics not only tolerable but actually enjoyable is that they don't seem particularly interested in convincing you that they have unlocked the secret to being a man. Hearon gives pop-culture commentary similar to Klein, but he isn't constantly positioning himself as the final authority on human behavior. Santagato and Alvarez are, for the most part, just goofy. They aren't selling you a masculinity framework.

This is the only reason a man should have a podcast, in my mind. I don't need you to teach me how to become a “better woman” or explain “female nature.” I don't need a six-part series on why modern dating is broken. I don't need you to tell 400,000 strangers about your relationship philosophy without any sort of credentials—or when you’re cheating on your wife. I just need you to be normal! Put the microphone down. Go outside. Have a conversation with one woman who isn't your producer.

And for people who are in the dating pool right now: whether you’re on the apps or meeting someone in real life, if you see that a potential male partner has a podcast, RUN.

#3581. A Painting Owned by Dutch Jewish Collector Once Hung in Hitler’s Home, New Research Finds

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A Nazi-looted painting that once belonged to a Dutch Jewish family, and at one point hung in Adolf Hitler’s home, has been traced in new research, the Dutch newspaper Het Parool reports.

The work in question is German painter Carl Gottlieb Peschel’s Joseph Sold by His Brothers (1831), which shows the biblical scene of Joseph being sold into slavery in Egypt.

The painting belonged to Bob Nijkerk, whose family had made its fortune in the iron and scrap-metal business in the 19th century. Nijkerk, who died in 1987, used that wealth to build a collection of bibliophile editions, modern Dutch typography, and paintings. He received the Peschel as a gift from his brother-in-law in 1941. When the Nazis occupied Amsterdam in 1940, he was a managing director of the family company, which the Nazis seized in 1941. Related Articles California Attorney General Argues for Return of Nazi-Looted Painting Held by Madrid Museum Nazi-Looted Monet Painting Held by FBI Expected to Be Returned to Owner's Descendants

Starting in 1942, the Nazis deported more than 100,000 Dutch Jews to killing centers. Nijkerk, his wife, and their three children were spared at first. Willem Sandberg, a curator at Amsterdam’s Stedelijk Museum, where Nijkerk had loaned and donated artworks, intervened on their behalf and got them placed on an exception list of about 700 Jews. In 1940 the two men visited the museum’s art bunker together, and the collector likely stored important works there.

The family was first taken to De Schaffelaar Castle, about 45 minutes outside Amsterdam. They were later deported to the Westerbork camp in the Netherlands and then to Theresienstadt in what is now the Czech Republic. “Ultimately, all five survived the war because they were brought to Switzerland by train in 1945 along with a select group of camp prisoners from Theresienstadt,” Het Parool reports.Nijkerk’s grandson Willem Nijkerk knew that his family had included bibliophiles but didn’t know how large his grandfather’s collection was. He learned of the family’s link to the painting from Diederik Oostdijk, a professor of English and American literature at the Vrije Universiteit in Amsterdam.

“I mainly knew about the period during and after the war. Through Oostdijk’s research, I was given a glimpse into their prewar life. They lived in relatively grand luxury,” Willem told Het Parool.

Oostdijk tells the Nijkerk family’s story in his new book, Overlevingskunst (The Art of Survival). According to its description, the book follows “three Jewish families [who] miraculously escaped the Holocaust thanks to their art collections.”

Oostdijk’s research shows that the Peschel spent some time in Hitler’s Berlin home. It was then moved to Hitler’s storage facility in Munich, where prized works were held for a planned Führermuseum.

It is still unclear whether Bob Nijkerk sold the painting in late 1941 or early 1942, or whether it was confiscated later, when German forces or their Dutch collaborators raided Jewish homes. After the war, Nijkerk recovered many of his books but “never uttered a word about Peschel’s painting,” Willem said. “I don’t think he had much of a connection to the painting himself. The painting does not evoke much emotion in me either.”

Nijkerk’s seven descendants have not filed a formal restitution claim for the Peschel. They also have not claimed three other paintings that Oostdijk has linked to the Nijkerk collection: two 1633 portraits by Caspar Netscher and a 1673 painting by Gerbrand van den Eeckhout. Those three works are at the Rijksmuseum in Amsterdam. Joseph Sold by His Brothers is now in the Martin von Wagner Museum in Würzburg, Germany. Willem said that if the paintings are ever restituted, the family would likely sell them.

“It is a bizarre thought that a painting belonging to my grandparents was in Hitler’s residence, one of the most horrific human beings who ever lived, and to think that he found this work so beautiful,” Willem told Het Parool. “I don’t necessarily want Peschel’s painting on my wall at home. I also find it an unpleasant thought that it lay in Hitler’s home.”

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Passengers waiting to board a flight were offered a choice by their captain: wait for the plane to be cleaned after a dog peed on the previous flight, or get home on time with the cabin smelling like urine. According to the woman who shared video, the pilot put it to a show of hands. This looks like Delta at New York LaGuardia to me, though readers please correct me. #573. Young people trust TikTok doctors without checking their credentials

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Young TikTok users often trusted doctor-influencers more than their own physicians, and none of 26 interviewees had checked a license.

Raised on social media, young people are usually quick to catch an influencer selling something. Put that influencer in scrubs, though, and some followers stop asking questions.

In long interviews, young adults who follow doctors on TikTok often described them as more caring and easier to reach than their own physicians.

Not one had checked whether those doctors were licensed.

That trust, the researchers wrote, may give doctors on social media more pull over their followers’ health habits than an ordinary office visit has.

Suzanne Makarem, an associate professor of marketing at the VCU School of Business, led the work.

Her co-authors were Brittany Beck of Appalachian State University and Jie Fowler of Valdosta State University.

It rests on 330 TikTok videos and interviews with 26 young followers, so it shows how people think about these doctors. It doesn’t measure what they do.

“Young consumers are usually very literate when it comes to social media influencers and commercial intent. But when you have a physician on TikTok, that becomes something different,” Makarem said.

The team started with the doctors. They picked 11 U.S. physicians from industry lists of top doctors to follow on TikTok and confirmed every license through the Federation of State Medical Boards.

All were big accounts, averaging about 875,000 followers apiece.

Researchers collected every post those doctors made during one month in 2022.

Two years later, they went back to update the numbers. Less than half of the posts were about health. The rest covered life as a doctor, home life, and opinions.

Most were filmed at home, in a car, or outdoors, yet 58% of all posts showed the doctor in scrubs or a lab coat. Even in the personal posts, almost half still had the medical outfit.

“They’re not singing and dancing like many others, but they do share personal information,” Makarem said.

“And they’re likely to wear medical attire, even if they’re not talking about medicine. Like, they’re making a peanut butter and jelly sandwich in their kitchen, but they have a lab coat on.”

On average, posts showing the doctor in medical clothes got about 1,150 comments, against about 170 for casual clothes.

Videos shot in a hospital or clinic averaged about 62,000 likes, more than twice the figure for videos shot at home.

Dancing, acting, and trending TikTok music didn’t raise likes, comments, or views. The posts that drew more engagement were the ones where the doctor looked like a doctor.

TikTok’s design makes that harder to read. The app decides which videos people see, and a popular video gets shown to more people.

Likes and comments also miss a big part of the audience. Most of the followers said they didn’t comment or send messages to the doctors.

They saved videos to watch again, or sent them to friends and family, and none of that shows up in a public count.

For the second half of the project, the team held interviews in 2023. Each of the 26 conversations lasted 45 minutes to an hour.

Everyone interviewed used social media at least weekly and had followed at least one doctor for a year or more.

They were recruited through business courses at one Southeastern university and through referrals from other participants.

Asked how they knew their favorite TikTok doctor was real, most pointed to a bio, a name badge, scrubs, or a stethoscope. All 26 said they had never looked up the license.

“I asked, ‘Have you checked whether these physicians are actual physicians?'” Makarem said.

“And the response was, ‘No, but I’m sure. They’re wearing a stethoscope; there’s a diploma hanging on the wall; they have scrubs on. I don’t think they’d be lying about this.'”

For these followers, the outfit seemed to be enough.

Most of the followers didn’t see product mentions as ads. They read them as medical advice. When a doctor sold a product of their own, several took it as proof the doctor believed in it.

Even followers who noticed the money had an answer for it.

“If they do recognize the commercial intent, they say, ‘Well, they have to. If they could do it for free, they would,'” Makarem said.

Their own doctors were judged more harshly. Several described them as rushed and more interested in repeat visits than in preventing illness.

“Doctors primarily just want to see you again. But TikTok doctors … they have no interest in seeing you,” one follower said.

The followers said they took the advice seriously, from drinking more water to starting vitamins.

Makarem’s team named the pattern the mere social media presence effect. Simply being on the platform seemed to make these doctors look kinder, and harder to doubt.

“What a physician-influencer recommends may carry more weight than what their own doctor tells them,” Makarem said. “That should give us pause.”

This was an exploratory study, built to find patterns rather than prove causes. The followers’ reports of buying products and following advice are their own accounts, and nobody tracked what they actually did.

The doctors were all large accounts on a single platform, and the authors warned against treating 26 interviewees as stand-ins for a whole generation.

Researchers also didn’t check whether the doctors’ advice was accurate, so they can’t say how much health misinformation reached these followers.

For people who trust a doctor without checking, that’s the question that matters most.

Two fixes came from the authors. Platforms could offer verified badges for board-certified doctors, and young viewers could learn to look up credentials and spot paid promotions.

Next, they wrote, come surveys and experiments that test whether a doctor’s presence on social media changes what followers do.

The full study was published in the journal Journal of Consumer Affairs.

#951. The Free K-Beauty Way to Stop Hair Loss

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It’s the great generational debate: Side part or middle part? Where we part our hair dictates the facial features we want to play up and sets the tone for our overall vibe.

But what if this defining hair feature that we spend a lot of time contemplating comes with unexpected consequences? According to Korean hair clinic Lee Moon Won, sticking to one hair part forever might be the cause of one of our most pressing hair concerns: unwanted shedding.

There are a few reasons why this might be. Wonuk Hwang, MD, clinic director at the Cheongdam Lebelle Clinic in Seoul, explains that the exposed skin where we part our hair has less protection from environmental stressors such as the sun’s harmful UV rays, and that should always be looked at seriously. Hwang points to studies that show UV radiation can cause cellular damage to the hair follicles and scalp tissue, which could increase hair shedding after severe scalp sunburn. Even though those studies don’t mention hair parts specifically, he says that exposed scalp will always need sun protection.

“It is definitely important to be mindful of cumulative sun exposure on the scalp where we part our hair,” says Helen He, MD, director of lasers and cosmetic surgery and codirector of Mount Sinai-Clinique Healthy Skin Dermatology Center. “Since that area of skin is more exposed to the sun without innate protection from hair, it may be more susceptible to developing skin cancers and precancerous lesions. While there is no current evidence that chronic UV exposure directly leads to increased hair shedding, there is a clear association with skin cancer risk, oxidative stress, and inflammation.”

Treating inflammation is a key part of treating hair loss in K-beauty. As He explains, inflammation around your hair follicles can disrupt the normal hair cycle and lead to increased hair loss and shedding. Several hair conditions, she says, such as seborrheic dermatitis and scalp psoriasis, showcase this; when they increase inflammation on the scalp, it will eventually exacerbate thinning hair. Autoimmune diseases such as alopecia areata, and cicatricial alopecia are considered chronic inflammation disorders that also cause hair loss.

“The thinking is that an inflamed scalp makes follicles more vulnerable,” adds Michelle Lee, chief marketing officer of K-Beauty World and former editor in chief of Allure. “It’s the same logic K-beauty applies to the skin, where you calm the barrier first and everything else works better.”

There’s also some mechanical stress with your regular hair part. He says that when hair is repeatedly parted, brushed, and styled in one direction, it may inadvertently put tension on your strands. “Over time, this repeated friction can accumulate and lead to hair breakage,” she explains. “While excessive pulling or tension can damage hair follicles and potentially lead to traction alopecia.”

He wouldn’t call this method essential, but she says it may be helpful. “It will prevent any one area of the scalp from excessive UV exposure,” she explains. “It would also prevent excessive tension to the same areas of hair.”

“It’s something so easy and immediately actionable, but it’s more about the mindset rather than thinking that changing your part will completely solve your hair-loss issues,” adds Lee. “It’s more about being thoughtful about how we’re treating the scalp in general and making sure that we care for it and keep it protected and nourished.”

Should you want to give this method a try, she says the Lee Moon Won Clinic, which she visited last year, suggests changing your part every six months and moving it at least four centimeters away from old parts so that the newly covered area isn’t the same spot that has been exposed and it ensures that the same strip of the scalp won’t be subjected to constant sun and dust, both of which can lead to inflammation.

Hwang says that there’s no single K-beauty approach to hair loss. Instead, it’s a holistic approach that assesses the scalp and identifies the causes of hair loss. “It is focused on optimizing scalp health to create an environment that supports healthy hair growth,” He agrees. Other than reducing inflammation, she says K-beauty prioritizes fortifying the scalp barrier and microbiome, gentle exfoliation to decrease buildup, and improving microcirculation.

For everyday care, she recommends regular washing to prevent buildup and excess oil. You can turn to known K-beauty soothing ingredients such as cica, centella asiatica, and tea-tree and green-tea extracts, which she says are staples in K-beauty skin care and scalp products for optimal scalp health. Hwang recommends avoiding styles that repeatedly pull on your hair and adding scalp sun protection into your routine with a hat or a water-resistant sunscreen with SPF 30 (or higher) broad-spectrum protection.

Lee says that the hair clinic recommended that she tweak certain lifestyle habits, such as changing her pillowcase every two to three days and taking a closer look at her diet. The clinic also gave her tips on how to better dry her hair and sleep to keep hair healthy. “What struck me is that hair loss was treated as a scalp and lifestyle problem,” she says. “At the end of my analysis and treatment, they weren’t trying to treat my issues with medications. Rather, they have their own concoction of natural ingredients as well as a whole list of lifestyle changes.”

And when in doubt, see a doctor for any persistent or unexplained shedding. Addressing any underlying causes, whether it be inflammation, hormones, stress, or an autoimmune condition, will always be the most important step when it comes to hair loss. Whether you have a side part or not.

#3591. Even German Bidders Can Now Pitch In to Support US Public Television, with the Country’s First Auction of a Bob Ross Painting

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For the first time, a Bob Ross painting will come to auction in Germany.

The painting comes to the block on December 5 at auctioneer Ketterer Kunst in Munich, from the collection of American Public Television (APT). Proceeds will benefit the organization as part of a charitable initiative dreamed up by Joan Kowalski, president of Bob Ross, Inc., to support public television stations in the US following cuts of $9 billion to previously approved government funding by a Republican-dominated Congress, at the behest of Donald Trump’s presidential administration. Related Articles Public Television is $1.3 M. Richer, Thanks to a Wildly Successful Bob Ross Auction  More Bob Ross Paintings Go to Auction to Raise Money for Public Broadcasting, After Successful Sales So Far

Mountain Splendor (1986) bears an estimate of €40,000–€60,000 ($46,800–$70,200). Ross painted it live on television during season seven, episode eight in of his beloved show The Joy of Painting. Ross typically created three versions of his compositions, the auctioneer points out: one in preparation, one on live television, and one for his instructional books, with the live-painted ones the most coveted. The painting has been in storage for decades and emerges in “exceptionally well-preserved condition,” says Ketterer Kunst in press materials.

Mountain Splendor will be on show at the house’s showrooms in Frankfurt, Cologne, Hamburg, Berlin, and Munich between November 8 and December 4.

“Bob Ross has inspired millions of people to take up painting and has long since been an icon of pop culture,” says Robert Ketterer, owner and auctioneer. “With Mountain Splendor, we’re auctioning off a piece of television history. What makes it so special is the fact that people can still watch the artist create this painting today. The fact that we’re able to offer an original for the first time in Germany—and at the same time support public television in the U.S.—makes this auction particularly special to us.”

Ross’s paintings have sold for as much as $1 million at benefit auctions, when late-night comedian John Oliver last year sold Cabin at Sunset, painted for a 1986 episode.

As far as public auction houses listed on Artnet’s price database, Ross’s auction high stands at $787,900, achieved with the 1990 painting Change of Seasons at Bonhams Skinner in January against a $60,000 high estimate. The auctioneer raised some $1.3 million for APT that night with the sale of three Ross paintings, with the record painting going for more than thirteen times its high estimate. Last November, Bonhams brought three of a planned 30 of his canvases to auction; they sold for a total of $600,000, all of which went to APT.

One private seller is trying to smash even the $1 million high, with Minnesota art gallery Modern Artifact asking $9.85 million for A Walk in the Woods, painted on the show’s very first episode.

“Bob Ross is one of America’s most beloved cultural exports, and audiences across the globe love his work as much as viewers here in the States do,” said Jim Dunford, CEO and president of APT, in press materials. “These auctions turn that worldwide affection for Bob into real support for local public television stations, and we are deeply grateful to Bob Ross Inc. for making it possible.”

Indeed, Ross is a hit in Germany; his show debuted on German television in 2001 and continues to air regularly. Samsung TV Plus Germany also features the Bob Ross Channel auf Deutsch, which launched this year.

The American public, too, can’t get enough of Ross, whose show aired some 403 episodes and ran from 1983 to 1994. Comic actor Owen Wilson played a fictionalized version of him in the 2023 feature Paint (which Shanti Escalante-de Mattei, writing for ARTnews, called “a flaw-filled film”). His estate has been at the center of a legal battle, depicted in a 2021 Netflix documentary that the estate called “inaccurate and heavily slanted.”

#3610. Christie’s First Abu Dhabi Art Auction to Be Accompanied by Exhibition of $60 M.–$80 M. Kandinsky

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The competition in the art world for business in the Gulf is heating up. Next week Christie’s is set to host its first fine art auction in Abu Dhabi, featuring some 70 lots of modern and contemporary Middle Eastern art.

The sale, dubbed “MAWASIM: 20 Years of Modern and Contemporary Middle Eastern Art,” features works by Turkish abstractionist Fahrelnissa Zeid, one of the first women to attend art school in Turkey; Iraqi painter Faisel Laibi Sahi; Egyptian modernist painter Mahmoud Saïd; and Palestinian American artist Samia Halaby, among others. Estimates for works in the sale range from $8,000–$12,000 to $500,000–$700,000. (Mawasim means “seasons” in Arabic and is meant to reference the 20 years since Christie’s held its first sale in the United Arab Emirates, in Dubai, in 2006.) Related Articles Christie's Will Offer $8 M. in Works from Mickey Cartin's Collection, from Netherlandish Painting to Contemporary Art to Rare Books Sotheby's Dinosaur 'Jodi' Sells for $47.3 M., More Than Five Times Its High Estimate

In an interview with the Khaleej Times, the house’s chairman for the Middle East and Africa, Ridha Moumni, called the sale “probably the most important event of modern and contemporary Eastern art for many years.”

The sale is something of a starting pistol for what is rapidly becoming the November arts season for the UAE: several weeks later, from November 19–22, Frieze will host the first edition of its Abu Dhabi fair at Manarat Al Saadiyat, and just a few weeks after that, on December 11, the Guggenheim Abu Dhabi will finally open its doors. And on December 10, Sotheby’s will culminate the second edition of its “Collectors’ Week” in the emirate with its inaugural fine art sale, titled “Breaking Ground,” to be hosted at the new Saadiyat Grove Experience Center on Saadiyat Island, the city’s cultural hub. (Sotheby’s sale, notably, features works with much higher top estimates and headline names like Andy Warhol and Claude Monet.)

There will be some major works featured by Christie’s in Abu Dhabi, though they won’t be for sale. Rather, the house has said it will be showing, starting Wednesday, three major works from its upcoming marquee New York sales: Wassily Kandinsky’s Betonte Ecken (1923), which carries a $60 million to $80 million estimate; Mark Rothko’s Untitled (1968), which carries a $10 million to $15 million estimate; and Pablo Picasso’s Tête de femme (1939), which carries a $12 million to $18 million estimate. All three works will be on view at Abu Dhabi Edition hotel on Al Bateen Marina.

One imagines that Christie’s is hoping those lots catch the eye of some deep-pocketed Gulf collectors ahead of the sales. After all, Moumni touted to the Times, “The UAE is the country where we have the biggest number of bidders in our auction. In terms of concentration, the UAE is the land of collectors of modern and contemporary art.”

#986. The Easiest Way to Make Your Eyes Pop? These Expert-Loved Highlighters

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From sparkling sticks to velvety mattes, the experts have spoken.

When you see the word “highlight,” you may get flashbacks to the reflective streaks that plagued the cheeks of beauty gurus throughout the 2010s. Or maybe a compact of Becca’s Champagne Pop pops into your mind. Chills. However, a decade later, highlighter has evolved. The once-iridescent makeup step is now all about creating a glass-like glow through different forms: sheer liquids, dewy balms, and subtle shimmers. And while cheekbones have long stolen the spotlight, there’s another area that deserves some shine: the eyes. With a quick swipe of eye highlighter, you can instantly make your eyes look brighter, bigger, and more awake.

“Highlighting is really about bringing light to certain areas of the eye to create brightness and dimension,” explains celebrity makeup artist Judi Gabbay. “It can make the eyes look bigger, more awake, and even subtly change their shape.” Highlighting doesn’t necessarily mean shimmer, either. “I use matte shadows all the time to bring certain areas forward—sometimes that’s actually more flattering.” There’s a variety of ways to apply eye highlighter, but the inner corner is a classic for a reason. “It’s such a small detail that can completely change the feel of the makeup,” Gabbay says. “You can elevate a look in seconds with a little crushed sparkle, open up and visually separate the eyes with a matte shadow, or bring a sexy, almost sultry feel with a subtle shimmer.”

From shimmer sticks to all-over powders and even matte shadows, ELLE editors have you covered with the five best eye highlighters on the market. We’ve swatched and tested a ton ourselves, read through countless customer reviews, and polled Gabbay on her go-tos. Find our winners below.

When it comes to eye highlight, there’s no easier application than with a shadow stick. This pick from Laura Mercier is a favorite amongst experts and editors for its quick application, smooth texture, and intense pigment.

Expert review: “These are my go-to when I need something quick and easy. I love that I can throw one in my bag and apply it without a brush. A quick swipe in the inner corner, tap it out with my finger, and I’m good to go.”—Judi Gabbay

Nothing pulls a glam together quite like a highlighter, especially when you use the same one across the entire look. “This is one I always come back to,” says Gabbay. “I love a product that does double duty, and I use this on the eyes just as much as the face. A little in the inner corner and on the cheekbones ties everything together beautifully.”

Customer review: “I’ve been using this nonstop for weeks, and I’m obsessed! It gives the skin the most beautiful, natural glass-skin glow without any chunky glitter. Just a gorgeous, fresh, wet-look radiance. I love it!”

Whether you want a matte or sparkly eye highlight, you can’t go grow with one of Patrick Ta Beauty’s eyeshadow palettes. “They’re so buttery and velvety, and they blend effortlessly,” Gabbay says. “I use both the matte and shimmer shades to brighten the inner corners.”

Customer review: “I LOVE this palette! The mattes are creamy and so bendable. The shimmers are much sheerer when dry and they appear foiled when applied wet. This palette has everything and is so good to travel with.”

Highlighting doesn’t always mean shimmer. “I actually reach for matte shadows quite often, especially when I’m working on mature women or really young girls,” says Gabbay. A soft cream or ivory shade can bring the eyes forward and brighten everything up without emphasizing texture or adding unnecessary sparkle.

Editor review: “In my opinion, no one does matte eyeshadows quite like Makeup by Mario. I gravitate towards this palette every time I’m in the mood to create a smoky eye. Whether placed in the center of the lid or on the inner corner (or both!), the cream shades in the palette provide the perfect pop of dimension.”—Emma Aerin Becker, associate beauty commerce writer

Sensitive skin? No problem. Tower 28’s GoGo Cooling Eyeshadow Stick is not only packed with metallic pigment, but also infused with skin-soothing ingredients like Irish moss.

Editor review: “I have both sensitive skin and eczema-prone eyelids, so I couldn’t wait to get my hands on these when they launched last year. The water-based formula feels incredibly soothing and cooling on the skin. With one swipe, I’m instantly met with an intense metallic pigment. I love using shade Whisky all over the lid and placing Champagne on the inner corner and brow bone to highlight.”—Emma Aerin Becker

How you apply eye highlighter depends on your chosen product’s formula. With a cream or a stick, Gabbay suggests lightly tapping it into the skin with your finger. For powders, “I like to use a small, rounded brush to concentrate the product exactly where I want it, then go in with a dome-shaped blending brush to soften any harsh edges,” she says. “I also love layering a little shimmer over a matte base when I want more dimension.” Sometimes the contrast between textures is what really makes the look.

Generally speaking, champagnes and soft gold work beautifully on brown eyes, rose gold and peach make blue eyes pop, and pink champagne or mauve tones pair great with green eyes. Hazel eyes also work well with warm golds and bronzes. “But I don’t really believe in strict makeup rules,” Gabbay notes. “I’ve been doing this long enough to know that sometimes the shade you least expect ends up looking the best.” In other words, have fun playing around with different tones, because that’s the true beauty of makeup.

Judi Gabbay is a celebrity makeup artist based in New York City.

#1133. The 8 Best Slip-On Walking Shoes, According to Our Editors

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Looking to get out the door quicker? Don’t let laces slip you up.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SLIDING INTO A pair of slip-on walking shoes might be the easiest thing you’ll do all day. This footwear was built for speed and convenience, making it an accessory that deserves valuable real estate by your door.

Slip-on walking shoes have a rocky history—older versions had a looser fit, raising the risk of instability and friction, especially with higher mileage. But newer designs use no-tie laces, sock liners, pull tabs, and collapsible heels to create a custom fit that rivals what you’ll get with traditional laces.

These shoes are an obvious choice if you have dexterity issues, but they're also just convenient for everyone looking for an easy pair of walking shoes. Slip-on walking shoes also offer more structure than other laceless shoe designs, which has earned them a fanbase with podiatrists. “They are typically a much better option than flip-flops or slides because they provide more stability and keep the foot properly secured,” says Melissa Lockwood, DPM, a podiatrist at Heartland Foot and Ankle Associates in Bloomington, Illinois.

Nicole Graf, DPM, founder of Lexington Podiatry and associate professor at the University of Kentucky, also calls slip-on walking shoes a “huge step up” from flip-flops and slides.

Slip-on walking shoes aren’t for everyone. The fit can still throw some people off, and some simply prefer the tighter feel that laces deliver. But if you want travel-friendly shoes you can toss on in a second, these are a solid choice. As people who value quality, supportive footwear, Men’s Health editors have put in plenty of miles in slip-on walking shoes. The below check all the boxes.

The On Cloud 6 is an excellent example of how good a slip-on shoe can be. You can get in and out of this sneaker with no issue thanks to its wide opening, but the shoe’s elastic “speed” laces and sockliner create a snug fit once you’re in. (Those elastic laces can be adjusted to find your perfect fit.)

The sneaker uses Zero-Gravity CloudTec, which is On’s signature lightweight foam with hollow pods below for a cushioned, yet stable, feel underfoot. (Just be aware that those pods are prone to trapping small pebbles and debris.) A built-in flexible plate, called a Speedboard, helps push you forward with each step. This shoe also comes in a waterproof option, if you’re into rainy strolls. With a wide range of color options and an unobnoxious design, you can pair these with just about anything.

It’s tough to find a quality shoe under $100 right now, let alone one from a big brand. But the Adidas Cloudfoam Cuxxion Sock Shoes have some colorways under $50, making them an ideal choice for budget shoppers who don’t want to mess with laces.

This shoe uses Adidas’ signature Cloudfoam technology at the midsole for a plush, cushioned feel. Its Adiwear outsole is durable and grippy enough for most needs, too. But the real standout in this shoe is its elastic sock upper. It molds to your foot to create a surprisingly snug feel, even for narrow feet. The elastic sock collar supports that secure feel, although it may be too tight for people with wider ankles. The engineered knit upper is highly breathable, lowering the odds you’ll end your walk with sweaty feet. We appreciate the oversized pull tabs in front and back, making the process of getting these on and off seamless.

Feet come in all sizes, making it tough to find a slip-on shoe that works for everyone—and putting adjustable features at a premium. The Nike Pegasus 42 EasyOn has a specialized EasyOn lacing and bungee system up top to help you get the level of tightness you want, before locking it into place. (The lock and bungee combination takes some practice to get right at first.)

The shoe has a collapsible heel that springs back into place once you’re in, making the process of getting these on seamless. It’s also crafted with the same ReactX foam and Air Zoom unit as the standard—and popular—Nike Pegasus 42 running shoe, delivering an energetic feel under your feet. Convenient pull tabs at the heel sit flush against the shoe when they’re not in use, adding to the shoe’s overall sleekness.

Read More: Best Walking Shoes for Men Over 50

Stability shoes are designed to guide your foot with each step and prevent too much inward rolling as you go. But choosing a stability shoe usually means sacrificing a high level of cushioning, since a shoe that feels too plush may not be overly supportive. Hoka’s Skyward Laceless manages to create a stable, locked-in feel while still delivering plenty of the brand’s signature cushioning underfoot.

The Skyward Laceless has a stretchy upper that gently hugs your foot, creating a sock-like feel. The ankle and instep are equally snug, which can be a good or bad thing, depending on your foot size and width. This shoe has plenty of foam below for a slightly bouncy feel, but it still corrects your stride as you go. The integrated tongue pull tab is a nice touch, making for easier entry.

The Under Armour SlipSpeed Mega is packed with features you won’t find in most slip-on walking shoes, creating a versatile, unique sneaker. For starters, this shoe converts from a sneaker design to a recovery slipper with the help of a collapsible heel. (Under Armour refers to this as going from “speed mode” to “slip mode.”)

The shoe skips elastic laces or a sockliner upper in favor of Under Armour’s specialized BOA fit system, a 12-point dial that you can turn to adjust the width across the midfoot. The wide base creates a stable feel, while lots of foam cushioning underfoot delivers a plushness you’ll appreciate. A heads-up: This shoe is slightly heavier than others at 11.7 ounces. That’s a difference you may start to feel on long walks.

After a grueling workout, it feels borderline pampering to slide into the right recovery shoe. The Kane Footwear Active Recovery Shoe is armed with features that should make it a fixture in your gym bag.

This slip-on has strong arch support and a slight rocker shape to help ease pressure on the bottom of your feet as you walk. Internal nodes in the footbed help to promote blood flow and circulation down below, while the shoe’s surprisingly secure design helps prevent slipping. The Active Recovery Shoe is water-resistant with an airy design that allows you to take this in and out of the gym shower without ending up with sopping feet. Heel tabs in back make it simple to get in and out.

Merrell is known for its rugged, outdoor footwear and hiking shoes. While the Merrell Jungle Moc isn’t exactly a hiking shoe, it’s a solid option when you want good traction, whether you’re navigating rocky ground or a slippery surface. The Jungle Moc has been a bestseller for years, with its simple slip-on design and high level of cushioning.

Dual side stretch panels and an exaggerated heel tab make it simple to get in and out of these shoes. The suede upper is breathable, but water-resistant to keep you dry in nearly all conditions. The Jungle Moc has a cushioned EVA midsole for stability and comfort, too. While these shoes are easy to get on, the curved heel makes it harder to slide out—just something to be aware of.

Clogs admittedly aren’t the right style for everyone—some offer less support than sneakers and may slip around more than a standard shoe. But the sleek Crocs Dylan Clog is a solid option for people who want a clog style without some of the common issues that come with it.

The Dylan Clog has a deep heel cup to help lock your foot in, lowering the risk it’ll move around as you step. It’s also made from one piece of Croslite (Crocs’ signature material) to prevent wear and tear over time. This shoe looks like leather thanks to its subtle pebbled texture, but it’s water-resistant and dries quickly when wet. The only major downside is that it’s not very breathable in front. If you’re prone to sweaty toes, this may not be the shoe for you.

Not all shoes are created equal, but the best slip-on walking shoes have all of the supportive features you’ll find in standard lace-up walking shoes. “You mostly get what you pay for in these,” says Richard H. Graves, DPM, a podiatrist at Sol Foot & Ankle Centers in Long Beach, California. He suggests taking a pass at dirt-cheap slip-on walking shoes. “The cheaper ones are not going to provide enough support in the long run, even if they might feel good at first,” Graves says. “There should be enough structure to support your arch and the midsole should be firm and not too flimsy.”

Many high-quality slip-on walking shoes provide similar comfort to regular sneakers. Look for elements like a breathable upper, pull tabs to make them easy to get on and off, and a roomy toe box. Structured heel panels and extra padding in this area help to keep your foot secure, even without standard laces.

Traction for slip-on walking shoes will vary depending on the type of shoe you choose. Most will offer decent traction—meaning, enough so you don’t feel like you’re slipping on wet or damp pavement. But a more rugged shoe like the Merrell Jungle Moc has a grippier sole to help provide extra traction over rockier ground.

Yes, slip-on walking shoes can provide arch support. If this is an important quality for you, look for features like contoured footbeds and a stable midsole. You’ll also want a shoe that fits well and stays put once it’s on to ensure your arch support is consistent. “Make sure that you're not getting them too loose just to make it easier to slide on and off—then you'll be sacrificing support,” Graves says.

To find the best men’s slip-on walking shoes, MH editors tested the most recent and popular pairs on the market, evaluating each for support, comfort, and traction. Because these shoes are designed for walking, not just comfort, durability was also a key factor. To round things out, we consulted three podiatrists for their input on how to evaluate a slip-on walking shoe, along with the value of this style. Based on their advice and our experience, we chose these slip-on walking shoes.

She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#547. A New Chemistry Breakthrough Could Change How Scientists Build Quantum Computers

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By modeling diamond defects as molecules, scientists can predict their behavior faster and identify promising building blocks for quantum computers and sensors.

Most materials being considered for quantum technology can hold a stable quantum state only when chilled to extreme temperatures. Diamond-based materials can work at room temperature, but their usefulness depends on defects in the crystal that have been difficult and computationally demanding to simulate.

At the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE) Princeton Plasma Physics Laboratory (PPPL), staff research scientist Mark Martirez has developed a far faster way to calculate how those defects respond to light and microwave pulses. The approach has identified promising boron- and oxygen-based defects that could serve as qubits, the basic information-processing units of quantum computers and sensors. The research was recently published in ACS Central Science and has already attracted attention from other experts in the field.

Pure diamond consists entirely of carbon atoms, but storing and processing quantum information requires a disruption in that structure. A carbon atom might be missing from its usual position, an atom of another element might take its place, or both changes might occur together. The cluster of atoms closest to the defect forms the qubit.

Scientists use laser light and microwave pulses to read information stored in these qubits. Most quantum diamond research has focused on the nitrogen-vacancy defect, in which a nitrogen atom replaces a carbon atom and a nearby position normally occupied by another carbon atom is empty. When illuminated by a laser, this qubit emits red light if it is in a particular quantum state. Calculating how other defects respond to light and microwaves helps researchers assess whether they, too, could be useful.

Martirez simplifies those calculations by computationally “carving out” a cluster of just 16 to 30 atoms around a defect and considering it in isolation. This makes highly accurate tools that chemists have used for decades to study simple compounds available for studying defects inside a solid.

“Because we’re treating the qubits as molecules, the cost of calculating these defects is equivalent to the cost of just modeling a simple molecule,” Martirez said. “This method really opened the floodgates” in terms of how qubits can be assessed.

The calculations identified oxygen-based defects that shine blue and boron-based defects that emit ultraviolet light. These defects, known as color centers, could potentially serve different roles within a single quantum device.

“One could imagine, then, combining different qubits of different responses, so you can basically probe one qubit but not the other,” Martirez said.

Understanding the chemistry could also change how researchers conceive of a qubit’s structure. Diamond-based qubits are often treated as a physics problem, but Martirez’s work shows that chemistry offers another way to understand them. A fuller chemical understanding could let scientists design and build qubits for specific purposes, much as chemists design custom molecules, such as a new medication.

Martirez encouraged other chemists to bring their expertise into solid-state physics. “At this moment in time, there’s a great emphasis on building the first state-of-the-art qubit computer,” he said. “But it’s not just physicists who should be participating in this research. Chemists have decades of developed intuition about chemical systems that could be easily translated into understanding defects in solids as well.”

Practical quantum devices could eventually support sensors with greater sensitivity and accuracy than those currently available, along with computers capable of tackling highly complex problems far faster than today’s conventional supercomputers. Developing these future technologies is among the strategic national priorities PPPL works to advance.

For quantum chemistry, that progress could create a useful cycle. The field could help develop tools that allow it to “enrich itself” in the decades ahead, Martirez said. He expects a practical system containing multiple qubits to greatly improve the accuracy of quantum chemistry calculations.

“We could describe larger molecules, biomolecules, proteins, and everything like that in a more accurate way, and that could improve all the chemical-adjacent industries, such as pharmaceutical science and materials science. Even our fundamental understanding of molecular systems could be enriched.”

Reference: “A Chemist’s Guide to Solid-State Qubits: Diamond Color Centers as Embedded Vacancy-Centered Molecules” by John Mark P. Martirez, July 27, 2026, ACS Central Science. DOI: 10.1021/acscentsci.6c00548

Funding for this research comes from PPPL’s Laboratory Directed Research and Development under prime contract number DE-AC02-09CH11466, as well as additional support from DOE Office of Science, Office of Fusion Energy Sciences.

#981. This Connor Ives Coat Is Fall's Celebrity Breakout Piece

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The fall season is known for many glorious things—leaf-peeping, Halloween, perhaps even some apple-picking—but true fashion people know it to be, above all else, one crucial thing: Coat season.

After the sweltering summer months, fall is prime time to dust off your best outerwear, pull it out of the back of your closet, and once again make the coat the focal point. And without fail, every season, there’s an enduring style that always pops up on all of the cool it girls. . . a groovy Penny Lane coat, that is.

Flash back to the 2000 classic film Almost Famous, and Penny Lane’s free-spirited wardrobe (worn by actor Kate Hudson) still feels extremely fresh. In fact, her shaggy, faux fur-trimmed coat is so iconic that has since been countlessly recreated by modern-day designers, and worn by celebrities such as Bella Hadid and Zoe Kravitz. But what if we told you. . . that there’s a new fall It coat in town this year. One that still feels Penny Lane-coded, but more unexpectedly so.

Cue this season’s Connor Ives robe coat. First debuted during the American designer’s fall 2025 runway show, the luxurious outerwear is now having a moment. Made of a silk-satin base and trimmed with repurposed vintage fox fur along the collar and cuffs, this bohemian topper has already been spotted on several occasions this fall season—on A-listers like Kendall Jenner and Kate Moss, no less—despite its hefty price tag ($6,000 and up).

Moss was one of the first trendsetters to rock it out on the streets—all the way back in January, in fact. The supermodel wore one of Ives’s shorter black silk robe coats, pairing it with a classic pair of light-wash skinny jeans. A few months later, Zoe Kravitz also wore a light-blue iteration while attending an SNL after-party with boyfriend Harry Styles. A star coat was born.

This autumnal season, the slightly 1970s statement piece has only continued to grow in its power. Model Laura Harrier wore the same black version as Moss this month, with capri leggings and pointed pumps in L.A. Just this week, Kendall Jenner wore a long, cream style, finishing off her look with black high-waisted trousers and a tonal Chanel maxi tote.

If you really think about it, it’s no wonder the Connor Ives coats are becoming a breakout hit of the season: Like the OG Penny Lane coats, they are a total no-brainer: You can pair them with your most discreet everyday basics, and the striking piece instantly does all of the work for your outfit. It makes a statement, but in a super low-lift way. Isn’t that what you want out of a splurge-worthy investment coat?

Most importantly, though, the Ives coats are super cozy and plushy—perfect for those frigid fall days. It’s essentially a (chic) way to wrap yourself up in a snuggly blanket; only, you can also wear it out to dinner or a party and still look very put-together. Sounds like a win-win to us.

#3964. 10 Qualities of Exceptional Advocates

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

If you’re like me, you probably have some role models, mentors, or idols you look up to and respect for their awe-inspiring skills, gifts. and talents. This is the case in almost every field, whether in the arts, entertainment, business, or government, and whether these idols are leaders, scientists, or athletes.

If I asked you why you admire these individuals, you would likely be able to quickly list several of their positive qualities, such as honesty, personal integrity, talent, creativity, intelligence, or physical abilities.

Similarly, I’ve been thinking recently about several people I admire who are advocates in the areas of social change, mental health, and humanitarian efforts. I’ve noticed there seems to be a common set of qualities among those who are highly skilled in fields that promote efforts to improve the lives of others.

So here’s my list of 10 qualities that I think exceptional advocates often exhibit:

Enthusiastic, driven, compassionate, caring. All of these characteristics convey the passion of exceptional advocates. Their work is more than a job requirement or a professional responsibility. It’s a calling and a lifelong mission.

Great advocates are often the most knowledgeable person in the room (region, state, nation) on their area of focus. They are continually learning, plugged into current streams of information, and always ready to share their knowledge and expertise to educate others.

Whether expressing themselves verbally or in writing, and regardless of the medium (print, video, social media), these amazing advocates communicate clearly, persuasively, and respectfully to share information and viewpoints, and to advance their mission.

Without a clear goal, there is no clear focus and no clear path forward. Exceptional advocates have laser-sharp goals, whether the goal is passing helpful legislation, creating new programs, or garnering support for worthy causes.

Great advocates seem to have connections everywhere, with extensive networks at all levels. They are adept at building partnerships and coalitions and in connecting people with useful resources. They are often the first person people go to for information, support, and advice.

The most effective advocates know the power of collaboration and compromise. Not every battle can be won. Sometimes small victories are the only road to eventual success. These advocates know that flexibility is essential in moving steadily forward to achieve their longer-term goals.

What does it mean to be inspiring? It’s hard to articulate, but great advocates move us, and they stir our thoughts and feelings. They give us hope for a brighter tomorrow, and they outline a path for how we can reach it.

The best advocates not only inspire us, but they also instill in us a desire to pitch in and help out to collectively achieve a worthwhile goal. They show us how to raise our own voices to be heard and how to help others find their voice. They also remind us that we all matter and we all have a part to play.

Advocacy is not a sprint; it’s a marathon. Important goals can take years or decades to achieve. Great advocates show up and keep showing up. They are not deterred by setbacks or disappointment. They are the first to arrive and the last to leave.

In their hearts, great advocates are public servants. They do their work not for glory or fame, but for the quiet satisfaction of helping others and the steadfast belief that the work is the right thing to do and the only thing to do.

While most of us may not reach the level of these exceptional advocates, we can certainly follow their example and try to learn how to use some of these same qualities in our own causes and passions.

And remember, you don’t have to be exceptional. You just have to have a desire to make a difference and a willingness to put in a little work and effort toward your goals. Let’s get started.

A version of this post also appears at davidsusman.com. Copyright 2026.

#1005. Elena Dudum on Her Searching New Memoir, ‘They Told Me Back Home Would Be Beautiful’

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

One of the most alienating experiences a young person can have is realizing that who they are—at least, as they’ve learned to understand it—diverges from how their identity is understood by the rest of the world. As Elena Dudum recalls in her debut memoir, They Told Me Back Home Would Be Beautiful, she had that experience early, raised in early aughts San Francisco by Palestinian-Syrian-American parents. Since then, she has endeavored to continue work started by her father to build an archive of Palestinian history, examining and ultimately uplifting her cultural heritage.

Vogue spoke to Dudum about drawing inspiration from writers including Sarah Aziza, Cathy Park Hong, and Tara Westover for her book and telling her very personal story at a fraught sociopolitical moment.

Vogue: What did the process of putting this book together look like for you?

Elena Dudum: There are words in the book that were written in 2019, but I would say the majority of the work of the book really happened between 2023 and now. In 2023, I had graduated from grad school, and I had 40,000 words of what would become my proposal. That process of [writing] the proposal took about a year, and then I moved to London in January of 2024. It’s very strange because I had actually completed the other 40,000 words of the book in London, and then I left London a week before the book came out. Being in London created all this distance for me; I was in a city where I didn’t know many people and wasn’t in the same old routine. But part of the process was writing a book that wasn’t going to be the book. I originally wrote a version of it that didn’t even include me or my father.

You write so intimately about your family. How did you approach talking about the book and preparing them to see themselves in print?

Well, I had seven years of allowing my family to kind of warm up to this idea that I was writing a book. When I started the book in 2019, my family was like, “Okay, what?” I think they were a little confused or curious, but, like, not curious enough. My parents in particular were kind of upset by the idea because they were worried I’d be airing our dirty laundry. What ended up happening is that I published a few sections of my my thesis, which were the bones of my book, and my parents were sort of able to come on board as the process evolved. I received a grant, and they were like, “Oh, it’s interesting that someone gave her money for this.” Then I received another grant and got offers from agents, and I think as accolades slowly began to trickle in, it helped my parents see this as a professional, serious thing and something they could get behind and be okay with.

Are there other examples of Palestinian memoir, or memoir from the MENA diaspora more broadly, that inspired you?

I loved Sarah Aziza’s memoir and Hala Alyan’s memoir. Those both came out rather recently and they were so beautiful; they were different, but they obviously engage with similar themes and questions, because diasporic stories tend to do that. I really enjoyed reading those, and before those books, Raja Shehadeh’s book We Could Have Been Friends, My Father and I was a good primer of, like, how do you write about a tense and complicated relationship with a parent? I love memoirs that make you feel like you’re sitting in a story, and this might be the most overused example, but I did think about Educated by Tara Westover often. I thought about that book in terms of how a reader might come to my story. I was unfamiliar with so many parts of Tara’s world, but she created a full story that I could really sit in and fully imagine. There’s a novelistic aspect of that book that’s similar to Heavy by Kiese Laymon; he has such a singular voice, and I looked a lot at his work. Another book that I thought a lot about was Minor Feelings by Cathy Park Hong. Structurally, I didn’t want to write essays, but what I loved about that book was how it got into what I call this icky feeling of not quite understanding why you feel the way you feel, or perhaps having shame around your feelings about your identity and your family. I really appreciated Cathy’s voice while trying to navigate that through line in my own work.

We’re in such a strange cultural moment where we are, perhaps, hearing more Palestinian narratives than we used to, but it’s set against the context of the war in Gaza. How do you navigate that tension?

It’s something that I really struggled with at the beginning, because while I started my book in 2019 and I had 40,000 words’ worth of work I was sitting on, I had been submitting to editors and small journals and no one was responding. Then they did respond, and quite quickly, in the fall of 2023. That was upsetting because these voices and these narratives have been important for forever. I really struggled with that because I felt as though my work was riding a wave that I didn’t want it to ride. I have a close friend who’s Palestinian and grew up in the Arab world, and I was bitching and moaning. I was like, “This is so awkward, this is terrible,” and she was like, “You’re a Palestinian who’s alive. What are you going to do with that?” I remember that so vividly because when she said that, I was like, “She’s right, I’m alive.” I did not choose to be part of the diaspora, and yet there is a hunger for these stories now, so are you going to sit and pity yourself? Or are you going to actually take advantage and try to share as many stories as possible and uplift as many other Palestinian voices as possible?

Another thing I will say is that I also had to come to terms with the fact that I am telling a Palestinian story that is a diaspora story. I am not telling a Palestinian story of living under occupation, right? All I can do is connect dots and uplift voices, but I am not going to speak on behalf of Palestinians who have their own story and their own voice. It’s my job to uplift those Palestinians’ voices, but I am never going to replace them.

What’s your biggest hope for this memoir?

I want to reach as many people as possible, and I want to reach those who are Palestinian or Arab and can feel like their stories are finally seen and centered, especially those who are like, I didn’t know how to talk about this terrible feeling of not coming to terms with my identity until later. I think this is a story that’s not just Arab or Palestinian; it’s so many diasporas. I wish there was a book like this when I was growing up that I could have read and that honored my experience in an important way. I also want the book to reach people who aren’t of the diaspora, and who can then see what a strange thing it is to have to constantly lie and navigate this big conversation from the age of five or six years old. I want people to feel less alone, but I also want it to serve as a wake-up call.

This conversation has been edited and condensed.

#3636. Pace Gallery Reports Losses of 35.5 Percent for 2024 With ‘Real Change’ Underway

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It has been a summer of change for Pace Gallery, and its 2024 accounts that were filed 10 months late with Companies House, the British government’s business register, reflect that.

The group’s turnover totaled $67.69 million in 2024, a 35.5 percent drop from 2023, which cashed in $104.95 million. A pre-tax loss of $2,429 was recorded for 2024 compared to a $6.8 million profit for 2023. (The mega-gallery’s UK registered company, The Pace Gallery Limited, serves as the umbrella company for its operations in Europe and Asia, excluding Tokyo as well as the US.) Related Articles Marina Perez Simão Wants You to Meditate in Front of Her Paintings at Frieze London Pace Gallery Is Seeking a Tenant for Part of Its New York Headquarters

“This reflected a continuation of the sector-wide contraction in global and UK art markets as well as a continued reluctance by some clients to buy which became most pronounced in 2024 and continued through 2025 and into 2026,” Pace Gallery said in the filing. “The market conditions led the directors to initiate cost saving measures across the group, but despite these measures, difficult market conditions continued.”

Net assets totaled $2.48 million compared to $4.51 million for 2023, while $9.39 million was payable to its US parent company, The Pace Gallery LLC, which in 2023 was $17.31 million. Further numbers show that The Pace Gallery Limited’s debt of $19.2 million will be turned into shares by the end of 2026 as it’s unable to repay $19.2 million to its US parent company.

“The proposed debt-for-equity conversion is part of a broader global financial management strategy, which reflects a capital structure decision within the Pace group and should not be interpreted as a loss or a liquidity issue for the UK business,” a Pace spokesperson said. “As part of the ongoing management of our global operations, we periodically review how our subsidiaries are financed to ensure that their balance sheets appropriately reflect the long-term nature of the capital invested in those businesses.”

The gallery added that the parent company has determined that a portion of its investment in the UK operation is more “appropriately reflected as equity rather than intercompany debt. The proposed transaction is intended to strengthen the UK company’s balance sheet and align its capital structure with the long-term needs of the business.”

Back in June, Pace’s London property was put on the market, with the gallery saying it was seeking a “less corporate” space in the British capital. Last month, Pace announced it was seeking tenants for part of its Chelsea headquarters.

Pace CEO Marc Glimcher recently told reporter Julie Brener Davich in the Substack The Appraisal that by early 2026, the company was convinced that “we needed to make real change in the structure of the business to adapt to the new reality.” He explains that this new reality takes into account factors such as: rising interest rates, global instability, the closing of the 1031 exchange tax loophole, as well as managing logistics costs for shipping and crating.
