#2928. Part 28 – Back in the Game (The Power of Pit Toilets)

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

At 11:30am sharp, Summer, our ride, showed up right on time. She picked up Beer Money, an Australian SOBO hiker, and me. We were travelling in one of those old cars with a driver’s seat and a boot… and apparently no particular consideration for passenger comfort. We had to experiment with various contortions to fit ourselves, our backpacks and whatever remained of our legs into the available space.

A minor detail. The ride promised to be an epic one.

We picked up Juliette on the way and, by noon, we were back at the trailhead. I was aiming for 10–12 miles that day, to give my knee some time to adjust, especially with a full resupply on my back. Another hot day, with water sources few and far between.

Juliette and I walked together for most of the afternoon. When we bumped into Grace, whom I had not seen since Etna, our little team of two suddenly became a group of three.

My mood, however, was still not quite there.

I could rationally explain what was happening to myself, but I was still tired and, somehow, not particularly excited about the days ahead. It was an odd feeling, perhaps the first time since I had started this whole adventure. I knew the fatigue would catch up with me eventually. I had never seriously considered quitting. Not an option. I’m not a quitter. Or at least, rarely!

But if I’m being honest, this feeling had been brewing for a few weeks already. Limping around for days and being unable to squat or climb without negotiating with my knee hardly makes for an exciting start to the morning.

The endless state of California. The countless miles occupying my every thought. The next water source, the elevation profile, converting feet into metres and miles into kilometres. Cereal bars and trail mix. Tortillas with tuna and chips. Ramen with whatever flavour I could find that was different enough to qualify as a culinary experience. The power of repetition had finally caught up with me.

I had thought that my spectacular collapse in Ashland, followed by three full days of rest, would be enough to bring me back to life. Instead, I was beginning to realise that I still had two entire states to cross. I was worried my body might not hold up, and, for once, I was even doubting my ability to make it all the way to the sacrosanct Canadian border.

More tortillas. More tuna. More ramen. More Clif Bars. More water sources. More disrupted nights. And of course, more miles. Good grief. Canada suddenly sounded like it was on the other side of the world. But no quitting. Not yet.

Luckily, the trail provides. And if it couldn’t give me a brand-new body and a completely refreshed mindset, at least it could provide some company to help me get back in the game. Juliette and Grace were great company, with very different personalities but equally fun and motivating. As the afternoon went on, we spotted a potential campsite on FarOut, conveniently located near a pit toilet.

Sometimes, it’s all about embracing the suck, as the saying goes. But sometimes, the only thing you need to cheer you up is a bloody pit toilet. No squatting. No falling over. No digging a cat hole. And actual toilet paper!

I don’t think I had ever been that excited about a toilet in my life. But let’s face it: after more than three months on the trail, avoiding the cat-hole routine and sparing my still-painful knee a few awkward movements had become legitimate priorities. I had even heard of hikers who would go out of their way to reach a pit toilet and then… hold it for the rest of the time.

Juliette and I collected enough water at a source a few miles before our campsite to last us through the night and into the next day. We continued hiking and were soon joined by Grace. We reached our planned campsite and enjoyed a beautiful sunset, with shades of pink lingering in the sky until the last light disappeared.

During dinner, while checking the map for the following day, we spotted a purple icon on FarOut. Purple usually meant a town, although sometimes it was just a couple of buildings with some sort of commercial activity. I had come to call these places Civilization.

This particular icon represented Hyatt Lake Resort, a tiny hotel in a remote location with hot meals and a small shop. It was a bit of a detour. But it took the three of us approximately three seconds to agree on a plan. Hyatt Lake Resort, here we come.

What a glorious morning! The night had been quiet, and I had barely woken up. The three of us got up and packed at roughly the same time, then set off after a light breakfast.

We were saving room for the feast awaiting us at Hyatt Lake Resort.

By that point, my appetite had got completely out of control. After a quick scan of the menu, we all opted for the monstrous pancakes with bacon and eggs. Add coffee, and we were all set. Breakfast of champions. Or, more accurately, breakfast of people who had already been walking for hours and were still planning to walk for several more.

The resort itself was fairly simple: a main building housing the restaurant and a tiny shop, surrounded by a few cabins. Across the road was a peaceful lake with kayaks available for rent. It was probably a popular spot earlier in the season, although it was rather quiet when we visited.

The decor was a little kitsch, with wooden animal ornaments, red checked tablecloths and the usual rustic touches. But the manager and waitress were lovely, genuinely curious about where we came from and what we were doing.

With our stomachs full, we left in excellent spirits and decided to walk around the lake rather than retrace our steps to the trail. In hindsight, this was perhaps not our brightest idea.

The scenery was pleasant enough, and the quiet, deserted lake offered some lovely views here and there. But after about 3km of road walking that felt suspiciously like 15, we were beginning to question our decision.

Then we spotted a truck slowing down. A man got out and waved at us. Juliette and I looked at each other. Was this guy taking pity on us and about to offer us a ride? Nah. Probably not.

We hurried towards him to find out. His name was Randy, and he had indeed spotted us at the resort and taken pity on our little expedition. Although the area was perfectly safe, he said he would feel better driving the three of us back to the trailhead.

Of course, we could have declined on principle. There are rules to this hiking business, after all, including the sacred principle of not hitchhiking around a boring section just because you can’t be bothered to walk it. But then there was the heavy pack, the post-pancake digestion process, and the prospect of walking an unknown distance on a road. Principles are important. Pancakes are also important. And humans are weak…

We climbed into the back of the truck, where Randy’s dogs were absolutely delighted to have some company.

As it turned out, the road was much longer than we had expected. Randy had saved us a fair amount of unnecessary walking, and we were very grateful. Back at the trailhead, we thanked him and resumed our journey, mostly uphill through the forest, which rewarded us with some spectacular views. I was happy to discover that my knee was finally behaving again. I tried to take it easy, and, thankfully, there were no major blowdowns to negotiate. Around 6pm, we decided to call it a day and camp together in an area scattered with fallen trees.

The day had been full of little adventures and plenty of laughter. I was grateful to be hiking again with a group. I know I needed that. We were all pretty independent and tended to walk at our own pace, but having people around after the fatigue of Northern California had given me a new burst of energy and motivation.

Over dinner, we chatted and exchanged random gossip while doing what had become our daily ritual: checking the next day’s mileage, water sources and elevation profile. And, naturally, we spotted another side quest. Fish Lake Resort offered yet another opportunity for burgers, fries and a beer.

The hiking gods were definitely on our side.

We left camp around 7am. As usual, the three of us soon split up, each walking at her own pace. After about five hours, we reached the main road where we planned to hitchhike to Fish Lake Resort. I had been restraining my appetite all morning. By almost 1pm, I could quite literally have eaten a whole cow.

Juliette had just arrived, and we stood together by the road, trying to come up with facial expressions and jokes that might convince passing drivers to stop. Or perhaps inspire pity. We were open to either approach. A few other hikers joined us, but for a while, nothing worked.

Then a car coming from the opposite direction slowed down. The occupants offered to take us back to the resort they had just left.

We thanked them profusely and jumped in. Katie and Dwayne, sitting in the front, explained that they had originally been heading the other way but had decided to help us because it was only a short detour.

The moment we arrived at the restaurant, we spotted Fred and Anna leaving to get back on the trail. We had about five minutes to catch up before they disappeared again.

Then came the usual routine: finding electrical outlets to recharge every device we owned, studying the menu and contemplating the burgers with barely disguised enthusiasm.

Somehow, my Dr Pepper consumption capacity had increased significantly since leaving Mexico. I was beginning to suspect that, by the end of the trail, my blood might be partially carbonated.

We settled down to enjoy the comfort of civilization. The burgers arrived, and Grace soon joined us. She had been behind us but had texted to say we should go ahead with hitchhiking. She had reached the road later and, as luck would have it, found a ride too.

We also bumped into a French couple hiking SOBO, exchanged some information about trail conditions, particularly in Washington, and shared what we knew about Northern California.

Three hours after arriving, we were finally ready to leave.

When we asked the manager about the best way to return to the trail, he casually informed us that the resort offered a free shuttle service. Of course it did. We could have saved ourselves the entire hitchhiking operation. But where would the adventure have been in that?

At 4:15pm, we were back on the trail, fully hydrated, thoroughly fed and ready for another 8.5 miles. Total mileage for the day: 25 miles, including our luxurious side quest. Victory!

Grace and I walked at a good pace through the forest and eventually found a lovely, empty meadow. It looked like a perfect place to camp.

Grace decided to cowboy camp, while I pitched my mesh tent.

We were having dinner when we suddenly heard some strange noises.

The sound was somewhere between a cow and a wild animal. It came again, then again, seemingly closer and from different directions. Now, we had been living in the wilderness for more than three months. We were both perfectly rational human beings. And yet, somehow, we were both getting a little nervous. We considered the possibilities. Bears? Unlikely. Zombies? Technically, no evidence either way.

After a thorough process of elimination based on absolutely no scientific knowledge whatsoever, we settled on bucks. Deer. Probably. That explanation seemed reassuring enough, so we decided to believe it. There was no point letting facts get in the way of a good night’s sleep.

Kelly, a hiker from Boston whom I had met at my hostel in Ashland, arrived around 8:30pm and joined us for dinner before setting up camp nearby.

The meadow was lovely. The wildlife was… mysterious.

The same strange animal noises woke me up at 5am.

I stayed firmly inside my sleeping bag. It was chilly, and absolutely nobody around me seemed to be moving. Not Kelly. Not Grace.

I saw no reason to be the first to leave the warmth of my sleeping bag and investigate the potential presence of whatever had been making those noises. Let the wildlife have its morning. I would have mine later.

As I lay there, I thought about the fatigue that had been creeping up on me over the past few weeks. I was glad that the worst of that feeling seemed to have faded. But would I manage to keep my motivation up? It was a question I had not really asked myself before.

The day itself unfolded much like any other on the PCT. Checking mileage. Checking elevation. Checking water sources. Making random small talk with other hikers. And before I knew it, I had covered 25 miles. Somewhere along the way, I had lost Grace, so I ended up camping alone in a meadow in the forest. I hoped for a quiet, uneventful night. Ideally, one without any unidentified wildlife concerts.

I crossed another burned area, reminiscent of Lassen National Park in Norcal, with a wide-open plain dotted with dead trees, their magnificent white trunks still reaching towards the blue morning sky.

But life was slowly reclaiming the landscape. Tiny pine trees were emerging, adding splashes of green to the otherwise desolate scenery. There was something moving about it: a reminder that even after destruction, things find a way to grow again.

The trail itself was, as so often in Oregon, smooth, rolling and forgiving. Of course, there were still blowdowns. I had been dreading the section between Fish Lake Resort and Crater Lake, which had a reputation for forcing hikers to scramble over or around more than 200 fallen trees. Luckily, a trail maintenance crew seemed to have passed through recently. I counted no more than 20 blowdowns, most of them easy enough to walk around. My IT band was grateful. So was I.

After the rest days in Ashland, my knee had improved considerably, and I was beginning to feel more confident about what lay ahead.

My goal for the day was Mazama Village, below Crater Lake, one of Oregon’s major highlights. I was excited about it. The plan was to arrive around lunchtime, enjoy some decent food, take a shower, collect my resupply box sent from Dunsmuir, wash my clothes and catch up with friends who were either ahead of me or still somewhere behind. In other words, a highly ambitious programme of civilization-related activities.

A long stretch of forest followed the burned area, and I started getting a little bored. Time for a podcast. I opted for a programme about Farinelli, one of the most famous Italian castrati, who had travelled across Europe and enchanted kings and queens with his voice.

And then something quite magical happened.

Listening to opera music in the middle of the forest was unexpectedly moving. The grandiose voices seemed to fill the trees, transforming an otherwise ordinary stretch of trail into something almost otherworldly. For a while, the music and the surrounding landscape seemed perfectly in tune. The forest became a concert hall, and I had a front-row seat. No tickets required. No dress code. And, best of all, no one checking whether I had showered recently.

I arrived at Mazama around 12:30pm, had a burger, called home and headed to the store. And who did I bump into? Corinna, whom I had not seen since Burney Falls! Pack Rat was there too, followed by Grace and then Juliette. Suddenly, the afternoon turned into a series of catch-ups, conversations, showers and laundry. I even managed to hand-wash my clothes and get everything dry within a couple of hours, thanks to the hot, sunny weather.

Perfect conditions for another essential trail activity: ice cream.

It is funny, because I hardly ever crave ice cream at home. But out here, somehow, it becomes a perfectly reasonable reward for walking around in the wilderness for months. Inspired by Grace, I treated myself to a scoop.

My resupply box was waiting for me, so I only needed to buy a few extra items. Other hikers began arriving gradually, until the entrance to the general store was practically taken over by hikers. Many of them had attended Trail Days, and the gathering had turned into a kind of travelling reunion. People were catching up on the latest news, swapping stories and comparing plans. The PCT bubble had caught up with us.

The campsite for PCT hikers was cheap, but located at the far end of the campground, among huge RVs and weekend visitors who had come to see one of the deepest crater lakes in the world. By the time I arrived, the place was full. I eventually found a tiny patch of ground just large enough for my tent.

Corinna, a few other hikers and I had dinner together. I ordered a huge pizza. It was so enormous that I barely managed half of it and had to take the rest away. Pizza on trail: another experiment in backcountry cuisine.

I headed back to the campsite early and chatted over a beer with a friend before we returned to our respective tents.

Would I manage to get up at 3:30am, pack my things and hike the climb to Crater Lake’s rim in time for sunrise?

This had been on my PCT bucket list for a long time. But getting there would require an early start and a climb of around four to five miles. Sunrise, inconveniently, was not going to wait for me.

I was also feeling a little demotivated at the thought of doing it alone.

So I decided to leave the decision until the morning.

And went to sleep with absolutely no faith in myself whatsoever.

#1014. 7 Health Benefits of Basil, From Antioxidants to Stress Relief

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If there’s one aromatic herb we tend to associate with summer, it’s basil. A staple of Italian cuisine—as well as Thai, Vietnamese, Indonesian, and Indian cuisines—its distinctive fragrance and flavor give character to everything from caprese salad and Genovese pesto to pho and green curry.

Despite its seasonal connotations, you don’t need to limit basil to summer. This wonderfully versatile herb deserves a place in our kitchens year-round, thanks to its culinary appeal and nutritional properties. Although we typically eat it in small quantities, basil contains vitamins, minerals, and antioxidant compounds that researchers have investigated for their potential health effects. Holy basil (tulsi), a related but distinct species, has even been studied for its potential effects on stress, mood, sleep, and anxiety. Most such studies, however, use concentrated extracts rather than amounts typically eaten in food.

In other words, this fragrant little herb has plenty going for it beyond its ability to elevate a bowl of pasta. You can enjoy it year-round, fresh, dried, or frozen. Below is a closer look at what makes basil so special.

Native to tropical Asia and widely cultivated across the Mediterranean basin, basil (Ocimum basilicum) belongs to the Lamiaceae (mint) family. Its distinctive fragrance comes from naturally occurring compounds such as linalool, estragole, and eugenol, found in its essential oils. Culinary basil comes in many varieties, including crinkly-leaved Napoletano, purple-stemmed Thai basil, and cinnamon-scented basil.

Naturally low in calories and made up largely of water, basil also contains a range of nutrients, though a typical garnish provides only small amounts of most. These include minerals such as copper, potassium, magnesium, calcium, iron, and phosphorus, along with vitamins K, A, C, and E, folate, and pantothenic acid.

It also contains antioxidants, including flavonoids such as quercetin and apigenin, and carotenoids such as beta-carotene, lutein, and zeaxanthin, the latter two of which support eye health.

Even in small quantities, basil contributes nutrients and beneficial plant compounds to our diets—giving us one more reason to be generous with those fragrant green leaves. While research into basil’s potential health effects is promising, evidence on the benefits of eating it in typical culinary amounts remains limited. Here’s what the research suggests about the health benefits of basil:

For most people, basil is perfectly safe when consumed in the quantities typically used in cooking. However, as with many herbs, concentrated extracts and essential oils are a different matter.

Pregnant and breastfeeding women should avoid medicinal doses or concentrated basil preparations unless advised otherwise by a health care professional, since their safety has not been adequately established.

People taking warfarin or similar vitamin K–antagonist anticoagulants should also be mindful of basil’s vitamin K content. Consistency in vitamin K intake generally matters more than avoiding vitamin K–rich foods altogether.

Basil is generally in season from spring through early autumn, making the warmer months the perfect time to enjoy it fresh. Use whole or chopped leaves to garnish everything from a traditional mozzarella and tomato salad to pasta, pizza, and grilled vegetables.

Still, there’s no reason to abandon basil when the temperatures drop. Thanks to greenhouse cultivation, supermarkets often carry fresh basil year-round, and dried or frozen basil is just as convenient.

One simple way to preserve it is to wash and thoroughly dry the leaves, portion them into an ice-cube tray, cover them with olive oil, and freeze immediately. Once frozen, transfer the cubes to a freezer-safe container and add them directly to soups, sauces, and other cooked dishes. Never store fresh herbs in oil at room temperature.

Basil’s talents extend well beyond pasta, pizza, and salads. Its fragrant, slightly peppery flavor adds a distinctly Mediterranean touch to all sorts of dishes—including a few you might not expect.

1. Give your grains a Mediterranean twist. Toss brown rice, spelt, barley, millet, or quinoa with freshly chopped basil, a drizzle of olive oil, sunflower or toasted sesame seeds (black sesame works beautifully too), a few drops of umami-rich shoyu soy sauce, and a sprinkle of Parmesan or nutritional yeast.

2. Add it to soups and legumes. Stir fresh or dried basil into classic minestrone; creamy soups made with pumpkin, sweet potatoes, or zucchini; or dishes featuring chickpeas, lentils, peas, mung beans, and adzuki beans. It’s an easy way to introduce a little extra fragrance and flavor.

3. Try it in smoothies. A few fresh basil leaves can lend an unexpectedly refreshing note to protein shakes and smoothies. Think banana and almond milk or cantaloupe blended with wheat germ and coconut milk.

4. Use it in dessert. Yes, really. A couple of basil leaves can add an elegant finishing touch to chocolate mousse made with silken tofu and unsweetened cocoa powder or to chia-seed pudding with strawberries, matcha, and Greek yogurt.

5. Elevate your tinned fish. Canned sardines have been having quite the moment on social media—and for good reason, given their protein, omega-3 fatty acids, and vitamin B12 content. A sprinkle of dried basil adds a fragrant Mediterranean touch.

6. Pair it with salmon. Try sprinkling dried basil over grilled or baked salmon. The combination may seem unexpected, but its aromatic freshness pairs beautifully with the fish’s richness.

7. Make a quick basil sauce. Blend olive oil, toasted almonds, garlic, and chopped basil into a simple sauce to add a little extra personality to your usual grilled chicken or turkey breast.

8. Upgrade your breakfast. Fresh or dried basil makes a delicious addition to avocado toast, scrambled eggs, omelets, and even pan-fried tofu.

9. Finish your roasted vegetables with basil. A scattering of fresh or dried leaves can brighten roasted squash, cauliflower, or sweet potatoes. For the freshest flavor, add delicate fresh leaves just before serving.

#335. Air France KLM’s $89 Card Offers 70,000 Miles—And A Strategy For Cheaper Business Class And First Class Access

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I receive compensation for content and many links on this blog. Be aware that websites may earn compensation when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Citibank is an advertising partner of this site, as is American Express, Chase, Capital One, and others. Any opinions expressed in this post are my own, and have not been reviewed, approved, or endorsed by my advertising partners. I do not write about all offers that are available -- instead focusing on miles, points, and cash back (and currencies that can be converted into the same). Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

Air France KLM Visa Signature® credit card

Air France KLM’s $89-a-year credit card is offering 70,000 bonus miles and enough status credits to earn Flying Blue Silver without taking a flight with qualifying spend. The miles can cover a one-way business class award to Europe when the lowest-priced seats are available, plus taxes and surcharges. But the reason this card interests me is what it can do for the price and availability of future awards.

I’ve already laid out my plan to use the card to help reach Flying Blue Platinum. That means access to more low-priced business class awards, free changes and refunds even on their cheapest awards, and permission to redeem miles for Air France La Première first class.

The current offer for the card is 70,000 bonus miles plus 100 Experience Points after $3,000 in purchases within the first 90 days of account opening. Experience Points, usually shortened to XP, are the credits Flying Blue uses for elite status. This is promoted as a limited-time offer, though it’s one we’ve seen before, including when they refreshed the product back in January. The card has an $89 annual fee and no foreign transaction fees. It earns: 3 miles per dollar on eligible Air France, KLM and SkyTeam airline purchases, including Delta. 3 miles per dollar on eligible dining. 1.5 miles per dollar on other purchases. 5,000 anniversary miles after at least $50 in purchases during the card anniversary year. Getting The Most Out Of The Miles Flying Blue remains one of the most useful programs for Americans trying to get to Europe in business class. They have substantial service across the Atlantic, generally make more award space available through their own program than to partners, and accept transfers from the major bank points programs. You can combine miles from this card with transferred points for the same award. I’ve written about 38 ways to get more out of Flying Blue. For this bonus, I’d focus on: Search the whole trip. If you want New York to Rome, search New York to Rome because . Don’t assume that pricing New York to Paris is the same price since adding a connection beyond Paris or Amsterdam can actually lower the mileage price. Look at Promo Rewards. Monthly offers discount selected routes by up to 25%. Use the child discount. Children ages 2 through 11 traveling with an adult receive a 25% mileage discount on eligible Air France and KLM awards. Consider a stopover. Flying Blue permits stopovers on eligible awards without additional miles, booked by phone. Flying Blue introduced Light, Standard and Flex awards on September 8. Business Light includes one checked bag, but no lounge access or voluntary changes or refunds unless your status supplies those benefits. Gold provides lounge access. Platinum and Ultimate members can change or refund Light awards without a fee, although changing to a more expensive award can still require additional miles. Why I Want Platinum I’ve used Flying Blue Gold obtained through Bilt for priority boarding, free checked bags and free exit row seats on Delta. My interest in Platinum is mostly about redeeming miles. Platinum and Ultimate members have access to more awards at the lowest mileage prices. And only Platinum and Ultimate members can redeem for Air France La Première. The awards are expensive, and the published limit is one award seat per flight. The Status Math And Timing The card provides these credits at the account anniversary: 20 XP without a spending requirement. Another 80 XP after $15,000 in purchases during the anniversary year. Another 60 XP after $25,000 in total purchases during that year. That’s 160 XP at the anniversary after $25,000 in spending. The $25,000 includes the first $15,000. Add the 100-XP welcome bonus and the card can generate 260 XP across the welcome offer and first anniversary. Flying Blue starts credits over as you move up: starting without status, you need 100 XP for Silver, another 180 for Gold, and another 300 for Platinum. That’s 580 XP from scratch. Starting with Gold, though, means you need just the 300 XP for Platinum. That’s where my Bilt Platinum status match comes in. Bilt Platinum members can activate 12 months of Flying Blue Gold by transferring 10,000 Bilt points (you receive those 10,000 Flying Blue miles as well). With Gold already in place when the initial bonus’s credits arrive, the card’s 260 XP can leave just 40 more to earn for Platinum. But the 160 annual credits arrive at the anniversary, not when you cross $25,000 in purchases. For someone using a fresh Gold match, the sequence is: Open the credit card first. Time the Gold match so it’s active before the welcome XP posts, while still leaving enough time to complete the $3,000 purchase requirement within 90 days. Leave a buffer between the card anniversary and the end of the matched Gold qualification period, since anniversary credits need time to post. Complete $25,000 in total purchases during the card year. The initial $3,000 counts toward this. Earn the additional 40 XP, through eligible paid flights or other qualifying activities, within the same Gold qualification period as the card credits. The initial bonus helps once. In later years, 160 card XP leaves you 140 XP short of the 300 needed to maintain Platinum. For someone maintaining Gold, the card’s annual maximum leaves a smaller, 20-XP gap. There’s a cost to directing $25,000 in spending here. On purchases earning 1.5 miles per dollar, that’s 37,500 miles, versus 50,000 transferable points from a card earning 2 per dollar. Rent is another way to generate the spending. As I covered in May, the card earns 3 Flying Blue miles per dollar on eligible rent payments through Bilt, up to $50,000 per calendar year, then 1.5 miles per dollar. There’s a 3% fee. You earn miles on the fee too. So $25,000 in eligible rent payments, plus $750 in fees, produces 77,250 miles while supplying the spending for the annual status credits. Whether that’s worthwhile depends on how you’d otherwise pay rent and earn rewards. Earn the additional 40 XP, through eligible paid flights or other qualifying activities, within the same Gold qualification period as the card credits. The initial bonus helps once. In later years, 160 card XP leaves you 140 XP short of the 300 needed to maintain Platinum. For someone maintaining Gold, the card’s annual maximum leaves a smaller, 20-XP gap. There’s a cost to directing $25,000 in spending here. On purchases earning 1.5 miles per dollar, that’s 37,500 miles, versus 50,000 transferable points from a card earning 2 per dollar. Rent is another way to generate the spending. As I covered in May, the card earns 3 Flying Blue miles per dollar on eligible rent payments through Bilt, up to $50,000 per calendar year, then 1.5 miles per dollar. There’s a 3% fee. You earn miles on the fee too. So $25,000 in eligible rent payments, plus $750 in fees, produces 77,250 miles while supplying the spending for the annual status credits. Whether that’s worthwhile depends on how you’d otherwise pay rent and earn rewards. Flying Blue remains one of the most useful programs for Americans trying to get to Europe in business class. They have substantial service across the Atlantic, generally make more award space available through their own program than to partners, and accept transfers from the major bank points programs. You can combine miles from this card with transferred points for the same award.

I’ve written about 38 ways to get more out of Flying Blue. For this bonus, I’d focus on:

Flying Blue introduced Light, Standard and Flex awards on September 8. Business Light includes one checked bag, but no lounge access or voluntary changes or refunds unless your status supplies those benefits. Gold provides lounge access. Platinum and Ultimate members can change or refund Light awards without a fee, although changing to a more expensive award can still require additional miles.

#1214. Inside Beyoncé and Jay-Z's Impressive Real Estate Portfolio

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Super Bowl halftime headliner and Beyoncé have spent hundreds of millions on homes from Malibu to Manhattan.

We’re always curious to peek inside celebrities’ homes, whether it’s Kendrick Lamar's Brentwood mansion or Gwyneth Paltrow's Los Angeles estate. With Jay-Z set to headline the 2027 Super Bowl halftime show, we’re turning our attention to the impressive real estate portfolio he shares with Beyoncé (who is no stranger to the halftime stage herself). Over the years, they've spent hundreds of millions on their dream homes, often working with notable architects to find hidden gems on both the east and west coasts.

The duo have bought and sold several properties in the last decade or so, including a $10 million midtown Manhattan penthouse. But we're most interested in the homes they currently own, and we have all the details. Here, find out where the ultimate power couple spends their days.

In 2023, Beyoncé and Jay-Z spent $200 million on a mansion in Malibu, according to TMZ, which went untouched in the recent L.A. fires. The house was designed by renown architect Tadao Ando, whose previous works have been owned by the likes of Kanye West. The 30,000 square-foot compound looks over the Pacific Ocean from the cliffs above, and features Ando's signature concrete-and-glass construction. Photos of the mansion show a museum-like interior, with floor-to-ceiling windows and several pools.

In 2017, the power couple spent $88 million on their Bel-Air home, according to Business Insider. Designed by architect Paul McClean, the nearly 19,000-square-foot mansion is yet another modern masterpiece. The home features eight bedrooms, 15 bathrooms, a 15-car garage, and basketball court, alongside outdoor spaces and several pools. One of the key interior features of the house is a spiral staircase that was carved out of a single piece of wood. The annual taxes alone cost nearly $1 million, Realtor.com reported.

In the ultra-wealthy Tribeca neighborhood of New York, Jay-Z reportedly bought the penthouse apartment in this downtown building in 2004 for $6.8 million. In 2008, this is where the couple got married. The penthouse, located at 195 Hudson Street, reportedly features an elegant balcony. The locale comes with famous neighbors, too: Hailey and Justin Bieber rent in the same building, and Bethenny Frankel is a former resident, according to the Post. A three-bed, 2-bath unit in the building was listed in May, 2024 and sold for $6.6 million, the New York Post reported.

Beyoncé and Jay-Z reportedly spent $25.9 million on an East Hampton compound aptly named the Pond House, which was originally listed at $35.9 million in 2009. Created by legendary architect Stanford White—who built Madison Square Garden in 1891—the palatial Pond House comes in at 12,000 square feet, and features seven bedrooms and nine bathrooms. The old-school classic elements include parquet floors, marble bathtubs, and 18th-century fireplaces. It was built to overlook the Georgica Pond.

While it’s never been publicly confirmed, Jay-Z and Beyoncé are the supposed owners of a Spanish Baroque church-turned-residence in the Garden District. An LLC linked to the couple acquired the property in 2015 and put it up for sale in 2021 for $4.45 million, but it never changed hands.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

Tatjana Freund is Hearst's Fashion & Luxury Commerce Editor, covering beauty, fashion and more across multiple brands. Previously, she worked at ELLE.com and Marie Claire. She's a fan of whiskey neat, podcasts that give her nightmares, and one time Zoë Kravitz laughed at a joke she made.

#3646. Christie’s Will Offer $8 M. in Works from Mickey Cartin’s Collection, from Netherlandish Painting to Contemporary Art to Rare Books

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Widely respected Connecticut collector Mickey Cartin will offer selections from his collection, begun in 1988, at Christie’s. Upward of 80 objects, estimated to fetch as much as $8 million, will be spread over more than 10 auctions in London, Paris, and New York, from this November until July 2027.

In 1971, Cartin began working at Connecticut’s Capitol Light and Supply Company, which had been started by his grandfather in 1918. He ultimately became the company’s president and grew it from a staff of a dozen to a national enterprise employing hundreds. His collection hung in his homes in the Constitution State; Delray Beach, Florida; and a 5,800-square-foot Greenwich Village apartment that is currently on the market for $10 million. Now 78, Cartin ranked on the ARTnews Top 200 Collectors list from 2007 to 2013. Related Articles Sotheby's Dinosaur 'Jodi' Sells for $47.3 M., More Than Five Times Its High Estimate Bill Koch to Sell More Than $40 M. in Art at Christie's, Led by Monet and Cezanne Paintings

Two of the top-estimated works sketch out a picture of the diversity of his holdings. One is Roman Opałka’s over-six-foot-high painting OPAŁKA 1965/1 – Détail 3820343 -3843981 (executed according to an artistic program conceived in 1965), tagged at $400,000–$600,000, which is part of a what the artist called a single work in which he aimed to paint every number from one to infinity.

Also estimated in the mid-six figures is a 15th-century Netherlandish panel painting, Resurrection of Christ, by the Master of the Virgo Inter Virgines, that was restituted to the heirs of the Dutch dealer Jacques Goudstikker after it was looted by the Nazis. It is the rare European work of its time to include a Black figure among the soldiers at Christ’s tomb, and it previously hung in Amsterdam’s Rijksmuseum. Artnet’s auction price database includes just nine works by the Master, his circle, or “in the manner of” the artist; his top sale to date was $35,874, paid for a crucifixion scene at Kunsthaus Lempertz in 1998.

Cartin also collects material as wide-ranging as outsider art and rare books stretching from the 13th century to contemporary artists’ books. “One of the things that gets said about Mickey is he was one of the earliest collectors who collected across categories,” Steven Holmes, Cartin’s curator of two decades, said in a phone interview. “But he never even really understood categories, and still doesn’t. He’s just compelled by the object on its own.”

Other top-estimated lots include Walton Ford’s watercolor Avatars – The Birds of India (1996), estimated at $200,000–$300,000; a 1950 Giorgio de Chirico self-portrait, at $180,000–$250,000; a 1517 book, the Theuerdank, printed for Emperor Maximilian I and recounting his youthful exploits in verse, at £100,000—£150,000 (about $133,000–$200,000); and German physician Georg Bartisch’s 1583 book Ophthalmodouleia: Das ist Augendienst, the first Renaissance manuscript on ophthalmic disorders and eye surgery, estimated at £70,000–£100,000 ($93,000–$133,000).

Selections from Cartin’s collection have come to market before, including in a 2021 exhibition at David Zwirner in New York that included artists as diverse as Josef Albers, Joseph Cornell, Albrecht Dürer, Vilhelm Hammershøi, Giorgio Morandi, Rembrandt van Rijn, Myron Stout, Adolf Wölfli, and Joseph Yoakum.

Cartin has been known to hang works in productive conversations that, naturally, dispense with categories. “I remember when we installed the collection in such a way that you could see an Agnes Martin, a Jean-Michel Basquiat, a Hans Memling Madonna from the 16th century, and then the Master of the Virgo Inter Virgines, an astounding painting from the Netherlands from the late 1400s,” said Holmes. “These each have their own story and their own holding power, but all together, it was such a an experience of them all going together in a way that made sense without there necessarily being any logic to it. There is something devotional about an Agnes Martin, which is what the Madonna is. There were notions of devotion, or miracle, or meanings above meaning.”

He added, “These can be really quite thrilling, not just visually or in terms of who the artists are, their art historical importance, but the energy that comes out of those conversations and proximities.”

“There are two tangible ideas in his collecting philosophy,” Michael Baptist, Christie’s vice president, specialist, and co-head of postwar and contemporary art day sale, said in a phone interview. “One, he is curious about ideas and things that are exquisite examples of artists even if he doesn’t know about them. If he sees an idea in an artwork, that turns him on as a collector.”

He continued, “But additionally, he acquires works because each individual object captures his imagination. It sounds so simple, but if you can really adhere to that as a collector, you’re going to have an outstanding collection, and it’s not going to follow the guidelines collectors often follow. Another thing that doesn’t get talked about enough is that once he’s identified an idea or a density of information, he collects that in depth.”

#598. Raw milk dangers go far beyond bird flu, experts warn

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pediatricians warn that raw milk carries serious health risks, including dangerous bacterial infections and new concerns about bird flu.

Parents who buy raw milk have usually heard that it is healthier or more natural than the pasteurized kind.

The pediatricians who wrote the newest guidance looked for the evidence behind that claim and couldn’t find any.

What they did find is a new entry on the list of things that can be living in that milk: the bird flu virus.

The pediatricians’ advice is the same as before, only firmer. No raw milk or raw-milk cheese for children or for anyone who is pregnant or breastfeeding, and no legal sale of it anywhere in the United States.

The guidance is a policy statement from the American Academy of Pediatrics, written by its infectious diseases and nutrition committees.

Adam J. Ratner of NYU Grossman School of Medicine led the writing with Anne Sardi, also at NYU, and Mark R. Corkins of the University of Tennessee Health Science Center.

The statement updates the academy’s 2014 guidance, and it is a review of published evidence rather than a new experiment.

“Parents may choose raw milk because they have heard it is healthier or more natural, but the scientific evidence tells a different story,” Corkins said.

“Pasteurized dairy products provide the same essential nutrients without exposing children and families to preventable infections from dangerous bacteria and viruses.”

The new risk is the strain of bird flu, H5N1, that wild birds passed to dairy cows and that cows now pass to each other.

In an infected cow, the virus concentrates in the udder, so it ends up in the milk at high levels.

In refrigerated raw milk, the virus stays alive for up to five weeks.

Among the states hit by the 2024 outbreak in dairy cattle, infectious virus turned up in nearly one-quarter of raw milk samples.

Pasteurization kills it, so properly pasteurized milk does not pose the same known H5N1 risk.

No human H5N1 infections have been confirmed from drinking raw milk.

Dairy workers have, though, from handling infected cows, and they came down with pink eye, fever, muscle aches and breathing symptoms.

Animals that drank the milk developed breathing problems and weak limbs, and some died.

The committees’ advice to doctors is practical. A person who drank raw milk and then developed flu symptoms, especially pink eye, should be tested for H5N1 and treated with antivirals if positive.

Bird flu is the newcomer. The bacteria that have contaminated milk for a century are still doing most of the damage.

From 2013 to 2018, raw milk caused 75 outbreaks in the United States, with 675 illnesses, 98 hospitalizations and two deaths.

Campylobacter was behind 49% of the outbreaks, followed by Salmonella at 17%, toxin-producing E. coli at 15% and the parasite Cryptosporidium at 11%.

Every person who caught Listeria in those outbreaks ended up in the hospital.

Nearly half of the people who got sick, 48%, were under 20, and 14% were under five.

Ten patients developed hemolytic uremic syndrome, a kidney failure that can follow an E. coli infection in young children. Two developed Guillain-Barré syndrome, a nerve disorder that can cause paralysis.

Of the outbreaks confined to one state, 78% happened in states that permit retail sales of raw milk.

The guidance emphasizes the serious risks of raw milk during pregnancy.

Pregnant people are 12 to 14 times as likely as the general population to develop listeriosis, and the infection reaches the fetus.

In pregnancies with listeriosis, 20% end in miscarriage or stillbirth.

Of the babies born alive to infected mothers, 68% are infected themselves, with pneumonia, sepsis or meningitis, and 24.5% of those infected newborns die.

The parasite Toxoplasma raises similar risks. Most Americans aged 15 to 44 have never been exposed to it, so they have no immunity during pregnancy.

Drinking unpasteurized goat milk raises the chance of catching it in pregnancy sevenfold.

Aged raw cheese can legally cross state lines if it was aged at least 60 days and labeled, and the authors wrote that the exemption still carries risk.

E. coli can survive that long, because aging a cheese is not the same as pasteurizing it.

A multistate outbreak traced to aged raw Gouda sickened 38 people and put 15 in the hospital. A Salmonella outbreak from raw milk and aged cheese reached 171 cases, 70% of them children.

Raw milk’s supporters say heating destroys fats, proteins, vitamins and enzymes. The committees reviewed the evidence and found little support for these claims.

Pasteurization doesn’t change the fats in milk, including its triglycerides and free fatty acids, and it doesn’t change the lactose. Calcium, magnesium and zinc come out the same.

A 2011 review of vitamins found that, among the vitamins milk is an important source of, pasteurization lowered only vitamin B2.

Heating does unfold milk’s proteins, but stomach acid does the same thing during digestion. Some active cow proteins, such as lactoferrin, are lost.

The two main milk allergens, whey and casein, are present in raw and pasteurized milk alike, so raw milk is no safer for a child with a milk allergy.

Ratner put the trade-off in one sentence.

“Pasteurization – a safe, effective process of heating and cooling milk before consumption that has been used for more than a century – dramatically reduces the risks without taking away the nutritional value families rely on,” Ratner said.

Raw milk is a small market. In surveys in 2016 and 2019, 4.4% of American adults said they had drunk it in the past year.

Young adults and rural residents were the likeliest to say so.

How often the less common organisms in raw milk, from Brucella to the rabies virus, actually make someone sick is largely unknown, the authors wrote. Whether H5N1 can pass through human breast milk is also unknown.

What the academy wants next is a ban on the sale and distribution of raw milk and raw-milk cheese nationwide.

It asks pediatricians to lobby their state legislators, to oppose bills that would legalize raw milk where it is banned, and to push for warning labels where it isn’t.

Until the laws change, the counsel to families is to buy only dairy labeled pasteurized.

The full study was published in the journal Pediatrics.

#4402. Halloween Candy Prices Take a Scary Bite Out of Shoppers’ Budgets

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Americans could spend over $4 billion on Halloween candy this year.

If you can’t afford a trip to a haunted house this spooky season, just swing by the supermarket candy aisle for a nasty scare. The price of sweets jumped over the last few years, soaring even faster than the average inflation rate, according to a recent report from the US Bureau of Labor Statistics, with higher ingredient costs as the primary villain.

October is important for candy sales, as shoppers stock up for parties, trick-or-treaters, or — let’s be honest — personal consumption.

“The biggest purchasing days are still ahead, with about 35% of Halloween seasonal sales expected this week,” a Hershey Company spokesperson told CNET.

Chocolate treats have become especially expensive, as a recent global cocoa shortage squeezed the market while candy companies were preparing shipments for Halloween.

But despite chilling prices, chocolate is still making its way into Halloween bags and buckets.

“Trick-or-treat participation intent is in line with last year,” the Hershey spokesperson said. “Confection remains a resilient category.”

Americans will likely spend over $13 billion on Halloween this year, the National Retail Federation reports. That’s almost twice the $6.9 billion people spent on the holiday 11 years ago, in 2015.

“Consumers continue to make Halloween a spending priority, but they are also approaching the season with affordability in mind,” Allison Zeller, a National Retail Federation vice president, said in a statement.

Candy remains the favorite purchase, with 95% of Halloween shoppers planning to snag some this year, followed by decorations, costumes and greeting cards, according to the National Retail Federation. In 2026, shoppers are expected to spend over $4 billion on Halloween candy alone.

An Oct. 20 report from research group Groundwork Collaborative broke down which candy prices have risen the most this year. The Mondelez Sour Gummy Candy variety pack, which jumped 39% in price, and Hershey seasonal Peanut Butter Cups, which saw a 23% increase, are this year’s most expensive Halloween candy compared with last season.

The squeeze doesn’t stop in the candy aisle — utility bills and other essentials are climbing, too. A Consumer Reports survey from earlier this month found that one in five American families has cut back on groceries due to rising energy costs.

“If you’ve been waiting until now to get your Halloween goodies, you’re right to do so. Halloween sales hit the high street and online two or three days before Halloween as stores try to clear stock,” says James Bricknell, CNET commerce managing editor.

“If you’re all set for this year, do what I did last year and grab a bunch of costumes and decorations the day after Halloween,” he adds. “Then, you can store them ready to go for Halloween 2027.”

#1030. Matching Outfits, Matching Smiles: Kate Middleton and Prince William Share a New Family Photo

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When Kate Middleton completed the National Three Peaks Challenge in June, becoming the first royal to do so, it turns out that she also conquered another challenge: the art of the family photo.

On October 25, she shared a new photo of herself, Prince William, and their three children—13-year-old Prince George, 11-year-old Princess Charlotte, and 8-year-old Prince Louis—along with others from her hike, in honor of Mountain Rescue Awareness Day.

“We send our heartfelt thanks to all those who volunteer their time and expertise to keep people safe in some of our most beautiful but challenging landscapes,” the Prince and Princess of Wales wrote in their Instagram caption.

“Through our connection with this remarkable organization, we have seen first-hand the dedication, professionalism and compassion that define their work. Meeting local mountain rescue teams during the Three Peaks Challenge this summer was an opportunity to say thank you in person for the vital role they play in keeping walkers and climbers safe on the mountains all year round,” they wrote, adding: “We are incredibly proud to support the extraordinary volunteers who respond in all conditions, often at a moment’s notice, to help others when they need it most. To everyone across the mountain rescue community, thank you for your unwavering commitment, courage and service.”

There was also some subtle outfit coordination in the photo, with the Prince and Princess wearing warm brown tops, and George and Charlotte wearing matching navy blue tops (a sweater for Charlotte; a polo shirt for George) and jeans.

#1150. Raw Denim vs. Regular Denim: Which Is Actually Worth Buying?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Here’s what to know about both kinds of jeans.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I COME FROM the denim era where streetwear juggernauts like FUBU and Rocawear reigned supreme, along with mall favorites like Abercrombie & Fitch and Hollister. I was in my preteens rocking all these clothing brands and you couldn’t tell me their baggy-stacking-at-the-shoe jeans weren’t the flyest on the market. Since I began working as a fashion editor, I’ve practically worn every pair of jeans in the denim universe. Slim, relaxed, stretch, washed, distressed—you name it, I’ve probably tried it. I’ve even tried my hand at denim-making in Japan, the mecca of old-school, high-quality denim, where I produced my own pair of selvedge jeans. Denim is one of those rare items in a guy’s wardrobe that never goes out of style.

And after years of being in the menswear space, I’ve gotten familiar with the nuances that distinguish one kind of denim from the next. Two worth knowing are regular denim and raw denim. Raw denim usually looks darker, stiffer, and more uniform in color, while regular denim has a softer feel and a more broken-in appearance with visible fading or whiskering from factory washing and finishing. Those differences also affect how each pair feels when you first put it on and how it changes over the years. Here’s what to know about both and which one deserves a spot in your rotation.

Regular jeans are usually made by weaving cotton yarn into denim, then cutting fabric into patterns and sewing them together to create the finished garment. These jeans have already gone through washing or finishing treatments to soften the fabric and give them that broken-in look, so you don’t have to spend weeks waiting for them to feel comfortable. If I want to throw on a pair of jeans and get on with my day, this is the kind I’m reaching for. I also like that you can find them in practically every fit and wash, whether you’re after a clean, dark indigo pair or something faded and lived-in. Most everyday options from brands like Levi’s and Wrangler run roughly $40 to $100, while designer and premium styles can cost considerably more. For me, prewashed denim makes the most sense when you want that comfortable feel from the jump. There’s nothing wrong with skipping the break-in period and getting straight to the good part.

I like raw denim because you get to see how jeans change the more you wear them. Unlike prewashed jeans, raw denim typically hasn’t been washed after the dyeing and manufacturing process, so it typically arrives stiff, dark, and pretty rigid. One thing to watch out for, though, is dye transfer. Indigo can rub off onto lighter-colored clothes, shoes, and other materials, including suede. I’ve had this happen before, so I'd be mindful of what you wear with a fresh pair. But that’s part of the appeal for denim enthusiasts. As you wear it, the fabric softens and develops fades and creases based on how you move, sit, and live in your jeans. You’re essentially breaking them in yourself, which makes the finished look feel uniquely yours. Prices can start around $60 to $100 for entry-level options and climb to $150 to $300 or more for premium denim, particularly Japanese-made styles. I’ll admit, the break-in period takes some patience, but if you enjoy watching your jeans evolve over time, raw denim is worth considering.

Raw denim and selvedge denim get lumped together all the time, but they actually refer to different things. Raw denim describes the fabric’s finishing state: It’s typically sold without a post-production wash that softens the fabric or creates a faded look. Selvedge, on the other hand, refers to how the denim is woven, creating a clean, self-finished edge you can often spot by rolling up the cuff. You can have raw selvedge denim, washed selvedge denim, or raw denim that isn’t selvedge at all. Having made my own pair of selvedge jeans in Japan, I’ve seen firsthand how the construction differs. Once you know what to look for, the distinction is pretty easy to spot.

At the end of the day, I don’t think you need to drop $300 on raw denim to dress well. After years of working in menswear, I’ve learned that a higher price tag doesn’t automatically make something the right choice for your wardrobe. I’ve seen guys look just as good in an affordable pair of Levi’s as they do in premium Japanese denim. What matters more is finding comfortable jeans that fit your body and work with the clothes you already own. I’m always going to appreciate the quality craftsmanship behind a great pair of raw denim, but there’s no single right way to wear jeans. I think every guy should have one dependable pair, whether that’s raw or prewashed. But ultimately, like most things in style, it comes down to personal preference—and knowing what works for you.
