#4224. A fast and secure tar parser for the Rust ecosystem

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Starting with 0.131, uv uses tar-codec to parse source distributions.

tar-codec is roughly twice as fast as our previous tar implementation (astral-tokio-tar) while also reducing uv's exposure to parser differentials and other security risks.

This blog post is about tar-codec itself: what it is, and what compelled us to build and optimize it. Read on if you, too, would like to know too much about tar archives.

What follows is an extended digression on the tar format. If all you care about is tar-codec, skip to it.

As a Python packaging tool, uv is in the business of resolving and installing Python packages. uv requests installable distributions from various sources (principally package indices like PyPI) and installs them on the user's behalf. This is how you go from uv add urllib3 to something you can actually import and use in your own Python codebase.

Installable distributions come in two primary forms:

"Built"2 distributions, also known as bdists or (most commonly) wheels, are essentially ZIP archives containing structured metadata and a fixed layout. Installers handle these by (more or less) plopping their contents into the right destination directory.

Many people think that bdist is short for "binary distribution," since wheels often contain compiled native code. This one common way to use wheels, but wheels can also contain pure Python. They're just another (and frequently better) way to package and install a distribution.

"Source" distributions, also known as sdists, are essentially tar archives. Unlike built distributions, a source distribution is essentially3 just a rollup of a source tree's state at release time.

By definition, source distributions execute arbitrary code at installation time. This makes them very powerful, but brittle (missing build tools or dependencies) and also a security risk (an attacker who controls the sdist can run code without the user needing to import anything).

Wheels are generally produced by running a build backend on a source distribution. Examples of that include uv's own uv_build, maturin, and flit.

Consequently, at a bare minimum, a Python package installer needs to understand at least two different archive formats: ZIP for wheels, and tar for sdists.

We've already talked a bit before about ZIPs on the Astral blog, and we'll certainly be talking about them again soon enough. But today we'll be focusing on tar.

It's fair to ask: why does uv want a new tar parser?

In short: security and performance, but mostly security.

Up until 0.13, uv used astral-tokio-tar for its tar operations, including both parsing and creation (in uv build). astral-tokio-tar is itself a fork of edera-dev/tokio-tar, which is a fork of vorot93/tokio-tar, which is a fork of dignifiedquire/async-tar, which is an async adaptation of composefs/tar-rs.

Around a year ago, we began to receive a number of security reports in astral-tokio-tar. This spurred us into looking more into our compression and archive handling stack, where we found additional problems.

Many of these boiled down to differentials that could cause a full parser desynchronization4: given an identical tarball, uv, pip, and other package tooling could be made to see different contents for the same package distribution. We found this particularly concerning in the context of archives that would appear "safe" to an index (like PyPI) but could extract dangerously when parsed by an installer like uv or pip.

This could the take the form of an "append" differential (where the attacker can append new files or new contents to existing files for a specific victim parser), or even a "full" differential (where the attacker can fully separate the extraction contents for each parser):

In the context of uv, the attacker model for these kinds of vulnerabilities is somewhat subtle: tar is only used for source distributions which, per above and unlike wheels, are assumed to execute arbitrary code at build time. Consequently, Python packaging mostly considers tar differentials to be a minor concern, since any attacker who would use them has a much more direct and official route towards writing arbitrary contents to disk (or executing arbitrary code).

Still, these findings unnerved us, and we embarked on a project to eliminate every differential we could find in astral-tokio-tar.

tar-codec grew directly out of that effort: we ultimately came to the conclusion that the tokio-tar family was too lenient by design, and that retrofitting constraints to it would be more troublesome and error-prone (including for existing non-Astral users of its APIs) than developing our own, ground-up implementation of tar. This would also give us the opportunity to systematically understand the tar format, and ensure that our assumptions around which aspects are (or aren't) differential risks hold true.

Similarly, we determined that our performance goals around tar parsing would be impossible to meet in the existing code base, at least not without refactors that would be tantamount to a rewrite.

It's important to understand that one does not simply build a tar parser from scratch. One must first understand how the tar format evolved into its current state5.

tar is an interesting format. Or more precisely, it's an interesting family of formats.

To summarize, there are three (and a half) variants that matter in practice:

There's UNIX V7 tar, which is the "original" tar. Very few tar implementations write this format by default anymore, but virtually all still read it. For example, Python's tarfile module will happily parse V7 tar files.

The UNIX V7 format is not widely used anymore because it comes with significant limitations on per-member file sizes (approximately 8 GiB) and path lengths (100 bytes).

Then there's the UStar/pax tar family6. This family is widely supported by tar implementations for both reading and writing. As far as tar goes, UStar and pax are what passes for a "gold standard" in terms of clarity of specification and documentation.

At this juncture, the main thing to know about the UStar/pax family is that it's derived from the UNIX V7 format, but with certain restrictions lifted:

GNU tar is documented in the GNU tar manual, but is not standardized per se. The source code of GNU tar (the CLI) is the de facto standard for GNU tar.

Like UStar/pax, GNU tar is derived from UNIX V7 tar. It's also derived from an early draft of UStar.

Also like pax, GNU tar makes file sizes and path/link lengths (effectively) unlimited. It does so in a completely different and much funnier way than pax does.

If you've ever written a parser for a binary format before, then you probably have a preconception for what a binary format should look like. To first, second, and third approximations, binary formats tend to look something like this:

Within this, too, certain idioms appear: binary formats typically use some form of TLV encoding, use different magics to signal different endiannesses, &c.

The tar format does not look like this, regardless of the variant. tar is fundamentally a block format, owing to its origins as a tape archival format8.

As a block format, a tar archive is comprised of one or more fixed-length blocks. There is no file-level header; instead, many of its blocks are headers, while other blocks contain actual member9 data:

In all flavors of tar, all blocks (including the header blocks) are always 512 bytes. Each header block declares metadata for the member that follows it. This includes important things like the size of the member and its filepath.

Member contents don't always fit cleanly into multiples of 512 bytes, so a tar that wants to encode a file member of N bytes will allocate ceil(N / 512) blocks and pad out the remainder of the last block. This doesn't affect extraction, since the reader knows (via the header block) exactly how many bytes out of the allocated space are actual file content.

Conventionally, the padding or "holes" in a tar archive are filled with NUL bytes. However, nothing stops a tar producer from filling them with uninitialized memory, or lines from the Bee Movie, or anything else. A recurring theme with tar (of all flavors) is that it has no opinions about what goes in the holes of the format. This malleability makes it very prone to parser differentials, since it gives the attacker ample space to place surprising data.

tar's block design also makes it relatively space-inefficient10 by modern standards. For example, a 513-byte file occupies an absolute minimum of 1,536 bytes when encoded as a tar member: one 512-byte block for the header, and two blocks for the content (one of which has only 1 "real" byte, followed by 511 bytes of padding). Similarly, a tar archive containing 1000 one-byte files requires a minimum of 1025 KiB of encoding (1 MiB for the headers and contents, and then 1 KiB for the trailing zero blocks).

So far, this all sounds pretty sane. It's not a "normal" file format by modern standards, but there's a simple state machine one could write to formalize a parse for the tar example above:

Unfortunately, it's not that simple. The state machine above is accurate for a V7 tar, but not for anything else in the tar format family.

To understand why, we have to remember V7 tar's limitations. All of those limitations come down to the C structure that represents a V7 member header, which we've annotated here:

Three big limitations stand out, and each member of the tar family tries to solve them in various different ways:

Let's see how each family works around these.

UStar's half-solution is to preserve the existing physical header block, while impressing some of its fields and padding into new roles.

This partially lifts the member name restriction (by raising it to 255 bytes), but retains the file size and linkname limitations.

pax fixes these remaining limitations by doing away with the single-block metadata design. Instead, pax has extensions that rely on two new UStar typeflags:

In both cases, an extended header block indicates its member's "size." But that size isn't for a file: instead, it's for the extension data itself.

So, what's in the extension data? pax calls it an extended header, and it's an entirely different encoding format. An extended header contains one or more pax "records," and each record is a string that looks like this:

This can be hard to visualize, so here's a concrete example:

Despite being a new encoding, the way that pax records work is relatively simple: if there is an effective pax record that corresponds to a tar member header's state, the pax record's value takes precedence. In the example above, this means that the subsequent member's size is 8000 bytes, even if the member's own header records a different size.

This is where the difference between global and local extensions comes in as well: global extended headers take precedence over all subsequent members in the archive, while local extended headers only affect the next file in the archive. Local extended headers also take precedence over any global headers, so we have the following precedence ordering:

(This, too, is simplified. More on that later.)

For this example, a pax-conforming tar implementation should extract two members:

hasdrubal.txt, with a payload of 10737418240 bytes and an mtime of 100.

Observe that this is both greater than the UStar size limit of 8 GiB and does not use the UStar header's own name (of hannibal.txt) or size (of 0), since the member's header is preceded by an x extended header that overrides those values13.

Moreover, the archive also contains a g extended header that overrides mtime for all subsequent members and x does not contain its own mtime, so the global extended header's mtime=100 also takes effect.

hamilcar.txt, with a payload of 1234 bytes and an mtime of 100.

Observe that, unlike hasdrubal.txt, there is no x extended header for this member. As a result, the UStar member states are used, with the exception of those overridden by the effective g extended header. Thus hamilcar.txt ends up with mtime=100, even if its UStar header sets a different value.

GNU tar takes a very different approach. First, here's the GNU tar header:

Observe that, unlike the UStar format, there is no char prefix[155] field. Furthermore, instead of pax extended headers with a fully general keyword/value format, GNU tar does two things:

It goes way harder than UStar on trying to re-use the existing 512-byte V7 member header. In particular, GNU tar adds its own base-256 encoding: if the first byte in a numeric field has its high bit set, then the remaining octets in that field are binary, rather than tar's normal octal form.

This effectively expands the per-byte entropy in numeric fields from 3 bits to 8 bits. For a char size[12], that makes the effective size limit 2**8014 or one yobibyte.

Instead of general x and g extended headers, GNU tar introduces a bunch of specialized typeflags for its own extensions.

Many of these are not relevant to the post, or even to modern tar archives at all. But two are:

These two typeflags effectively fill the role of the pax path and linkpath records, respectively.

For this example, a GNU tar implementation should extract two members:

hasdrubal.txt with a payload of 10737418240 bytes.

Observe that this size, which exceeds 8 GiB, was encoded directly in the header. However, the effective filename of hasdrubal.txt comes from overriding the header's original filename of hannibal.txt via the preceding GNU L extension.

publius.txt, which links to another file named africanus.txt.

Observe that publius.txt originally declared publius.txt -> scipio.txt as its link, but this gets overridden to africanus.txt by the preceding GNU K extension.

Finally, while GNU tar does not have pax's notion of global vs. local precedence, GNU tar extensions can stack. For example, to override a member's name and linkname at the same time, you'd have a tar header sequence like this15:

Now that we understand very roughly how the various tar family formats look and work, let's see what tar-codec has to do to parse them safely and efficiently.

First we'll revisit the state machine. Our nice and simple V7 state machine now understands the different pax and GNU states, and has become this monstrosity:

(Note that the error transitions in this state machine are dramatically simplified: in practice, any I/O or validation failure at any point also results in a Failed state.)

What in the world happened? A few different things:

Our state machine begins with a "classification" stage, which effectively locks the remainder of the parse to a single family (either UStar/pax or GNU16) based on the identity (magic and version) of the first block.

This is done for differential reduction reasons: while researching tar-codec's design, we realized that many other widely used tar parsers attempt to merge states across various tar families, with inconsistent results.

For example, with some tar parsers, it's possible to stack headers like so:

This has unclear semantics: GNU tar expects an L or K to apply to the next "file" member, but the next physical member in the stream is a pax local extension that also wants to modify the next "file" member. In practice we've seen other parsers do all kinds of things in this state, including attempting to merge the extensions, honoring one over the other(s), attempting to apply the L state to x instead of the file member, and so forth. Each of these is a potentially exploitable differential!

Instead, tar-codec will reject a tar archive if its individual headers don't agree about what family of tar it is. Real-world tar encoders don't mix tar families (there's no good reason to), so this doesn't pose a significant breakage risk.

The GNU part of the state machine is slightly more strict than the GNU documentation implies: the GNU documentation allows stacking of L/K extensions, including multiple of the same kind, in any order.

tar-codec tightens that to allowing L and K in any order, but only one of each per underlying member.

The UStar/pax part of the state machine models local and global extended headers rigidly.

There's a significant amount of complexity beneath this: pax allows x and g anywhere in the tar stream. For example, this is a perfectly valid extension sequence in pax:

This brings us to one of the most painful parts of pax that we glossed over before: extended headers don't shadow each other fully, they compose depending on what state the "stack" of effective pax records is in. The state of any given pax record can be:

Undeclared: no effective pax record exists anywhere in the current stack of local and global extensions, which means that the member's own UStar header's state takes precedence.

Active: there's an effective pax record in the stack, and it has a value.

Tombstoned: there's an effective pax record in the stack, but it has no value (for example, 8 path=\n instead of 12 path=abc\n).

You would think that this would be the same as the Undeclared state, but it's not! pax considers this record explicitly deleted, and prescribes:

If the field is zero length, it shall delete any header block field, previously entered extended header value, or global extended header value of the same name.

This has fascinating consequences for the state machine, because it means that an effective-but-deleted path record means...a file with no path? How are you supposed to extract that17?

Needless to say, tar-codec tries to follow this faithfully. In particular, it attempts to be diligent about rejecting logically-consistent-but-nonsense states that fall out of pax's strange record state machine, including empty paths and global extended headers that try to declare size and other key states for all members.

One of the early design choices we made with tar-codec was to isolate the implementation into a few layers. From lowest to highest:

The tar-framing crate is responsible for taking a stream of blocks and turning it into a stream of frames. This happens in two refinement stages:

The stream or "physical" API honors the big state machine above and yields each block in a given state, one block at a time.

The "logical" API consumes the blocks produced by the "physical" API, and yields them as a stream of assembled members. Each assembled member is essentially its "header" (which is the physical header, merged with any effective extension state that affected the physical framing), a separate extension state for non-framing extensions (such as GNU L and K), and a readable payload for the member's actual contents.

The tar-codec crate takes the "logical" API from tar-framing and turns it into something that can be extracted in memory or onto disk.

Extracting an archive (of any format) onto disk is its own can of worms, with its own differential risks18. Another one of our early observations is that every archive implementation tends to have its own extract-to-disk implementation, with its own edge cases.

We built archive-trait to address that. archive-trait exposes Archive and ArchiveBuilder traits (along with associated types and machinery) that allow any format to expose uniform APIs for reading and creating archives. We then built archive-trait's own filesystem extraction on top of cap-std, giving us the ability to "anchor" extraction into a single directory and avoid a large class of link and path traversal footguns.

Writing a differential-resistant tar parser is surprisingly hard, given how ubiquitous tar files of all flavors are, and how simple the original (V7) format is.

This post has only scratched the surface in terms of complexity. To recap:

Each tar family has its own state machine, which is not always clearly laid out in that family's documentation or reference implementation. It is imperative that these state machines do not mix, but real-world tar parsers regularly allow them to.

Each tar family has its own, non-trivial rules for determining the precedence of state (pathname, linkname, and size) attached to archive members.

Some of this state affects the framing of the parser itself, meaning that faithfully handling e.g. pax archives means handling size and similar records up-front, rather than trying to abstract the parse across individual 512-byte header blocks.

Separately, there were numerous places where we had to compromise on strict pax conformance because of observed behavior from other implementations:

The pax spec is explicit that pax record values must be UTF-8. However, both GNU tar and star historically emitted some vendor-specific records19 as raw binary. See this bug-tar thread for more context.

UStar and pax are explicit that uname and gname should be NUL-terminated in the UStar header, meaning that each can be at most 31 bytes long. However, CPython's tarfile (and probably others) will happily fill either to 32 bytes, only overflowing to the pax record at 33 bytes or more. See CPython#159322 for more context.

More generally, tar implementations will frequently leave mode, uid, gid, and mtime as all NUL, rather than zeroed or similar. This doesn't pose a framing/parser synchronization risk, so tar-codec tracks them as "missing" rather than assigning an arbitrary default value.

Finally, in the process of designing and implementing tar-codec, we found many parser differential primitives that cause various tar implementations to behave very differently on identical inputs. Some of these are currently being disclosed as security vulnerabilities. However, some of the general classes we saw:

pax/GNU state machine confusion, as mentioned above. In particular, some tar implementations can be fooled into applying a pax extension's states to a GNU extension (or vice versa) instead of to the subsequent member, often resulting in full desynchronization and different extraction results.

Mis-handling of pax extension precedence, in particular both global (g) extended headers and record "tombstoning." For example, some implementations will accept a g header with size=1234 but ignore it, despite pax saying clearly that such a size record overrides any UStar size fields. This also typically results in a full desynchronization primitive.

Mis-handling of pax record parsing. For example, some implementations will ignore the prefix and instead assume that pax records can be safely split on \n. This is not true, since the of a pax record can contain a verbatim \n. As you may have guessed, this also typically results in a full desynchronization primitive.

More generally, we found a great deal of variety in how lax various tar implementations are when it comes to parsing integer fields: many use their language's native "integer from string" API, which can behave surprisingly in the presence of different (non-Western Arabic) numeral forms, leading and trailing whitespace, and so forth. These small variations can percolate into two different implementations parsing the same size field as different integers, once again resulting in desynchronization.

Decode and encode performance was not the hard part: we found a lot of low-hanging fruit while optimizing both, including optimizing tar's checksum generation and calculating pax record length prefixes.

We also picked up significant performance gains by ensuring that tar-codec reuses allocations and batches/buffers its work wherever possible (and where doing so does not weaken the API's internal consistency or cancellation correctness in an async setting).

Conversely, performant secure extraction is somewhat hard: directory capability tracking (of the kind that cap-std does) adds measurable overhead to many of our filesystem operations, particularly when extracting on hosts that lack openat2(2) or an equivalent system call20. This negatively impacts extraction on macOS in particular.

All in all, tar-codec's design rests on well-worn principles for secure parser design: we are conservative rather than liberal in what we accept, and we enforce correctness by construction through increasingly constrictive layers (from member framing to streaming to extraction). These design choices have made us confident that tar-codec is more resistant to differentials (by correctly rejecting them) than other tar implementations.

The fractured and under-specified nature of tar makes it impossible for us to say that we are entirely devoid of differentials, or that new differentials won't be discovered in the future. However, we believe that tar-codec's primitives are the correct ones if and when those contingencies occur.

This is not the end of our work, on several levels:

While tar-codec is essentially feature-complete for our own purposes (within uv), we're interested in making sure that it also solves problems that the larger Open Source community has around secure and performant tar parsing.

To that end, we'll be looking at supporting other, more niche aspects of the various tar families (like sparse files, and more control over vendor-specific pax records) as the need arises.

Separately, we'll be working with others in the community on improving the correctness of other tar implementations. In particular, we see Harmen Stoppels's nascent tar-minefield project as a promising way to test and re-align other implementations with stricter, more differential-resistant interpretations of the tar family formats.

Separately, as you may have guessed from our decision to build a generic archive-trait library: tar is not the only format we're thinking about here!

We're also actively looking into replacing uv's ZIP parsing, for similar reasons. We hope to share our lessons from that work soon, along with material benefits to uv itself.

tar-codec has been available since 0.12, but was disabled by default behind the --preview-features tar-codec flag. As of 0.13, it is the default and can be disabled by setting UV_LEGACY_TAR_BACKEND=1. ↩

The term "built" is the source of a great deal of confusion for newcomers when they first encounter Python packaging. In the context of Python packaging, "built" refers to the fact that the distribution is produced by a "build backend", i.e. contains structured metadata that a source distribution is not guaranteed to have.

It does not imply that the wheel contains shared libraries or other native code, although it can. Pure Python packages are also regularly distributed as built distributions, because installing them is faster and more reliable. ↩

Historically, a source distribution was literally just a source archive, with the convention of having a pyproject.toml or setup.py at the archive's root. More recent Python packaging standards state that source distributions must contain a PKG-INFO file containing standard metadata. See PEP 643 for more on that.

As a result, many source distributions contain no metadata other than the build system itself, which must be executed to access the distribution's metadata. ↩

We recommend Edera's "TARmageddon" blog post for a detailed treatment of one such differential. ↩

Martin Dørum has a great blog post on the original tar format and how it evolved over time into the constellation of variants that currently exist. We highly recommend reading his post. ↩

Whether or not UStar and pax should be clumped together in the same family is doubtless extremely controversial to the dozen-or-so other people on Earth who have thought about the tar format this much. I consider them to be in the same family, since pax is effectively a strict superset of UStar and comes from the same standards body (POSIX) that produced UStar. ↩

There are actually two different GNU tar formats: there's oldgnu i.e. "old GNU" and gnu i.e. "new GNU". The difference between these two is minor (relative to everything else) and GNU tar itself emits "new GNU" as of tar 1.12, which was released in 1997. So that's enough about "old GNU." ↩

"tar" of course being short for "Tape ARchive." Magnetic tapes (like all mass storage) store their contents in sequences of fixed-sized blocks, and tar seals 1979's glorious 512-byte blocks in amber.

tar is also far from the only block-style file format. cpio is another well-known one. ↩

Archive members are things like files and directories, but also things like sockets and device files. I'm not really clear on what it means to archive a socket, but both UStar/pax and GNU tar will happily let you express that you've done so! ↩

Before compression, of course. tar knows nothing about compression. It's just the format that encodes one or more files into a single file. ↩

V7 tar handles directories in two ways: it creates directories that are implied to exist (for example, a member whose name is foo/makeme/bar will create foo and foo/makeme if needed), and it will explicitly create a directory if a regular file member ends with a slash, e.g. foo/makeme/. ↩

In other words, it's a self-describing length. This is somewhat unusual when compared to a "normal" TLV/LTV encoding, where the length component is typically either a fixed length or something with only limited length variation, like LEB128. ↩

You might be thinking: why bother putting states in the member's UStar header at all, if they're just going to be overridden by the pax extended header? Why not just fill those parts of the block with NULs?

The answer has to do with backwards compatibility: both GNU and pax do a disgusting hack interesting trick where the extension/extended header's own name field is also a valid path, conventionally a specially formatted one like %d/PaxHeaders.%p/%f, where %d is the dirname of the pax path record, %p is the current PID, and %f is the basename of the same path record.

The theory behind this is (probably) that a subsequent tool can repair the extraction, by looking at the pax headers which have been extracted as a regular file instead. In practice I suspect this doesn't work well in the greater-than-8 GiB case, which is one of the cases where it matters the most. ↩

This is 2**80 instead of 2**96 because we lose size[0] to the base-256 sign bit and size[11] to the NUL byte. Implementations that don't require a NUL terminator for fields like size could in principle allow up to 2**88 instead. ↩

The order of GNU extensions before the member they affect does not appear to matter in any implementation. In other words, you can have L -> K -> 1 or K -> L -> 1. ↩

That's correct: we don't support parsing V7-style tar files. An early review revealed that they're nonexistent in the Python packaging ecosystem, probably because Python's own tarfile never supported creating them. The gap between V7 and UStar is small, however, so adding it would not be difficult if needed. ↩

Many tar implementations do random things here, like trying to extract to an empty path (which of course fails), or picking some previous filename or extended header state. ↩

Extraction differentials themselves are out of this post's scope, but to summarize: extraction depends heavily on the target filesystem and its state, including any pre-existing symbolic and hard links.

Nuances abound, including how to handle files that differ in casing inside the archive but not on the filesystem itself (macOS and Windows), how to handle symlinking on systems where symbolic links are privileged (Windows), and how to handle "funky but valid" filenames (non-UTF-8, UTF-8 with NULs, control characters, &c.). ↩

A pax "vendor record" is a normal pax record, but with a name like VENDOR.keyword instead of keyword. pax itself does not specify their semantics and various implementations do wildly different things with them, so tar-codec rejects them by default (with an option to skip/ignore them instead). SCHILY.xattr.* is one common example of a vendor record namespace. GNU also has their own vendor namespace, e.g. GNU.sparse.* for sparse members. ↩

Without openat2(2) or a similar source of RESOLVE_BENEATH semantics, cap-std needs to open each directory component in a path, which means that its system calls scale linearly with the depth of the paths it needs to track. This is on top of the system call overhead for the extraction I/O itself. ↩

#778. Check Your Fridge: This Plant-Based Butter Was Recalled Over E. Coli, Yeast &amp; Mold

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

More than 1,300 cases of butter were pulled from shelves.

Before you slather your morning toast in butter, double-check which brand you have in your fridge. A recently classified recall affects butter sold in 10 U.S. states.

Miyoko’s Creamery European Style Salted Plant Milk Butter has been voluntarily recalled from store shelves by distributor Prosperity Organic Foods because it may contain yeast, mold, and E. coli. The affected butter was sold in six-pack containers with eight-ounce packaging and has the UPC code 857554005773, lot number 07871, and a best-by date of November 5, 2026.

The affected product is perishable and stored in the refrigerated and frozen sections at stores. The recall includes 1,304 cases of the butter, packaged in wax paper wrapping in an SBS carton.

The affected cases of butter were distributed in 10 states, including Connecticut, Florida, Georgia, Iowa, Illinois, Indiana, Maryland, New Jersey, New Hampshire, and Wisconsin.

Initial reports of the recall date back to September, when prominent retailers including Sprouts and Wegmans began posting notices to consumers. Market Basket reported that testing found that the butter did not “meet their quality standards” and may contain E. coli. The official FDA recall notice also lists both yeast and mold contamination.

According to Miyoko’s Creamery, the company “voluntarily pulled a very small number of products off the shelves out of an abundance of caution. Any potentially affected products have fully been removed from shelves.”

While no illnesses associated with the recall have been reported, consuming the affected product could cause temporary or medically reversible adverse health effects.

Consumers who have the affected product in their possession should not consume it; instead, they should dispose of it or return it to the place of purchase. Select retailers are offering refunds.

#1058. An Exclusive Look Inside the 30th Anniversary Celebration of ‘Rent’

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Monday night, members of the original Broadway cast of Rent—the iconic rock opera that dropped the plot of La Bohème into Alphabet City during the HIV/AIDS crisis—united with a sprawling company of theater talent to celebrate the show’s 30th birthday before a sold-out crowd at New York’s Lunt-Fontanne Theatre.

Rent played its first preview off-Broadway, at the New York Theatre Workshop, on January 25, 1996—the same day that Jonathan Larson, its 35-year-old composer and librettist, died suddenly at his home in Manhattan. Three months later, on April 29, it opened at the Nederlander Theatre, going on to earn 10 Tony nominations (the most of any production that season) and four wins, including for best musical. After making stars of its young actors, including Anthony Rapp, Adam Pascal, Taye Diggs, and Idina Menzel, Rent’s subsequent iterations across the country, in regional and touring productions, also helped to launch the careers of performers like Aaron Tveit, Brandon Uranowitz, and Norbert Leo Butz. (Its Broadway run ended in 2008, after a dozen years and 5,123 regular performances.)

Celebrity guests at Rent during its first year on Broadway:

Barbara Walters and David Geffen on Rent’s opening night, 1996.

Shirley MacLaine at a performance of Rent in May 1996.

Paul Newman and Joanne Woodward at Rent in May 1996.

Nicole Kidman and Tom Cruise at a performance in May 1996.

Jim Carrey at a performance in July 1996.

Eddie Murphy at a performance of Rent in October 1996.

Al Hirschfeld’s caricature of the original Broadway cast of Rent.

To mark 30 years of Rent—and to raise money for Broadway Cares/Equity Fights AIDS—nearly 70 actors and singers whose lives have intersected with the show in some way, from Neil Patrick Harris (who starred in the 1997 national tour) to Rachel Zegler (who had to quit a high school production to make her screen test for West Side Story), took the stage of the Lunt-Fontanne for a rollicking and emotional one-night-only concert. (The full thing is available to stream until 11 p.m.)

On the scene to capture the staging’s stars was photographer Curtis Brown. See his portraits—and relive the concert’s joyous celebration of one of musical theater’s most groundbreaking and influential works—below.

According to The Sun’s research back in 2022, Dancing With the Stars make $250,000 per season. However, there are reports that their income can reach upwards of around $1.2 million per season (this was late judge Len Goodman’s reported salary).

There’s less info about Alfonso Ribeiro and Julianne Hough’s salaries, but their predecessor Tom Bergeron was reportedly earning $150,000 per episode (that’s five times Bruno’s alleged salary) according to Celebrity Net Worth. So clearly the job pays well!

#354. Bilt Will Stop Awarding You 4% Bilt Cash Earning At Card Renewal—Unless You Change This App Setting

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I receive compensation for content and many links on this blog. Be aware that websites may earn compensation when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Any opinions expressed in this post are my own, and have not been reviewed, approved, or endorsed by my advertising partners. Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

Bilt Rewards is changing how rewards are earned on their credit cards. Today, cardmembers have a choice:

Earn 4% back in Bilt Cash, in addition to points. That Bilt Cash can be redeemed for points-earning on housing payments, faster points-earning (accelerators) and redemptions on hotel stays or with partners like Blade, Lyft, Walgreens, etc.

Skip Bilt Cash and earn ‘up to 1.25x on housing payments’ based on your month’s spending. Bilt is moving everyone over to the second option, however existing cardmembers and those who apply, are approved and accept their card through December 31 can opt in to preserving #1. It’s possible to do better with earning up to 1.25x on housing payments, but for most people doing significant spend on the card (where they’re earning more Bilt Cash than their housing payment would consume) they’ll come out ahead earning Bilt Cash. And of course you can always choose to move over to the new model later if you prefer! You need to proactively choose continuing to earn Bilt Cash, to avoid moving over. Once you actually move over (starting at your card renewal date) it cannot be reversed. I’ve already protected my Bilt Palladium Card (See rates and fees) with the legacy earning structure. This is the card I use for most of my spending. Here’s where you go in the app to do it. Go to your wallet and select your Bilt card. Then either go to your current choice for Bonus Rewards (e.g. “Flexible Bilt Cash”) underneath Your card rewards or go to Manage card and select bonus rewards which is the last option at the bottom. Select ‘your rewards are updating’ which is the top option on the screen. The default, which is already selected, is the new rewards model. Swipe right… Select ‘flexible Bilt Cash’ and hit save. You have until your card renewal date to make this choice, but if you preserve the old earning model you can always make the (irreversible) switch later. I’ve already made my choice. #791. Congress Is Cracking Down On THC Drinks—And Consumers Have Until December To Speak Up

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A booming market is suddenly staring down a very different future.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Stock your doomsday shelves with as many cans of Cann and Squier's Elixir as you can—the hemp-derived THC industry may soon be on borrowed time. Despite the steady rise of THC-infused drinks and snacks, changes to federal hemp law set to take effect over the next several weeks could knock many hemp-derived products out of the legal hemp category, potentially decimating a now-booming industry. But Congress still has time to change course before the biggest provisions take effect.

Back in 2018, the Farm Bill legalized hemp production and removed qualifying hemp from the federal Controlled Substances Act, helping fuel a new market for hemp-derived CBD and THC products. Hemp and marijuana both come from the cannabis plant, but the law defined hemp as containing no more than 0.3% delta-9 THC by dry weight and included its derivatives, extracts, and cannabinoids.

That definition helped create the opening for today's hemp-derived THC market, although the Farm Bill didn’t automatically legalize every hemp-derived food or drink and the FDA retained authority over consumable products. The THC drink market alone is now projected to reach $5.6 billion by 2035.

The Farm Bill was intended in part to create new legal markets for hemp, including uses like textiles and fibers. But lawmakers have since pointed to the rise of intoxicating hemp-derived products as an unintended consequence of the way the law defined hemp.

Congress has already moved to close what lawmakers call a loophole in the 2018 law. Under legislation passed in 2025, the federal definition of hemp will become much narrower, excluding certain synthetic or manufactured cannabinoids as well as final hemp-derived cannabinoid products containing more than 0.4 mg of total THC and other cannabinoids with similar effects per container—a limit that would sideline most current THC drinks and edibles.

Supporters of the change have pointed to concerns about intoxicating hemp products being sold with inconsistent federal oversight, including products such as gummies, drinks, and candy.

Congress recently delayed most of those restrictions until December 11, which Joe Gerrity, CEO of Crescent Canna, believes buys time to replace prohibition with smart regulation. While restrictions involving certain synthetic cannabinoids are still scheduled to take effect November 12, Gerrity points to two proposed alternatives to the current federal restrictions.

“Lawmakers already have two bipartisan starting points,” Gerrity explains. “The Lawful Hemp Protection Act would set national standards for labeling, age verification, serving limits, and FDA oversight, [while] the Beverage Regulatory Parity Act focuses on THC drinks, with a 21+ requirement, TTB oversight, potency limits, testing, and taxation.”

While the two bills differ, both favor regulating hemp-derived THC products rather than relying on the current restrictions. Mike Viellion, founder of Sip Elixirs, believes the hemp industry should be regulated like any other adult product, with age restrictions, testing, labeling, responsible dosing, and enforcement.

“We think prohibition is the wrong answer,” he shares. “Consumers have clearly demonstrated that they want responsible alternatives to alcohol, and THC beverages are becoming part of that choice.”

Both Gerrity and Viellion encourage the general public to speak up now.

“Contact your representatives and ask them to cosponsor the Lawful Hemp Protection Act and support including its language in the spending bill,” suggests Gerrity. “A short, personal note about why these products matter to you carries real weight with lawmakers.”

Sites like the U.S. Hemp Roundtable Action Page even provide phone scripts to streamline the process and make it easier than ever to contact your local officials.

“People who enjoy these products have every right to participate in that conversation, and their voices are extremely important to the process,” Viellion adds.

“States like Minnesota, Louisiana, Tennessee and Georgia show a regulated market can work,” Gerrity says. Demand for these products isn’t going away, and Gerrity and Viellion argue that the new restrictions could push some consumers toward unregulated, black-market versions.

The industry could also lose significant jobs and businesses. According to Whitney Economics’ U.S. Hemp Cannabinoid Report for 2026, if the federal restrictions remain in place, roughly 225,000 jobs could be displaced, and state sales-tax potential could decline by $2.1 billion. The report estimates there are roughly 50,000 hemp operators nationwide and projects that a majority could be forced out of business.

As we learned back in the ‘20s, prohibition isn’t all it’s cracked up to be.

#3656. Sotheby’s Dinosaur ‘Jodi’ Sells for $47.3 M., More Than Five Times Its High Estimate

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The dinosaur market has produced another monster sale.

“Jodi,” a 67-million-year-old Nanotyrannus skeleton, sold Tuesday at Sotheby’s New York for $47.3 million, including fees, more than five times its $9 million high estimate. The skeleton hammered at $40.5 million, making it one of the most expensive dinosaur fossils ever sold at auction.

The result comes just three months after Sotheby’s sold “Gus,” a Tyrannosaurus rex skeleton, for a record $50.1 million. That July sale, which carried a $20 million–$30 million estimate, established a new high for prehistoric fossils, surpassing the $44.6 million paid for the Stegosaurus “Apex” in 2024. Jodi came remarkably close to that record, despite being considerably smaller and lacking the brand recognition of a T. rex. Related Articles Bill Koch to Sell More Than $40 M. in Art at Christie's, Led by Monet and Cezanne Paintings Sotheby's Deepens Abu Dhabi Relationship With Inaugural Fine Art Auction in December

The nearly 20-foot-long skeleton was discovered in Montana’s Hell Creek Formation in 2019 by rancher Jodi Pierson, for whom the fossil was named. According to Sotheby’s, it’s one of only three known Nanotyrannus skeletons and includes 191 fossil bone elements, among them a nearly complete skull.

Until recently, scientists weren’t even sure Nanotyrannus was a separate dinosaur. For decades, researchers debated whether the smaller predator was simply a juvenile T. rex. A study published in Nature last year helped settle the argument, identifying Nanotyrannus as a distinct genus that lived alongside its much larger cousin.

Jodi has already had something of a career in the art market. In 2023, when it was known as “Chomper” and still identified as a juvenile T. rex, London dealer David Aaron offered it for $20 million at Frieze Masters. (The work didn’t sell then as the current consignor acquired it in 2021, according to Sotheby’s provenance listing.) Three years later, with a new name and a new scientific identity, it has sold for more than twice that amount.

The sale is just more evidence that dinosaurs have become serious trophy objects for wealthy collectors, joining blue-chip paintings, rare watches, and sports memorabilia at the top end of the luxury market. In recent years, prices have climbed at a remarkable pace. Christie’s sold the T. rex “Stan” for $31.8 million in 2020, while a juvenile Ceratosaurus brought $30.5 million at Sotheby’s last year against an estimate of just $4 million–$6 million.

The boom has also raised concerns among paleontologists, who worry that scientifically important fossils are increasingly being priced beyond the reach of museums and disappearing into private collections. Auction houses counter that private investment helps fund the expensive work of excavating, preserving, and studying specimens.
